CFA Level I Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): He violated Standard II(A) by trading on material nonpublic information he obtained. Standard II(A) prohibits acting on material nonpublic information regardless of how it was obtained, including an overheard private conversation. Materiality is judged by whether a reasonable investor would consider the merger news relevant to a trading decision. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.
  2. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): She violated Standard III(D) by presenting hypothetical back-tested results as if they were part of her actual performance record. Standard III(D), Performance Presentation, requires clear disclosure whenever performance figures are simulated, hypothetical, or back-tested rather than actually achieved, and blending unlabeled hypothetical results into a real composite misleads a reasonable prospect into overestimating the manager's true track record. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.
  3. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): She violated Standard III(B) by failing to deal fairly with clients of similar objectives. Standard III(B) requires that clients with similar objectives receive fair, not necessarily identical, treatment when allocating scarce opportunities like an oversubscribed IPO. Systematically favoring the wealthiest accounts first breaches that duty. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.
  4. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The advisor violated Standard III(C) by recommending an investment unsuitable for the client's needs. Standard III(C) requires updating and following a client's investment profile before making recommendations, particularly for someone nearing retirement with low risk tolerance. A leveraged speculative product is plainly inconsistent with stated income and stability needs. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.
  5. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): She violated Standard IV(A) by misappropriating confidential employer records before departing. Standard IV(A) requires members to protect an employer's confidential business information, including client lists and account histories, and not use it to compete after leaving. Client information developed on the job belongs to the firm, not the departing employee. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.

Disclosure alone would not cure the underlying independence problem here. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.

Fabricating expenses is a clear act of dishonesty. Casual social conversation about specific account details is not a permitted exception. The other two options misapply a different Standard to facts that do not actually support it, since neither the conduct nor the duty they describe matches what actually occurred here.

The volume of trading must serve the client's goals, not the advisor's compensation.   51. The correct answer is B (Standard IV(B), Additional Compensation Arrangements): members must obtain written consent from their employer before accepting compensation or other benefits from a third party that could create a conflict with the employer's interest. Dana's paid board seat is exactly this kind of outside arrangement, and hiding it from her employer prevents the firm from assessing whether it competes with client interests. Option A concerns broader duties of loyalty and care in managing the portfolio itself, not outside compensation. Option C addresses disclosing conflicts to clients generally, but the more specific and directly applicable rule here governs undisclosed third-party compensation.
  52. The correct answer is A (Standard V(A), Diligence and Reasonable Basis): members must have a reasonable and adequate basis, supported by appropriate research, for any recommendation. Relying solely on a company's own press release, with no review of primary financial documents or independent analysis, falls well short of that standard. Option B concerns freedom from undue influence on judgment, not the thoroughness of the research itself. Option C addresses how conclusions are communicated to clients, which is a separate issue from whether the underlying analysis was adequate.
  53. The correct answer is C (Standard II(B), Market Manipulation): entering trades intended to artificially affect a closing price, with no genuine investment purpose, distorts the price-discovery process and misleads other market participants. Lucia's rapid, purposeless trading near the close is a textbook example of this prohibited practice. Option A is a catch-all for conduct involving dishonesty or fraud but is less precise than the manipulation-specific standard that directly addresses artificial price effects. Option B concerns equal treatment of clients, which is not implicated by manipulating a market price.
  54. The correct answer is B (Standard IV(C), Responsibilities of Supervisors): a supervisor must make reasonable efforts to detect and prevent violations by those under her authority, and an unverified assumption that disclosure occurred does not satisfy that duty. The compliance officer needed to actively confirm the arrangement was properly disclosed rather than take it on faith. Option A concerns understanding applicable laws and regulations generally, not the specific supervisory duty to investigate a subordinate's conduct. Option C describes the portfolio manager's own duty to his employer, not the supervisor's separate oversight obligation.
  55. The correct answer is C (Standard III(E), Preservation of Confidentiality): members must keep confidential all client information learned in the course of the professional relationship unless disclosure is required by law or the client consents. Casually mentioning a specific client's balance and trading activity to an outside acquaintance violates that duty regardless of the manager's intent. Option A concerns false or misleading statements about qualifications or services, not disclosure of true client information. Option B addresses undisclosed outside compensation, which is unrelated to this scenario.
  56. The correct answer is A (Standard VI(B), Priority of Transactions): client transactions must take priority over a member's personal and beneficial-owner transactions in the same securities. Filling his own account and family trust before any client orders directly subordinates client interests to his own, which this Standard specifically prohibits. Option B concerns matching investments to client circumstances, not the sequencing of trade allocation. Option C addresses maintaining documentation, which is not the issue in this allocation scenario.
  57. The correct answer is B (Standard I(C), Misrepresentation): members must not make any statement that misrepresents the performance of investment products or services, and knowingly claiming a losing fund has never had a down year is a clear false statement made to secure business. Option A concerns trading on undisclosed material information, which has nothing to do with a false performance claim. Option C addresses compensation for referring clients to other parties, an unrelated topic here.
  58. The correct answer is A (Standard III(D), Performance Presentation): members must ensure that performance information is fair, accurate, and complete, and presenting only cherry-picked top accounts as representative of overall results is materially misleading. Option B is a general catch-all for dishonest conduct but is less specific than the standard directly governing how performance results must be presented. Option C concerns a supervisor's duty to oversee subordinates, which is not the issue when the marketing team itself creates the misleading materials.
  59. The correct answer is C (Standard II(A), Material Nonpublic Information): members must not trade or cause others to trade on information that is both material and not yet available to the public, and merger news overheard before any announcement clearly qualifies. Option A concerns the adequacy of research supporting a recommendation, which is irrelevant since no legitimate research produced this information. Option B addresses maintaining independent judgment, not trading on inside information obtained by chance.
  60. The correct answer is B (Standard VI(C), Referral Fees): members must disclose to clients any compensation, consideration, or benefit received from recommending products or services to them, so the affected clients can weigh the objectivity of the referral. Failing to tell referred clients about the commission arrangement is precisely the omission this Standard is designed to prevent. Option A concerns the broader duty of prudent care in managing assets, not disclosure of referral compensation specifically. Option C addresses knowledge of applicable law, which is a separate and less precise fit.
  61. The correct answer is A (Standard I(C), Misrepresentation): falsely claiming to hold a professional credential the candidate has not actually earned is a straightforward misrepresentation of qualifications made to gain an advantage with employers. Option B governs how members and candidates who are legitimately entitled to reference the CFA Program describe that status, which does not apply since this candidate has not passed and is not entitled to claim the charter at all. Option C is a broad catch-all for dishonest conduct but is less specific than the standard that directly addresses false qualification claims.
  62. The correct answer is C (Standard III(A), Loyalty, Prudence, and Care): members owe clients a duty to seek best execution and act in their clients' best interests, and directing trades to a broker mainly for personal research benefits, without confirming clients still receive the best combination of price and execution, breaches that duty. Option A concerns the sequencing of personal versus client trades, not brokerage or soft-dollar arrangements. Option B addresses artificially affecting market prices, which is unrelated to a soft-dollar relationship.
  63. The correct answer is C (Standard IV(C), Responsibilities of Supervisors): a supervisor who becomes aware of repeated misconduct must take reasonable steps, such as restricting activity or escalating the matter, rather than issuing an informal reminder that fails to address the underlying pattern. Option A concerns equal treatment among clients, which is not the core issue when a supervisor's response to known misconduct is inadequate. Option B addresses general knowledge of law rather than the specific duty to respond effectively to detected violations.
  64. The correct answer is B (Standard I(B), Independence and Objectivity): members must not allow business relationships, such as an underwriting mandate, to compromise the objectivity of their research conclusions. Issuing a favorable rating despite known red flags because the firm has a financial stake in the company is a clear compromise of independent judgment. Option A concerns the depth of underlying research, but here the analyst actually found the red flag and ignored it rather than failing to look. Option C describes duties owed to the employer, not the objectivity of the analysis itself.
  65. The correct answer is A (Standard III(B), Fair Dealing): members must treat all clients fairly when disseminating investment recommendations and taking investment action, and giving the largest clients advance notice ahead of everyone else violates that equal-treatment obligation. Option B concerns disclosing conflicts of interest, which is not the issue when the manager is simply favoring some clients over others in timing. Option C is a general catch-all that is less specific than the standard directly addressing fair treatment in disseminating recommendations.
  66. The correct answer is B (Standard VII(A), Conduct as Members and Candidates in the CFA Program): candidates must not engage in any conduct that compromises the integrity of the CFA exam process, including cheating and then lying to proctors about it. Option A concerns misrepresenting facts to clients or employers about qualifications or services, which is a different context from exam-related misconduct. Option C addresses trading on undisclosed material information in financial markets, which has no bearing on exam-room behavior.
  67. The correct answer is C (Standard III(C), Suitability): members must consider a client's individual risk tolerance, objectives, and constraints before making investment recommendations, and defaulting every client into the same aggressive strategy regardless of circumstances ignores that duty entirely. Option A concerns the depth of research behind a recommendation, not whether it is matched to a specific client's needs. Option B addresses undisclosed outside compensation, which is unrelated to a firm-wide product-placement practice.
  68. The correct answer is A (Standard V(C), Record Retention): members must retain records supporting their investment analysis, recommendations, and other communications for the period required by applicable law or, absent such requirement, the period generally recommended by CFA Institute, which is longer than two years. Shredding the supporting workpapers this early violates that minimum retention obligation. Option B concerns protecting client information from disclosure, not the retention of internal analytical records. Option C addresses general knowledge of law rather than the specific record-keeping duty.
  69. Option C is a general catch-all for dishonest conduct that is less specific than the standard directly governing duties owed to an employer.
  70. Option A concerns fair and accurate presentation of investment results, which is unrelated to an undisclosed gift. Option B addresses artificially distorting market prices, which has no connection to this scenario.
  71.   72. Option C addresses false statements about a member's own qualifications or services, which is a different category of misconduct.

  79.   80.   81.   82.   83.   84.   90. Common ownership, shared personnel, and shared decision-making across the three units point toward defining them as a single firm for GIPS purposes despite separate brand names.   125. Grouping dissimilar strategies together purely to produce a longer or more favorable track record undermines this purpose and misleads prospective clients about what the composite actually represents. Option B incorrectly suggests firms have unrestricted discretion to define composites for marketing convenience. Option C invents a portfolio-count limit that is not the actual basis for why this grouping would be improper under GIPS.
  126. Trading on information overheard directly from a company insider, rather than derived through independent mosaic-theory analysis, falls squarely within this prohibition.   127. The correct answer is B (The Code recognizes that the profession's credibility depends on members promoting ethical conduct industry-wide.): The Code of Ethics requires members to practice and encourage others to practice in a professional and ethical manner, reflecting the understanding that public trust in the investment profession depends on ethical behavior being widespread, not merely followed individually by isolated members. This is why the Code extends beyond personal compliance to encouraging ethical conduct among colleagues and the broader industry. Option A incorrectly dismisses this as a mere formality, when it is an active component that can factor into professional conduct evaluations. Option C wrongly limits the requirement to compliance officers, when it applies broadly to all members and candidates.
  128. The correct answer is B (She must not knowingly participate or assist in the violation and should dissociate from the activity.): Standard I(A), Knowledge of the Law, requires members who become aware of violations of applicable law by others to dissociate from the activity, which may include steps such as reporting internally through appropriate channels, and to refrain from knowingly participating or assisting in the violation. Simply staying personally uninvolved is not enough once she has knowledge of ongoing misconduct. Option A understates her obligation once she has actual knowledge of the violation. Option C prescribes a specific direct-confrontation approach that is not what the Standard actually mandates, since dissociation and appropriate escalation are the required response.
  129. The correct answer is A (The firm should establish safeguards separating research compensation from trading volume and disclose the practice.): Standard I(B), Independence and Objectivity, is implicated when compensation structures create incentives that could compromise the objectivity of research, and firms are expected to establish reasonable safeguards, such as insulating research compensation from commission-generating trading volume, along with appropriate disclosure of any remaining conflict. This addresses the risk without requiring an outright prohibition on all commission-linked business models. Option B overstates the required remedy by demanding elimination rather than reasonable safeguards and disclosure. Option C incorrectly limits the Standard's application to individuals only, when firm-level structures that create these incentives are also relevant to the underlying independence concern.
  130. The correct answer is B (Standard I(C), Misrepresentation, because presenting others' work as one's own constitutes plagiarism.): Standard I(C), Misrepresentation, prohibits plagiarism, which includes copying original phrasing, analysis, or conclusions from another source and presenting them as one's own work without proper attribution. Directly lifting another analyst's original language and conclusions without credit falls squarely within this prohibition. Option A misplaces responsibility onto a supervisor for a failure that is fundamentally the associate's own act of misrepresentation. Option C is unrelated, since the facts describe copied analytical content rather than any misrepresentation of performance results specifically.
  131. The correct answer is C (Yes, because conduct involving fraud and deceit reflects adversely on professional integrity even outside employment.): Standard I(D), Misconduct, applies broadly to any conduct involving dishonesty, fraud, or deceit that reflects adversely on a member's professional reputation, integrity, or competence, regardless of whether the conduct occurs within the scope of employment or involves clients directly. A fraudulent real estate scheme against an unrelated party still speaks to the member's honesty and character. Option A incorrectly narrows the Standard to only work-related conduct, contrary to its actual scope. Option B similarly overstates a limitation that does not exist within Standard I(D).
  132. The correct answer is A (As material nonpublic information subject to restriction, since it was received directly from a company insider.): Standard II(A) treats specific, undisclosed financial figures conveyed directly and clearly by a company insider, such as an upcoming quarterly loss disclosed by the CFO in a private meeting, as material nonpublic information that the analyst must not trade on or pass along. This is distinguishable from the mosaic theory, which applies to nonmaterial pieces of information pieced together through independent analysis, not a single clear disclosure of material undisclosed facts from an insider. Option B misapplies the mosaic theory to a scenario involving a direct, unambiguous insider disclosure rather than assembled public information. Option C incorrectly suggests that omitting attribution removes the restriction, when the restriction depends on the nature of the information itself.
  133. The correct answer is B (Standard II(B), Market Manipulation, because the orders were designed to create a false appearance of demand.): Standard II(B), Market Manipulation, prohibits practices that distort prices or trading volume with the intent to mislead market participants, and this includes placing and rapidly cancelling orders solely to create a false impression of demand, a practice sometimes called spoofing. Option B correctly identifies this scheme's true nature. Option A misapplies a fair dealing rule that concerns treatment of a firm's own clients, not manipulation of the broader market. Option C incorrectly claims order cancellation itself is inherently unlawful, when the violation lies in the manipulative intent and effect, not the mechanical act of cancelling an order.
  134. The correct answer is C (No, because best execution requires evaluating total transaction quality, not just the lowest stated commission rate.): Standard III(A), Loyalty, Prudence, and Care, requires members to seek best execution for client trades, which involves evaluating the full range of brokerage services and total transaction costs, including execution quality, not simply selecting the broker with the lowest stated commission. Ignoring meaningfully better execution prices in favor of a marginally cheaper commission can produce worse overall outcomes for clients. Option A wrongly equates lowest commission with best execution, which is not how the duty is defined. Option B incorrectly excludes trade execution decisions from the scope of Standard III(A), when execution quality is a core part of this duty.
  135. The correct answer is C (Standard III(B), Fair Dealing, because clients must receive investment recommendation changes without unfair preferential treatment.): Standard III(B), Fair Dealing, requires members to treat all clients fairly when disseminating investment recommendations or changes to those recommendations, which means avoiding advance notice to a favored subset of clients ahead of the broader client base. Giving the largest institutional clients an early opportunity to sell before other clients learn of the downgrade is a clear breach of this fair dealing obligation. Option B misidentifies the issue as a documentation failure rather than the substantive unequal treatment of clients. Option A misapplies a compensation disclosure rule to a scenario that is actually about unequal access to information, not compensation.
  136. The correct answer is B (No, because the allocation must be evaluated against this specific client's stated objectives and risk tolerance.): Standard III(C), Suitability, requires members to evaluate an investment or strategy for its suitability against the specific client's investment policy statement, including stated risk tolerance and objectives, rather than basing allocation decisions on what has worked well for other, differently situated clients. Allocating heavily to speculative small-cap equities directly contradicts this client's stated moderate risk tolerance and capital preservation objective. Option A incorrectly treats another account's performance as a valid basis for this client's suitability determination. Option C wrongly suggests after-the-fact notice cures a suitability violation that occurred at the time of the investment decision.
  137. The correct answer is B (The claim of GIPS compliance must be true, and results must be calculated and presented accordingly.): Standard III(D), Performance Presentation, works together with the broader duty of fair and accurate presentation, requiring that a claim of GIPS compliance be substantively true, meaning the firm's performance results must in fact be calculated and presented in accordance with GIPS requirements across the composites for which compliance is claimed. Making the claim without the underlying substance would itself be a form of misleading performance presentation. Option A incorrectly suggests the obligation ends once the claim is made, ignoring the ongoing duty for accuracy. Option C wrongly permits a partial, selective compliance claim to be presented as if it applied broadly.
  138. The correct answer is A (She should consider that confidentiality does not require concealing knowledge of a client's intended illegal activities.): Standard III(E), Preservation of Confidentiality, generally protects client information but is not intended to shield knowledge of a client's illegal activities, and members should consider applicable law along with firm policy in determining an appropriate response when they reasonably believe a client intends unlawful conduct. Treating confidentiality as an absolute bar in this situation misunderstands the purpose and limits of the duty. Option B incorrectly claims the duty admits no exceptions whatsoever. Option C wrongly makes client consent the only path to disclosure, when legal and firm-policy considerations, not client permission, govern this situation.
  139. The correct answer is C (This is a violation because she is actively competing against her employer while still employed there.): Standard IV(A), Loyalty to Employer, prohibits members from undertaking independent competitive activity that could injure the employer while still employed, including secretly soliciting the employer's own clients to move to a competing business the member is establishing. This conduct directly harms the employer's client relationships and business interests during the employment relationship. Option A incorrectly treats all outside business activity as automatically permissible, ignoring the specific harm of soliciting current clients away from the employer. Option B wrongly suggests eventual disclosure cures a violation that is occurring in real time while she remains employed and undisclosed.
  140. The correct answer is B (She must obtain her employer's written consent before accepting this additional compensation from the client.): Standard IV(B), Additional Compensation Arrangements, requires members to obtain written consent from their employer before accepting compensation or other benefits from clients that could create a conflict with the employer's interests, and this obligation applies whether the additional compensation comes from a client or another third party. Accepting a bonus tied to prioritizing one client's account without employer consent creates exactly this kind of conflict. Option A incorrectly assumes client-sourced compensation is exempt from the disclosure requirement, which it is not. Option C overstates the rule by treating all client-sourced compensation as banned rather than subject to disclosure and consent.
  141. The correct answer is C (No, because delegation does not relieve the supervisor of ultimate responsibility for adequate oversight and follow-up.): Standard IV(C), Responsibilities of Supervisors, allows supervisors to delegate supervisory duties, but delegation does not relieve the supervisor of the ultimate responsibility for adequate compliance procedures being in place and followed effectively. Repeated undetected violations over several months suggest the oversight system, for which the supervisor remained responsible, was inadequate. Option A incorrectly treats delegation as a complete discharge of supervisory duty, contrary to how the Standard actually operates. Option B wrongly narrows the Standard to only direct personal observation, when the duty extends to establishing and maintaining adequate systems of oversight.
  142. The correct answer is C (No, a reasonable basis requires testing key assumptions rather than relying solely on management's optimistic projections.): Standard V(A), Diligence and Reasonable Basis, requires members to have a thorough, reasonably independent basis for investment analysis, which includes critically evaluating and stress testing key assumptions rather than adopting a company's own optimistic projections uncritically, especially in a cyclical industry where growth assumptions can vary significantly across the cycle. Relying solely on management's rosy projection without independent testing falls short of this diligence requirement. Option B incorrectly assumes company-sourced figures are automatically sufficient, when independent scrutiny is required. Option A invents a categorical prohibition on discounted cash flow models that does not exist under the Standard.
  143. The correct answer is A (The interest rate expectation must be clearly identified as an opinion, distinct from verifiable historical facts.): Standard V(B), Communication with Clients, requires members to clearly separate opinions, such as forward-looking interest rate expectations, from facts, such as historical expense ratios and realized returns, so clients can appropriately judge the reliability of each type of statement. Presenting a subjective forecast in the same unqualified tone as verified historical data blurs this important distinction. Option B incorrectly suggests sincere belief alone satisfies the requirement, when clear labeling is what the Standard actually demands. Option C overstates the remedy, since forward-looking opinions are permitted under Standard V(B) as long as they are properly identified as opinion.
  144. The correct answer is C (No, because such records generally belong to the firm, and removal is a firm property matter.): Standard V(C), Record Retention, and the related duty of loyalty under Standard IV(A) generally treat research files and work papers created during employment as records belonging to the firm, meaning the departing analyst's authorship does not entitle her to remove or retain copies without the firm's permission. This protects the firm's proprietary work product and client-related records. Option A incorrectly treats individual authorship as creating a personal ownership right over firm records. Option B invents a twelve-month retention compromise that has no basis in the Standard's actual treatment of firm records.
  145. The correct answer is B (No, disclosures must be presented clearly and prominently enough to be reasonably understood, not technically included.): Standard VI(A), Disclosure of Conflicts, requires that conflicts be disclosed clearly, prominently, and in a manner reasonably likely to be understood, rather than technically included in dense boilerplate that clients are unlikely to read or comprehend. Burying the disclosure on page thirty of a lengthy document without plain-language reinforcement during onboarding fails this standard of effective communication. Option A incorrectly treats any inclusion, however obscure, as automatically sufficient. Option C invents a verbal repetition requirement at every meeting that the Standard does not actually impose.
  146. The correct answer is B (No, IPOs are limited opportunities that warrant specific restrictions or preclearance to protect client priority.): Standard VI(B), Priority of Transactions, requires firms to establish policies that give client transactions priority over personal transactions, and because IPO allocations are inherently limited and highly sought after, firms typically need specific restrictions or a preclearance process to prevent employees from crowding out or competing with client access to these scarce opportunities. Simply applying the same general terms without tailored safeguards does not adequately protect client priority in this context. Option A wrongly assumes equal general terms alone are sufficient. Option C incorrectly excludes IPO allocations from the Standard's scope, when limited offerings are a primary concern the Standard is meant to address.
  147. The correct answer is A (No, disclosure should include enough detail, such as the nature and value of the benefit.): Standard VI(C), Referral Fees, requires members to disclose referral arrangements with enough specificity, generally including the nature and, where reasonably determinable, the value of the benefit received, so clients can meaningfully evaluate the adviser's objectivity in making the referral. A vague acknowledgment that 'an arrangement exists,' without describing its structure or value, does not give the client enough information to assess the conflict. Option B understates the required level of detail the Standard actually calls for. Option C incorrectly claims referral fees from accounting firms are categorically banned, when the actual issue here is inadequate disclosure, not a prohibited fee source.
  148. The correct answer is C (Yes, comments about difficulty and format are permitted as long as specific content is not disclosed.): Standard VII(A) prohibits candidates from disclosing specific, confidential exam content, but it does not prohibit general, non-specific comments about overall difficulty or format that do not reveal actual question topics or wording. This kind of general conversation does not compromise the security or validity of future exam administrations. Option A overstates the prohibition, treating even non-specific general comments as a violation, which is not how the Standard is applied. Option B invents an employer-relationship restriction that has no basis in the actual scope of Standard VII(A).
  149. The correct answer is B (No, verification is generally not required to claim compliance, though it provides independent assurance if obtained.): Under GIPS, a firm may generally claim compliance based on its own determination that it meets the requirements, since independent verification, while strongly encouraged and providing additional credibility and assurance to prospective clients, is not itself a strict precondition for claiming compliance. This distinguishes the compliance claim itself from the separate, optional verification process. Option A incorrectly treats verification as an absolute mandatory precondition, which overstates its actual role. Option C invents an asset-size threshold for verification that is not the basis for GIPS's treatment of verification as optional.
  150. The correct answer is A (Standard VI(A), Disclosure of Conflicts, because both relationships create conflicts that must be disclosed to clients.): Standard VI(A), Disclosure of Conflicts, is the most directly applicable Standard here because both the board membership and the speaking fees are actual or potential conflicts of interest affecting her objectivity toward clients who hold investments in the same company, and both must be disclosed clearly to those clients. While other Standards, such as independence and objectivity, may also be implicated, disclosure of these specific conflicts is the central requirement raised by these facts. Option B misapplies a confidentiality rule that does not govern conflict disclosure obligations of this kind. Option C invents a securities law registration requirement that is not the issue these facts actually raise.
  151. The correct answer is A (Decline the reallocation because it fails to serve the client's best interests despite the firm benefit): Standard III(A), Loyalty, Prudence, and Care, requires members to act for the benefit of clients and place client interests before the interests of the member's firm. A reallocation driven by higher fees to the firm rather than any demonstrable improvement in the client's outcome subordinates the client's interest to the firm's revenue goal. The manager must be able to justify any recommendation on the basis of the client's needs, objectives, and circumstances, not internal profitability. Proceeding, or even partially proceeding, with an unjustified higher-fee product still breaches this duty.
  152. The correct answer is B (Standard IV(A), Loyalty, because undisclosed competing activity harms the employer's interests): Standard IV(A) requires members to act for the benefit of their employer and not deprive the employer of the member's skills and abilities, engage in conduct that injures the employer, or compete directly with the employer, unless the employer consents. Operating an undisclosed advisory business that competes for the same client base uses the member's time and expertise against her employer's interest. While related law or disclosure concerns could arguably apply, the core violation here is disloyalty to the employer through undisclosed competition. Members undertaking independent practice must obtain permission and disclose the nature, scope, and duration of the activity.
  153. The correct answer is C (Standard I(B), Independence and Objectivity, because the gift is conditioned on a favorable outcome): Standard I(B) prohibits members from accepting gifts, benefits, or compensation that could reasonably be expected to compromise their own or their firm's independence and objectivity. A trip explicitly conditioned on issuing a favorable report is a direct attempt to influence the analyst's professional judgment. Even absent an outright bribe, any gift tied to a specific research outcome creates an unacceptable conflict that must be avoided or, at minimum, fully disclosed. The resulting report's independence, not merely its underlying research quality, is what is most directly compromised.
  154. The correct answer is B (Standard III(B), Fair Dealing, because clients were not treated fairly in the allocation process): Standard III(B) requires members to deal fairly and objectively with all clients when disseminating investment recommendations or taking investment action, including allocating limited investment opportunities such as oversubscribed IPOs. Fair dealing does not require identical treatment for every client, but it does require a fair and consistent allocation methodology, such as pro rata allocation based on account size, applied across similarly situated clients. Systematically excluding smaller retail clients with objectives similar to the favored institutional accounts is inconsistent with this fairness requirement. The firm should have a documented, equitable allocation policy applied uniformly.
  155. The correct answer is A (Obtain written consent from her employer before accepting the arrangement): Standard IV(B), Additional Compensation Arrangements, requires members to obtain written consent from their employer before accepting compensation or other benefits from clients that are in addition to compensation from the employer, if the arrangement could create a conflict with the employer's interest. A separate bonus tied to prioritizing one client's account over others is exactly the kind of arrangement that could compromise the advisor's objectivity toward other clients. Written consent, describing the terms, duration, and nature of the compensation, must be obtained before or promptly upon accepting the arrangement. Simply accepting the payment because it comes from the client rather than a third party does not satisfy this requirement.
  156. The correct answer is A (Disclose the board position to clients before providing any related investment advice): Standard VI(A), Disclosure of Conflicts, requires members to disclose to clients all matters that reasonably could be expected to impair independence and objectivity or interfere with duties owed to clients. A board seat at a company clients are considering investing in is a material conflict that could influence the charterholder's advice. Disclosure must be made proactively and clearly, not only when a client happens to ask, and it must occur before advice is given so the client can weigh the potential bias. Resignation is not required by the Standard; disclosure that allows the client to evaluate the conflict is the required remedy.
  157. The correct answer is C (Suitability, because the investments were not consistent with the client's stated objectives and constraints): Standard III(C), Suitability, requires members to make a reasonable inquiry into a client's investment objectives, risk tolerance, time horizon, and constraints and to ensure that investment recommendations are consistent with those factors and with the client's investment policy statement. Applying a template appropriate for aggressive clients to a client with a short horizon and low risk tolerance directly disregards this individualized suitability analysis. While the scenario does not involve misrepresented facts or personal gain, it clearly reflects a failure to tailor the portfolio to this specific client's documented profile. The advisor should reassess the strategy against the client's own objectives before implementation.
  158. The correct answer is B (establish and enforce reasonable procedures to detect and prevent the violations): Standard IV(C), Responsibilities of Supervisors, requires supervisors to make reasonable efforts to detect and prevent violations of applicable laws, rules, regulations, and the Code and Standards by those under their supervision. Merely receiving compliance information without investigating or acting on it, and assuming someone else will address it, fails this affirmative duty to monitor and respond. A supervisor cannot delegate responsibility by inaction or by assuming another manager will eventually notice the problem. Reasonable supervisory systems require prompt follow-up on flagged irregularities such as repeated position-limit breaches.
  159. The correct answer is C (Standard II(A), Material Nonpublic Information, by communicating information that could be used to trade): Standard II(A) prohibits members who possess material nonpublic information from acting or causing others to act on that information, which includes communicating it to others who could then trade on it, commonly known as tipping. The merger information here is both material, since it would likely affect the target's stock price, and nonpublic, since it arose from confidential negotiations. Telling a friend who then trades on it is a direct violation of the mosaic and confidentiality principles underlying II(A), regardless of whether the banker herself traded. This is distinct from a general confidentiality breach because the information's market-moving, nonpublic nature is the defining feature of the violation.
  160. The correct answer is A (Standard III(A), Loyalty, Prudence, and Care, by misusing client brokerage for personal benefit): Standard III(A) requires members to use client brokerage for the benefit of clients, including directing commissions in a manner that reflects best execution and legitimate client benefits such as research, rather than personal perks unrelated to investment services. Directing commissions specifically to obtain personal dinners and travel diverts client assets, in the form of commission dollars, toward the manager's own benefit rather than the client's. This is a breach of the duty of loyalty owed directly to clients, since client brokerage is being used to enrich the manager personally. Proper use of soft dollars is limited to services that directly assist in the investment decision-making process for client accounts.
  161. The correct answer is B (Standard V(A), Diligence and Reasonable Basis, because the recommendation lacks independent, thorough analysis): Standard V(A) requires members to exercise diligence, independence, and thoroughness in analyzing investments and to have a reasonable and adequate basis for any recommendation. Relying solely on a company's own investor presentation, without independent verification of assumptions or comparison against external data, does not constitute a reasonable basis for a published recommendation. This is a fundamental failure of investment research process rather than a false performance claim or a guarantee of results. Analysts must corroborate management's projections with independent sources before issuing formal recommendations.
  162. The correct answer is B (Standard III(B), Fair Dealing, because some clients received the information before others): Standard III(B) requires that investment recommendations and changes to prior recommendations be disseminated to clients in a manner that treats all clients fairly, without favoring certain clients over others based on size or relationship. Providing a twenty-minute advance notice to the largest institutional clients allows them to trade ahead of the general client base on the same rating change, disadvantaging retail and smaller clients. This is a classic fair dealing violation because it concerns the timing and manner of dissemination rather than the content or independence of the research itself. Firms should use simultaneous or near-simultaneous distribution methods to avoid this kind of unequal treatment.
  163. The correct answer is A (Standard IV(A), Loyalty, by taking employer records that constitute confidential firm property): Standard IV(A) prohibits members from misappropriating employer records, trade secrets, or proprietary information, including client lists and account records compiled during employment, for use in future competitive endeavors. Client contact information and account histories developed using the employer's resources belong to the firm, not personally to the departing advisor. Removing such records to solicit the same clients at a new employer constitutes disloyal misappropriation of employer property, independent of any separate confidentiality concern about the clients themselves. Members may generally use only public information and their own recollection of general client relationships when transitioning to a new firm.
  164. The correct answer is C (Standard VII(A), Conduct as Members and Candidates in the CFA Program, by misrepresenting her exam status): Standard VII(A) prohibits members and candidates from engaging in conduct that compromises the reputation or integrity of CFA Institute, the CFA designation, or the CFA Program, which includes misrepresenting one's status in the program, such as claiming to have passed an exam that was only attempted. Stating that she "successfully completed" Level I when she in fact failed is a clear misrepresentation of her exam results and progress in the program. This differs from a general employment misstatement because it directly concerns the integrity of the CFA credentialing process itself. Candidates must accurately describe their pass/fail status and level of completion at all times.
  165. The correct answer is C (Standard III(E), Preservation of Confidentiality, by disclosing client information without a permissible reason): Standard III(E) requires members to keep information about current, former, and prospective clients confidential unless disclosure is required by law, the client consents, or the information concerns illegal activity. Casually mentioning details of a client's confidential pending business sale to an unrelated third party at a social event has no permissible basis under the Standard. This is a straightforward breach of client confidentiality rather than a market-abuse issue, since nothing indicates the listener traded or that the information was even still material and nonpublic by the time of the disclosure. Members must safeguard client information learned in the course of the professional relationship regardless of the informal setting in which it might be shared.
  166. The correct answer is A (still a violation, because it reflects dishonesty that reflects adversely on professional reputation): Standard I(D), Misconduct, prohibits members from engaging in any professional or personal conduct involving dishonesty, fraud, or deceit that reflects adversely on their professional reputation, integrity, or competence, even when the conduct is unrelated to their employment or clients. Falsifying tax documents is an act of dishonesty and fraud that calls into question the manager's integrity as an investment professional, regardless of the personal, non-work context. The Standard is deliberately broad enough to capture personal conduct of this nature, not limited to workplace or client-related actions. Public reporting is not a prerequisite for the violation to exist, though it may affect the practical consequences.
  167. The correct answer is B (obtain written consent from his employer describing the nature and terms of the compensation): Standard IV(B) requires members to obtain written consent from their employer before accepting compensation or other benefits from a source other than the employer that relates to, or might reasonably be expected to create a conflict with, the employer's interests. Serving as a paid advisor to a client's foundation, separate from his regular salary, is exactly the type of outside compensation the Standard addresses, since it originates from a client relationship connected to his employment. The consent must describe the compensation's nature, amount, and duration so the employer can assess any potential conflict. Routing payment through the employer's payroll is not itself required; obtaining informed written consent is the operative obligation.
  168. The correct answer is A (Standard III(C), Suitability, because the recommendation conflicts with the client's stated preferences): Standard III(C) requires that investment recommendations be suitable for the client, taking into account the client's investment objectives, risk tolerance, and stated preferences such as diversification and volatility limits. Concentrating 60% of a portfolio in one volatile stock, when the client has explicitly requested diversification and low volatility, directly contradicts these documented preferences regardless of the stock's recent performance. Recent strong performance does not justify overriding a client's clearly stated risk parameters. The advisor must align portfolio construction with the client's investment policy statement rather than chasing recent returns.
  169. The correct answer is C (Standard VI(B), Priority of Transactions, by trading ahead of clients on the pending recommendation): Standard VI(B) requires that client transactions take priority over transactions in securities or investments in which a member has a beneficial ownership interest, meaning personal trades should not be made ahead of trades benefiting clients on the same information. Purchasing shares for her personal account the evening before a firm-wide buy recommendation is disseminated to clients allows the manager to benefit personally from information before clients have the opportunity to act on it. This is a front-running violation specific to the ordering of personal versus client transactions, distinct from a pure independence or asset-management concern. Firms typically require blackout periods or pre-clearance procedures to prevent exactly this kind of trading ahead of clients.
  170. The correct answer is B (Standard III(D), Performance Presentation, because the results shown are not fair, accurate, or complete): Standard III(D) requires members to make reasonable efforts to ensure that performance information communicated to clients or prospective clients is fair, accurate, and complete. Highlighting only the three best-performing composites while omitting eleven other composites with materially weaker results from the same period creates a misleading picture of the firm's overall track record. This is a selective, cherry-picked presentation problem rather than outright fabrication of specific numbers, which distinguishes it from a Standard I(C) misrepresentation. Full and fair presentation requires disclosing the broader performance context, not just favorable outliers.
  171. The correct answer is A (Standard IV(C), Responsibilities of Supervisors, by failing to investigate reasonable indications of misconduct): Standard IV(C) obligates supervisors to make reasonable efforts to detect and prevent violations by those they supervise, which includes following up on compliance alerts and other reasonable indications of potential wrongdoing rather than dismissing them without inquiry. Repeatedly ignoring flagged unusual trading volume, later confirmed to reflect churning, shows a clear failure of this affirmative supervisory duty. The manager did not personally mismanage client assets or act dishonestly himself, which distinguishes this from Standards III(A) or I(D); the violation is specifically supervisory inaction. Reasonable supervisory systems require prompt, documented investigation of red flags such as these.
  172. The correct answer is B (comply with the more strict of the applicable law and the Code and Standards): Standard I(A), Knowledge of the Law, requires members to comply with the more strict of applicable law, rules, and regulations, including those of any governing regulatory organization, and the CFA Institute Code and Standards. When local law permits conduct that is less restrictive than the Code and Standards, the member must still adhere to the stricter Code and Standards requirement. Members may not use permissive local law as an excuse to disregard higher CFA Institute ethical requirements. The Standard establishes a floor set by the more stringent of the two frameworks, not a choice between them.
  173. The correct answer is C (Standard III(A), Loyalty, Prudence, and Care, because best execution requires more than lowest cost alone): Standard III(A) requires members to seek best execution for client transactions, which encompasses more than the lowest commission rate and includes factors such as execution quality, timing, market impact, and the broker's overall capability to handle order size and complexity. Routing all trades based solely on commission cost, without evaluating these other execution factors, fails this duty of prudence and care owed to clients. This is particularly problematic for larger orders where poor execution quality can materially erode returns despite low nominal commissions. Members must periodically evaluate brokers on a holistic basis rather than cost alone.
  174. The correct answer is A (Standard II(B), Market Manipulation, by artificially distorting the security's closing price): Standard II(B) prohibits members from engaging in practices that distort prices or artificially inflate trading volume with the intent to mislead market participants, which includes marking the close, where large orders are placed near the end of trading specifically to manipulate the closing price. Placing and then largely canceling orders solely to inflate the closing price for reported performance purposes is a textbook example of this prohibited conduct. The performance reporting consequence that follows is a downstream effect, but the core violation is the price manipulation itself. Genuine market manipulation undermines the integrity of the capital markets that Standard II is designed to protect.
  175. The correct answer is C (Standard IV(A), Loyalty, by soliciting the employer's clients while still employed there): Standard IV(A) prohibits members from soliciting their current employer's clients on behalf of a future employer while still employed, since doing so undermines the employer's business interests before the employment relationship has formally ended. Contacting top clients about following her to a new firm, even discreetly and before resigning, constitutes prohibited solicitation of the current employer's clients. Members may take reasonable steps to prepare for departure, such as arranging personal logistics, but soliciting clients before resignation crosses into disloyal competitive conduct. This differs from a pure fair-dealing or general misconduct issue because the core harm is disloyalty to the current employer's client relationships.
  176. The correct answer is B (Standard III(B), Fair Dealing, because fair dealing requires fairness in both time and treatment): Standard III(B) requires that changes in investment recommendations be disseminated to clients in a manner designed to treat all clients fairly, which includes both the substance of the information and the timing of its delivery. Systematically giving institutional clients a ninety-minute head start over retail clients, even under an operational justification, creates an unequal opportunity to act on the same information. Operational convenience does not excuse a dissemination practice that consistently disadvantages one group of clients. Firms should design distribution processes, such as simultaneous release methods, that minimize timing disparities between client segments.
  177. The correct answer is A (disclose the referral fee arrangement to clients before the referral is made): Standard VI(C), Referral Fees, requires members to disclose to clients any compensation, consideration, or benefit received from or paid to others for the recommendation of products or services, including referral arrangements with other professionals. Clients being referred to the attorney have a right to know that the advisor receives a percentage of their legal fees, since this could influence the objectivity of the referral itself. Disclosure must occur before or at the time of the referral, not only if the client happens to ask. Referral fee arrangements are not prohibited outright; the Standard's core requirement is transparent disclosure so clients can evaluate the referral's independence.
  178. The correct answer is B (Standard III(C), Suitability, because the portfolio no longer reflects the client's current circumstances): Standard III(C) requires members to periodically review and update a client's investment policy statement and to ensure the portfolio remains suitable as the client's circumstances, objectives, and risk tolerance change over time. Retirement, a shift to drawing income from the portfolio, and a significantly reduced risk tolerance are all material changes that should trigger a reassessment of asset allocation. Failing to revisit the portfolio for four years despite these substantial life changes leaves the client's investments misaligned with his current needs. Suitability is an ongoing obligation, not a one-time determination made only at account opening.
  179. The correct answer is C (the gift could reasonably be expected to affect her loyalty or judgment, requiring employer disclosure): Standard I(B), Independence and Objectivity, requires members to disclose to their employer any gift or benefit from a client that could reasonably be expected to compromise their independence, objectivity, or loyalty. There is no fixed CFA Institute dollar threshold that automatically triggers or exempts disclosure; the analysis is based on whether the gift could reasonably compromise the advisor's loyalty or judgment. An unsolicited personal gift unrelated to a transaction may still require disclosure if its value or nature could reasonably influence the relationship. Written documentation from the client is not a substitute for the advisor's own obligation to inform her employer.
  180.   1. The correct answer is C (3.00): the slope coefficient in simple linear regression equals the covariance between X and Y divided by the variance of X, or 45 ÷ 15 = 3.00. This formula shows how much Y is expected to change for each one-unit change in X, based on their statistical relationship. Option A (0.33) incorrectly inverts the ratio, computing 15 ÷ 45 instead. Option B (60.00) mistakenly adds the covariance and variance together rather than dividing them.
  2. The correct answer is A (0.64): the coefficient of determination equals the correlation coefficient squared, or R-squared = 0.80^2 = 0.64. This value indicates that 64% of the variation in the dependent variable is explained by the independent variable in the regression model.   3. The correct answer is B (3.33): the standard error of estimate equals the square root of SSE divided by the degrees of freedom, or √(200 ÷ 18) = √11.111 = 3.33. Option C (3.16) mistakenly divides SSE by the total number of observations, 20, rather than by the correct degrees of freedom, 18.
  4. The correct answer is C (0.50): the correlation coefficient equals covariance divided by the product of the two standard deviations, or 12 ÷ (4 × 6) = 12 ÷ 24 = 0.50. This standardized measure captures the strength and direction of the linear relationship between X and Y. Option A (2.00) incorrectly inverts the ratio, computing 24 ÷ 12 instead. Option B (3.00) mistakenly divides the covariance by only one of the two standard deviations, 12 ÷ 4, ignoring the other.
  5. The correct answer is A (0.80): for mutually exclusive events, the addition rule states P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) = 0.50 + 0.30 = 0.80, since the two events cannot occur simultaneously and there is no overlap to subtract. This simple sum applies only because mutual exclusivity rules out any joint occurrence. Option B (0.15) mistakenly multiplies the two probabilities together as though testing for a joint, not a union, probability. Option C (0.20) incorrectly computes the complement of the correct sum, 1 − 0.80, which answers a different question entirely.
  6. The correct answer is A (0.80): for events that are not mutually exclusive, the addition rule requires subtracting the joint probability to avoid double-counting, giving P(A or B) = 0.60 + 0.50 − 0.30 = 0.80. This adjustment ensures the overlapping outcomes where both A and B occur are counted only once. Option B (1.10) forgets to subtract the joint probability of 0.30 entirely, simply summing the two individual probabilities. Option C (0.50) mistakenly subtracts the joint probability of 0.30 twice from the sum of 1.10.
  7. Because those cash flow timing decisions are made by the client rather than the manager, this measure isolates the manager's own investment decisions. Option A describes the money-weighted return, which is heavily influenced by the size and timing of client cash flows and better reflects the client's own realized return. Option C, a simple holding period return, does not properly account for the compounding effect of interim cash flows at all.

This property allows analysts to apply normal-distribution-based inference techniques, such as confidence intervals and hypothesis tests, to sample means from many types of populations. Option A incorrectly claims the sampling distribution becomes more skewed, the opposite of what actually occurs as sample size grows. Option B incorrectly claims variance increases with sample size, when in fact the variance of the sampling distribution decreases as sample size grows.

This produces the characteristic ordering mean greater than median greater than mode. Option A describes a perfectly symmetric distribution, which does not apply to a skewed distribution by definition. Option C reverses the correct ordering, which instead describes a negatively skewed distribution with a long left tail.

Selecting a lower significance level directly reduces the chance of committing this type of error. Option B describes a Type II error, which occurs when a false null hypothesis is not rejected, and its probability is not controlled by sample size alone. Option C mislabels the error as a generic sampling error and incorrectly claims it is unrelated to the significance level, when the two are directly linked by definition.

The chi-square distribution's shape reflects the specific sampling behavior of variance estimates.
  43. Regression coefficients describe association in one direction and do not by themselves establish reverse causation.

The correct answer is C (5.04%, subtracting 1.645 standard errors from the sample mean): the standard error equals 1.0% ÷ √100 = 1.0% ÷ 10 = 0.10%, and the lower bound equals 5.2% minus (1.645 × 0.10%) = 5.2% minus 0.1645% ≈ 5.04%. The full critical z-value must be multiplied by the standard error before subtracting it from the sample mean. Using a rounded margin or ignoring the z-multiplier entirely would produce an inaccurate confidence interval bound.
  45. The correct answer is B (50.0%, the joint probability of a growth outperformer over the total outperform probability): the total probability of outperformance equals (0.40 × 0.30) + (0.60 × 0.20) = 0.12 + 0.12 = 0.24. Applying Bayes' theorem, the probability the pick is a growth stock given outperformance equals 0.12 ÷ 0.24 = 0.50, or 50.0%. Despite growth stocks representing a smaller share of total picks, their higher outperformance rate offsets that smaller weighting exactly in this case.

Every possible outcome must be weighted by its own probability and included in the sum. Averaging only the positive outcomes, or all outcomes equally, would ignore the actual likelihood of each scenario.

This measures excess return earned per unit of total risk taken. Dividing the standard deviation by the excess return, or using total return without subtracting the risk-free rate, would produce an incorrect ratio.

The correct answer is C (16, the probability-weighted sum of squared deviations from the expected return): the expected return equals (0.50 × 10%) + (0.50 × 2%) = 6%, and the variance equals 0.50 × (10% minus 6%)² + 0.50 × (2% minus 6%)² = 0.50 × 16 + 0.50 × 16 = 8 + 8 = 16. Each squared deviation from the mean must be weighted by its own probability before summing. Simply taking the difference between outcomes, without squaring and weighting, does not produce a valid variance.

Because this calculated t-statistic of 3.00 exceeds the approximate critical value of 2.0, the slope coefficient would be judged statistically significant. Inverting the ratio, or adding the two values instead of dividing, would not produce a valid test statistic.
  51. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Elastic demand; a price increase would decrease total revenue because quantity falls proportionally more than price rises: the price elasticity coefficient here is 8% ÷ 4% = 2.0 in magnitude, which exceeds 1 and therefore classifies demand as elastic. Because the percentage decline in quantity demanded outweighs the percentage increase in price, total revenue (price × quantity) falls when price rises. This is the opposite relationship found under inelastic demand, where revenue moves in the same direction as price. Firms facing elastic demand generally avoid raising price if revenue maximization is the goal.
  52. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Inferior good, because quantity demanded moves in the opposite direction from income: since purchases of canned soup fall by 6% as income rises by 10%, the income elasticity of demand is negative, which is the defining characteristic of an inferior good. The premium organic soup, by contrast, shows a positive income elasticity and qualifies as a normal good. A Giffen good is a special, rare case of an inferior good where quantity demanded rises as its own price rises, which is not what is described here. The sign of income elasticity, not its magnitude alone, determines the normal-versus-inferior classification.
  53. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Substitutes, because consumers shift toward tea when coffee becomes relatively more expensive: a positive cross-price elasticity of demand means that a price increase in one good is associated with an increase in quantity demanded of the other good, which is the definition of substitute goods. Complements exhibit a negative cross-price elasticity because a price rise in one good reduces demand for the good typically consumed alongside it. Independent goods have a cross-price elasticity close to zero since a price change in one has little measurable effect on the other. The magnitude of +1.8 also indicates the substitution relationship is fairly strong.
  54. The new intersection of supply and demand occurs at a higher equilibrium price and a lower equilibrium quantity than before the shock. This outcome follows directly from comparative statics analysis of a leftward supply shift holding demand constant. A simultaneous rise in both price and quantity would instead require an outward shift of demand, not a reduction in supply.
  55. This gap between quantity demanded and quantity supplied is the defining feature of a shortage and tends to persist as long as the ceiling remains binding. A surplus would instead arise from a price floor set above equilibrium, not a ceiling below it. The market cannot clear at the ceiling price precisely because the ceiling prevents the price mechanism from equilibrating supply and demand.

The firm should therefore expand output until the two are equal at the margin. Decreasing output would be appropriate only if marginal cost exceeded marginal revenue, sacrificing profitable units. Comparing marginal revenue to average total cost rather than marginal cost would give an incorrect signal for the optimal output decision.

This confirms that for a linear demand curve, marginal revenue falls twice as fast as price as output expands, so marginal revenue lies below the demand curve at every positive quantity. A marginal revenue of $60 or $40 would require either a smaller quantity or a misapplication of the doubling coefficient in the marginal revenue formula. This relationship is central to understanding why a monopolist's optimal quantity differs from the competitive outcome.
  60.
