Producing fewer than 40 units, such as 20, would leave marginal revenue above marginal cost and forgo profitable additional output. Producing more than 40 units, such as 60, would push marginal cost above the market price and reduce profit on those extra units. The profit-maximizing rule of setting price equal to marginal cost applies specifically because the firm operates in a perfectly competitive market and cannot influence price.
  61. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Perfect competition, because entry continues until price equals minimum average total cost: the description of many small firms, a homogeneous product, and free entry and exit are the defining structural characteristics of perfect competition. In long-run equilibrium, free entry drives economic profit to zero because new firms enter whenever positive economic profit exists, shifting industry supply until price falls to the minimum point of each firm's average total cost curve. A monopoly involves a single seller restricting output, which contradicts the presence of many small competitors described here. Oligopoly involves a small number of interdependent firms, which is also inconsistent with the scenario's many-firm structure.
  62. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A higher price and a lower output level than the competitive outcome: a monopolist restricts output below the competitive quantity to raise price above marginal cost, since it faces the entire downward-sloping market demand curve rather than a flat price-taking demand curve. This restriction generates the monopoly's economic profit but also produces a deadweight loss relative to the competitive equilibrium. A competitive firm, by contrast, expands output until price equals marginal cost, producing more output at a lower price than a monopolist facing the same demand and cost conditions. The same price and output outcome would only occur if the monopolist behaved as a price taker, which contradicts its market power.
  63. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Total economic surplus, which is maximized at the competitive equilibrium in the absence of externalities: consumer surplus captures the value buyers receive above what they pay, and producer surplus captures the value sellers receive above their cost, so their sum represents the total net benefit generated by the market. At the competitive equilibrium quantity, this combined surplus is maximized because every unit for which a buyer's willingness to pay exceeds a seller's cost is produced and traded. Deadweight loss instead refers to surplus lost when the market quantity deviates from this efficient level, such as under a tax, price control, or monopoly restriction. Total revenue is a distinct concept measured only as price times quantity and does not incorporate cost or willingness-to-pay information.
  64. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Deadweight loss: when a per-unit tax raises the effective price to buyers and lowers the effective price received by sellers, the quantity traded falls below the efficient competitive level, eliminating some mutually beneficial transactions that would otherwise have occurred. The lost surplus from these forgone trades, which accrues to neither the government, consumers, nor producers, is defined as deadweight loss. Consumer surplus and producer surplus are the remaining areas of net benefit still captured by buyers and sellers after the tax, not the loss itself. The size of the deadweight loss generally increases with the elasticity of supply and demand and with the size of the tax.
  65. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Marginal cost intersects average variable cost precisely at its minimum point: this is a standard cost-curve relationship in microeconomics, where the marginal cost curve crosses the average variable cost curve exactly at the output level where average variable cost is minimized. Below this output, marginal cost lies below average variable cost and pulls the average down; above this output, marginal cost lies above average variable cost and pulls the average up. Marginal cost is not below average variable cost at every output level, since it must eventually rise above it as output increases. This intersection point also marks the short-run shutdown point for a competitive firm facing a price below average variable cost.
  66. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Shut down production immediately, because losses would exceed fixed costs otherwise: when price falls below average variable cost, the firm cannot even cover its variable costs on each unit produced, so continuing to operate would generate losses larger than simply shutting down and losing only fixed costs. The shutdown rule states that a firm should stop producing whenever price is below average variable cost in the short run, regardless of whether price covers average total cost. Continuing to produce at price equals marginal cost, or merely reducing output slightly, would still leave the firm losing more than its fixed costs alone. This decision is specific to the short run, since fixed costs must still be paid whether or not the firm produces.
  67. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): $20 per unit: average total cost equals total cost divided by output, and total cost is the sum of total fixed cost and total variable cost, so ($10,000 + $30,000) ÷ 2,000 = $40,000 ÷ 2,000 = $20 per unit. A figure of $15 or $25 per unit would require different total cost or output values than those provided in the scenario. This average total cost figure combines both the $5 per unit average fixed cost and the $15 per unit average variable cost implied by the given totals. Average total cost is the relevant benchmark for comparing against price to determine whether the firm earns a profit in the long run.
  68. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Marginal cost rises as marginal product of labor falls, since fewer additional units are produced per additional worker: diminishing marginal returns mean each additional worker adds less extra output than the previous worker, so the labor cost required to produce one more unit of output rises as marginal product falls. This inverse relationship between marginal product and marginal cost is a fundamental link between the production function and the cost structure of the firm. Marginal cost cannot fall as marginal product falls, since a smaller output gain per worker requires more labor spending per additional unit produced. Marginal cost is directly tied to marginal product in the short run and is not independent of it.
  69. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Economic profit is driven toward zero as entry increases competition and shifts demand for the firm inward: positive short-run economic profit attracts new entrants offering similar differentiated products, which draws customers away from the existing firm and shifts its individual demand curve leftward. Entry continues until the firm's demand curve becomes tangent to its average total cost curve, at which point economic profit equals zero, mirroring the long-run outcome in perfect competition despite ongoing product differentiation. Product differentiation alone does not create a durable barrier strong enough to sustain positive economic profit indefinitely under monopolistic competition. This firm also does not become a natural monopoly, since monopolistic competition by definition involves many competing firms rather than a single dominant supplier.
  70. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Oligopoly, characterized by a small number of interdependent firms and significant barriers to entry: the scenario describes only four firms dominating the market, each explicitly aware that its own pricing and output choices affect its rivals' profits, which is the hallmark of strategic interdependence found in oligopoly. Perfect competition instead assumes many firms that act as price takers with no meaningful strategic interaction between them, which contradicts the description given. Monopolistic competition involves many firms and relatively easy entry, neither of which matches a market dominated by just four large interdependent firms. The presence of significant barriers to entry further supports classifying this market as an oligopoly rather than a more competitive structure.
  71. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The kinked demand curve model, which helps explain price rigidity among interdependent oligopolists: this model assumes rivals match price cuts to avoid losing market share but ignore price increases, creating a demand curve that is more elastic above the current price and less elastic below it, with a corresponding discontinuity in marginal revenue. This discontinuity means a range of marginal cost changes can occur without inducing the firm to change its price, explaining observed price stability in some oligopolistic industries. The Cournot model instead focuses on simultaneous quantity decisions rather than price-matching behavior, and the Bertrand model assumes firms compete on price to the point where price converges toward marginal cost. Neither of those alternative models produces the specific kinked demand curve and price rigidity described in the scenario.
  72. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The firm must be able to prevent resale between groups and identify each group's elasticity: third-degree price discrimination is only profitable if the monopolist can sort consumers into groups with different price elasticities of demand and prevent the lower-priced group, such as students, from reselling to the higher-priced group. Without this ability to segment the market and block arbitrage, the price differential would collapse as consumers in the lower-priced group resold to those facing the higher price. Identical elasticities across groups would eliminate any incentive to price differently, since uniform pricing would already be optimal. This strategy also requires some degree of market power, which is inconsistent with operating in a perfectly competitive market for a homogeneous commodity.
  73. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Output rises toward the competitive level while consumer surplus is captured entirely by the firm as producer surplus: because the perfectly price-discriminating monopolist charges each unit at the buyer's exact willingness to pay, it has an incentive to keep producing and selling additional units as long as that willingness to pay exceeds marginal cost, pushing output up to the competitive, efficient level. Unlike single-price monopoly, no deadweight loss remains, but all of the surplus that would have gone to consumers is instead extracted by the firm. Output does not fall below the single-price monopoly level, since price discrimination removes the incentive to restrict output that exists under uniform pricing. Output and consumer surplus are therefore not identical to the single-price monopoly outcome, since both the quantity produced and the distribution of surplus change substantially.
  74. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Economies of scale, where per-unit costs fall continuously as output expands: when long-run average cost declines across the entire range relevant to market demand, a single large firm can produce the industry's total output at a lower average cost than multiple smaller firms could achieve, which is the classic condition giving rise to a natural monopoly. Diseconomies of scale describe the opposite pattern, where per-unit costs rise as output expands, which would instead favor smaller-scale production by multiple firms. Constant returns to scale imply average cost stays flat as output changes, offering no particular cost advantage to a single large producer over several smaller ones. Industries such as utilities with substantial fixed infrastructure costs often exhibit the economies-of-scale pattern described in this scenario.
  75. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): 10 units: setting price equal to marginal cost gives 30 = 10 + 2Q, so 2Q = 20 and Q = 10. Producing only 5 units would leave marginal cost below the market price, meaning the firm is forgoing additional profitable units it could still produce. Producing 15 units would push marginal cost above the market price, causing the firm to lose money on those extra units beyond the profit-maximizing quantity. Setting price equal to marginal cost is the correct profit-maximizing rule for a perfectly competitive firm because it is a price taker facing a horizontal, perfectly elastic demand curve at the market price.
  76. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): To sell an additional unit, the monopolist must lower price on all units sold, not just the marginal one: because the monopolist faces the entire downward-sloping market demand curve, expanding output requires lowering price on every unit sold, not merely on the newest unit, so the revenue gained from the extra unit is partially offset by the revenue lost on all previously sold units at the higher price. This is why marginal revenue falls below price at every output level for a monopolist, unlike a competitive firm that can sell additional units at the same market price without affecting revenue on prior units. The monopolist does not face a perfectly elastic demand curve, since that characteristic belongs to price-taking competitive firms rather than firms with market power. Marginal revenue is always less than or equal to price for a downward-sloping demand curve, never greater than price, which rules out the third option.
  77. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Marginal cost equals average total cost exactly at the minimum point of the average total cost curve: this is a standard result of cost-curve geometry, where the marginal cost curve intersects the average total cost curve precisely where average total cost reaches its lowest value. Below that output level, marginal cost lies beneath average total cost and pulls the average down as output rises; above it, marginal cost lies above average total cost and pulls the average up. Marginal cost cannot lie entirely above or entirely below average total cost at every output level, since it must cross the average curve from below to above as output expands through the minimum point. This relationship holds mathematically whenever a marginal value pulls an average value toward itself as quantity changes.
  78. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A long-run supply response that worsens the housing shortage created by the binding price ceiling: while a price ceiling directly reduces the quantity supplied at the controlled rent, landlords also respond over a longer horizon by cutting maintenance, converting units to condominiums, or exiting the rental market entirely, further shrinking the effective supply of rental housing available at the controlled price. This long-run supply response tends to make the shortage more severe over time than the simple short-run analysis alone would predict. Producer surplus for landlords generally falls under a binding rent ceiling rather than rising, since the controlled price sits below what landlords could otherwise charge. Demand for rental housing is not eliminated by this policy and the shortage persists or worsens rather than disappearing.
  79. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A surplus develops because quantity supplied exceeds quantity demanded at the floor price: a price floor set above the equilibrium price encourages producers to supply more at the higher legally mandated price while discouraging some buyers from purchasing at that higher price, creating an excess of quantity supplied over quantity demanded. This surplus is the mirror image of the shortage that results from a binding price ceiling set below equilibrium. Governments supporting agricultural price floors often must purchase or store the resulting surplus output to prevent the price from collapsing back toward equilibrium. The market cannot clear at the floor price precisely because the floor prevents price from falling to the level that would equate quantity supplied and quantity demanded.
  80. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Continue producing in the short run because price exceeds average variable cost: even though price ($16) is below average total cost ($18), meaning the firm incurs an economic loss, price still exceeds average variable cost ($14), so continuing to produce allows the firm to cover all variable costs plus a portion of its fixed costs. Shutting down immediately would forfeit the entire contribution toward fixed costs and result in a larger loss equal to total fixed cost. Increasing output until price equals average total cost is not generally achievable through the firm's own output decision alone, since price is set by the market for a price-taking competitive firm. This distinction between the shutdown rule, based on average variable cost, and the exit decision, based on average total cost, is central to short-run versus long-run firm behavior.
  81. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The Cournot quantity-competition model, where firms simultaneously choose output levels: the Cournot model assumes duopolists simultaneously and independently choose profit-maximizing output quantities, each taking the rival's output as fixed, and equilibrium occurs where each firm's chosen quantity is a best response to the other's, forming a stable Nash equilibrium in quantities. The Bertrand model instead assumes firms compete by simultaneously setting price rather than quantity, which typically drives price down toward marginal cost under homogeneous goods. The Stackelberg model differs because one firm moves first as a leader and commits to an output level before the follower responds, rather than both firms choosing simultaneously. The simultaneous, quantity-based, mutual best-response structure described in the scenario is specifically characteristic of the Cournot framework.
  82. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A higher output and greater profit than it would earn under simultaneous Cournot competition: by committing to an output level first, the Stackelberg leader can credibly signal its production plan, forcing the follower to accept a smaller residual market and respond with lower output, which allows the leader to capture a higher output share and greater profit than under simultaneous Cournot competition. This first-mover advantage arises because the leader's commitment changes the follower's best-response calculation in the leader's favor. A lower output and lower profit would describe the follower's outcome relative to Cournot, not the leader's. The leader's output and profit are not identical to the Cournot outcome precisely because the sequential structure changes the strategic interaction between the two firms.
  83. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The incentive to cheat, where each member can profit by exceeding its quota while others comply: because cartel members restrict output to raise the market price above the competitive level, each individual firm can earn additional profit by secretly producing beyond its assigned quota and selling at the elevated price, as long as other members continue to honor their own restrictions. This incentive to cheat is a central reason why cartel agreements are inherently unstable without effective monitoring and enforcement mechanisms. Economies of scope and first-mover advantage describe entirely different cost and strategic concepts unrelated to the instability of output-restriction agreements among cartel members. The larger the number of cartel members, the harder it typically becomes to detect and punish cheating, further weakening cartel discipline.
  84. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Marginal cost is at its minimum, since each additional unit of output requires the least additional labor cost: marginal cost and marginal product of labor are inversely related, so the output level at which marginal product of labor reaches its maximum corresponds exactly to the output level at which marginal cost reaches its minimum. At this point, each additional worker is contributing the greatest additional output, meaning the labor cost of producing one more unit is at its lowest. Marginal cost would be at its maximum where marginal product is at its minimum, not its maximum, so the first option describes the opposite relationship. Marginal cost is directly derived from the production function through marginal product and is therefore never constant or unrelated to changes in labor productivity.
  85. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Tangent to the average total cost curve, resulting in zero economic profit but excess capacity relative to the minimum-cost output: in long-run monopolistic competition, entry and exit continue until each firm's downward-sloping demand curve is tangent to its average total cost curve, which drives economic profit to exactly zero at that tangency point. Because the demand curve is downward sloping rather than horizontal, this tangency occurs at an output level below the point where average total cost is minimized, producing the excess capacity commonly associated with monopolistic competition. The demand curve cannot lie below average total cost everywhere in long-run equilibrium, since firms earning negative economic profit would exit until the tangency condition is restored. The demand curve intersecting marginal cost at the minimum of the average total cost curve describes the perfectly competitive long-run outcome, not the monopolistically competitive one.
  86. The correct answer is A (Business purchases of new equipment and unsold inventory changes held by firms during the period): The investment (I) component of GDP under the expenditure approach captures gross private domestic investment, which includes business fixed capital such as new machinery and structures plus the change in business inventories over the period. Household purchases of financial securities like bonds and stocks are excluded because they represent transfers of existing financial claims rather than newly produced goods or services. Government transfer payments are excluded from GDP entirely since no corresponding production of a good or service occurs in exchange for the payment.

The correct answer is B (Approximately $538.46 billion, after removing the effect of the 4% price increase): Real GDP is calculated as nominal GDP divided by the GDP deflator expressed as a decimal, so $560 billion ÷ 1.04 equals approximately $538.46 billion. This adjustment strips out the portion of nominal GDP growth attributable purely to rising prices, isolating the change in actual physical output. Simply reporting the unadjusted nominal figure ignores inflation entirely, and doubling the deflator adjustment has no basis in the calculation.

The correct answer is C (120, calculated by dividing nominal GDP by real GDP and multiplying by 100): The GDP deflator formula is (nominal GDP ÷ real GDP) × 100, so $720 billion ÷ $600 billion × 100 equals 120. A deflator value above 100 indicates that the overall price level has risen relative to the base year in which real GDP is valued. Reversing the ratio, as in option B, or omitting the multiplication by 100, as in option A, produces an incorrect index value.
  89. The correct answer is B (A common informal signal is two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP, though official dating relies on broader indicators): Many commentators use two consecutive quarters of negative real GDP growth as a convenient rule of thumb for identifying a recession, even though this shorthand is not the formal definition used by official business cycle dating authorities. Official recession dating typically considers a broader set of indicators, including employment, industrial production, and real income, evaluated over time rather than a single mechanical rule. Unemployment thresholds and central bank money supply announcements are not the basis for recession classification.
  90. The correct answer is A (The average number of weekly hours worked in manufacturing, since employers adjust hours before hiring or layoffs): Average weekly manufacturing hours is a classic leading indicator because employers typically adjust existing employees' hours up or down before committing to costly hiring or layoff decisions, so it signals turning points ahead of the broader cycle. The unemployment rate is a lagging indicator, since firms usually wait to confirm a downturn has already taken hold before reducing headcount. Outstanding commercial and industrial loans also tend to lag the cycle, since businesses often keep borrowing for a period after a peak has already occurred.
  91. The correct answer is C (Frictional unemployment, arising from the normal time needed to match workers with suitable job openings): Frictional unemployment describes the short-term, voluntary search process that occurs even in a healthy labor market as workers evaluate offers and seek the best match for their skills. This differs from structural unemployment, which reflects a genuine mismatch between a worker's skills and available jobs, and from cyclical unemployment, which reflects an economy-wide shortfall in aggregate demand. The graduate's situation involves normal search behavior rather than either a skills mismatch or a broader downturn.
  92. The correct answer is A (Structural unemployment, resulting from a persistent mismatch between workers' skills and the skills employers require): Structural unemployment arises when technological change, automation, or shifting industry demand permanently eliminates the need for certain skill sets, leaving affected workers unable to find suitable jobs even after extended searching. This differs from frictional unemployment, which involves only the brief, voluntary transition time between jobs for workers whose skills remain in demand. It also differs from cyclical unemployment, which reflects a temporary, economy-wide decline in aggregate demand rather than a permanent skills mismatch.
  93. The correct answer is B (Frictional and structural unemployment combined, since cyclical unemployment is assumed to be zero at this rate): The NAIRU represents the unemployment rate consistent with stable inflation, which corresponds to the economy operating at full employment where only frictional and structural unemployment persist. At this rate, cyclical unemployment is by definition zero, since the economy is neither overheating nor operating below its potential. Describing the NAIRU as reflecting only cyclical or only structural unemployment omits one of the two components that genuinely make it up.
  94. The correct answer is C (The GDP deflator reflects prices of all domestically produced goods and services, while the CPI reflects a fixed representative consumer basket): The GDP deflator is a broad measure covering the prices of everything produced domestically, including capital goods and government purchases, and its composition changes each period to match current output. The CPI, by contrast, tracks a fixed basket of goods and services typically purchased by urban consumers, including some imported goods, and is updated only periodically rather than every period. Describing the CPI as having a changing basket while the deflator has a permanently fixed one reverses the actual construction of the two indexes.
  95. The correct answer is A (Demand-pull inflation, driven by aggregate demand outpacing the economy's ability to increase real output): Demand-pull inflation occurs when total spending in the economy exceeds the economy's short-run capacity to produce additional goods and services, forcing the general price level upward as buyers compete for limited output. This differs from cost-push inflation, which originates from the supply side through rising production costs rather than excess demand. The scenario described involves excess demand pressure across markets rather than any mention of rising input costs, which rules out a cost-push explanation.
  96. The correct answer is B (Cost-push inflation, caused by rising input costs that shift the short-run aggregate supply curve leftward): Cost-push inflation originates on the supply side when rising costs for key inputs, such as oil, raise production expenses across the economy and shift short-run aggregate supply leftward, raising prices even without any increase in demand. This is distinct from demand-pull inflation, which is driven by excess spending rather than rising costs, and the scenario explicitly states that overall demand remains largely unchanged. Attributing the inflation solely to money supply growth ignores the clearly stated supply-side cost shock driving the price increase.
  97. The correct answer is C (Approximately 4%, obtained by subtracting expected inflation from the nominal interest rate): The approximate Fisher relation states that the real interest rate equals the nominal interest rate minus the expected inflation rate, so 7% minus 3% equals approximately 4%. This approximation is commonly used in practice because the more precise formula involves a small compounding term that has little practical effect at typical interest rate levels. Adding the two rates together or using only the inflation figure alone does not correspond to any valid application of the Fisher relation.
  98. The correct answer is A (Purchasing government securities in the open market, which injects reserves directly into the banking system): Open market purchases are the central bank's most routinely used tool for expanding the money supply, since buying government securities from banks directly increases the reserves available for lending, which in turn puts downward pressure on short-term interest rates. Raising the reserve requirement would instead reduce banks' lending capacity and contract the money supply, the opposite of the stated goal. Raising the discount rate would similarly discourage bank borrowing from the central bank, working against the objective of expanding the money supply.
  99. The correct answer is C (The money multiplier increases, allowing banks to extend more loans from each dollar of deposits received): Lowering the required reserve ratio directly raises the money multiplier, since banks must hold back a smaller fraction of each deposit as required reserves and can lend out a larger proportion, expanding the money supply through repeated rounds of lending. This is the opposite of option A, which describes what would happen if the reserve requirement were raised rather than lowered. Option B is also incorrect because a lower requirement increases banks' capacity to lend rather than forcing them to hold more reserves in excess of what is required.
  100. The correct answer is B (An automatic stabilizer, since these changes occur mechanically as economic conditions shift without new legislative action): Automatic stabilizers, such as progressive income taxes and unemployment insurance programs, are built into fiscal policy so that tax revenue and transfer spending adjust automatically as economic conditions change, without requiring lawmakers to pass new legislation. This differs from discretionary fiscal policy, which requires deliberate legislative action to change tax rates or spending levels in response to economic conditions. Describing these automatic changes as contractionary policy is also incorrect, since they act to cushion the downturn rather than deliberately restrain aggregate demand.
  101. The correct answer is A (Approximately $160 billion, using a spending multiplier of 4 derived from the given MPC of 0.75): The simple spending multiplier equals 1 divided by (1 minus the MPC), so with an MPC of 0.75 the multiplier is 1 ÷ 0.25, which equals 4. Multiplying the $40 billion increase in government spending by this multiplier of 4 yields a total predicted increase in equilibrium GDP of approximately $160 billion. Assuming a multiplier of exactly one ignores the repeated rounds of induced consumption spending that the multiplier model is specifically designed to capture.
  102. The correct answer is C (A significant unexpected increase in consumer confidence that leads households to increase spending at every price level): A shift in consumer confidence that changes desired spending independent of the price level causes the entire aggregate demand curve to move, rather than producing a movement along a fixed curve. Options A and B both describe changes in the domestic price level itself, which by definition produce movements along the existing aggregate demand curve through the wealth effect and the international trade effect rather than shifting the curve. Only a change in an underlying determinant of spending, such as confidence, genuinely shifts the curve at every price level.
  103. The correct answer is B (In the long run, wages and input prices fully adjust to price-level changes, so output returns to its potential level): The long-run aggregate supply curve is vertical because, given enough time, wages and other input prices adjust fully to changes in the overall price level, leaving real output determined by the economy's productive capacity rather than by the price level itself. This full adjustment is what distinguishes the long run from the short run, where sticky wages and prices allow output to temporarily deviate from potential. Claiming that supply plays no role or that firms permanently fix prices misstates the actual mechanism behind the vertical long-run curve.
  104. The correct answer is A (An inverse relationship between the unemployment rate and the inflation rate in the short run): The short-run Phillips curve traces an observed historical tradeoff in which lower unemployment tends to be associated with higher inflation, and higher unemployment tends to be associated with lower inflation, over a given period. This relationship is distinct from any direct link between GDP growth and money supply growth, or between exchange rates and budget deficits, neither of which the Phillips curve is constructed to depict. The curve's central insight concerns the short-run tension between the two labor-market and price-level outcomes named in the correct option.
  105. The correct answer is B (The price level P rises proportionally with the increase in the money supply, since V and Y stay unchanged): In the equation of exchange, M × V = P × Y, holding velocity V and real output Y constant means that any increase in the money supply M must be matched by a proportional increase in the price level P to keep the equation balanced. This is the core prediction of the quantity theory of money in its classical form, linking sustained money growth directly to inflation. Claiming the price level falls or that velocity absorbs the increase contradicts the stated assumption that both V and Y remain fixed.
  106. The correct answer is C (Savings deposits, small-denomination time deposits, and retail money market mutual fund balances): M2 is a broader measure of the money supply that includes everything in M1 plus somewhat less liquid assets such as savings deposits, small time deposits, and retail money market funds. Currency in circulation and demand or checkable deposits, described in options A and B, are both already part of the narrower M1 measure rather than being unique to M2. The distinguishing feature of M2 is the inclusion of these additional, slightly less liquid savings-type instruments beyond what M1 captures.
  107. The correct answer is A (The central bank actively buys or sells its own currency and foreign reserves to keep the exchange rate at the target): Maintaining a fixed exchange rate requires the central bank to intervene directly in the foreign exchange market, buying or selling its own currency against foreign reserves whenever market pressures push the rate away from the target level. This intervention is the defining mechanism of a fixed regime and stands in direct contrast to a floating regime, where the rate is left to adjust freely to market forces, as described in option B. Abandoning interest rate policy entirely, as in option C, is not how fixed exchange rate commitments are typically implemented.
  108. The correct answer is B (PPP predicts that the exchange rate equalizes the price of an identical basket of goods across the two countries): Absolute purchasing power parity holds that the exchange rate should adjust so that the same basket of goods costs the same amount whether priced in either country's currency, once converted to a common currency. This concept relates directly to relative price levels between countries rather than to interest rate differentials, which is instead the basis of interest rate parity relationships. PPP also does not imply that the exchange rate becomes permanently fixed, since it continues to adjust as relative price levels change over time.
  109. The correct answer is C (Country A's currency should depreciate by approximately 4%, moving the rate to about 10.4 units per unit of B): Relative PPP predicts that the percentage change in the exchange rate approximately equals the inflation differential between the two countries, here 6% minus 2%, or approximately 4%. Because Country A has the higher inflation rate, its currency is expected to depreciate against Country B's currency, meaning more units of A's currency are needed to buy one unit of B's currency, moving the rate from 10 to approximately 10.4. Predicting appreciation of Country A's currency, as in option A, reverses the direction implied by its higher relative inflation rate.
  110. The correct answer is A (Approximately $1.2233 per euro, reflecting the higher U.S. interest rate relative to the eurozone rate): Covered interest rate parity states that the forward rate equals the spot rate multiplied by the ratio of one plus the domestic interest rate to one plus the foreign interest rate, so $1.20 × (1.05 ÷ 1.03) equals approximately $1.2233 per euro. Because the U.S. interest rate exceeds the eurozone rate, the dollar is expected to trade at a forward discount, meaning more dollars are needed per euro in the forward market than in the spot market. Assuming the forward rate equals the spot rate, as in option C, ignores the interest rate differential that covered interest rate parity is specifically designed to capture.
  111. The correct answer is C (UIP asserts that the currency with the higher interest rate is expected to depreciate against the lower-interest currency): Uncovered interest rate parity predicts that the expected change in the spot exchange rate offsets the interest rate differential between two countries, so the higher-yielding currency is expected to depreciate by approximately the amount of the interest rate advantage, leaving expected returns equalized across currencies. This differs from covered interest rate parity, which uses a contractually fixed forward rate to lock in a riskless relationship rather than relying on an uncertain expectation. Claiming interest rate differentials have no relationship to expected exchange rate movements directly contradicts the entire premise of the UIP relationship.
  112. The correct answer is B (The net value of a country's exported manufactured goods minus its imported manufactured goods over the period): The current account records the trade in goods and services, along with income flows and current transfers, so the net value of exports minus imports of manufactured goods is a textbook current account transaction. Purchases of foreign equity shares and a foreign central bank's purchase of government securities both involve the transfer of financial asset ownership across borders, placing them in the financial account rather than the current account. Only transactions involving the actual flow of goods, services, income, or transfers belong in the current account.
  113. The correct answer is A (A foreign firm's direct investment to build and operate a new manufacturing plant within the domestic economy): Foreign direct investment involves a cross-border acquisition or creation of a productive asset and represents a change in ownership of a financial or physical asset, which is precisely what the financial account is designed to record. Payments received by residents working abroad are classified as primary income and belong in the current account rather than the financial account. Imported consumer electronics purchased from an overseas manufacturer are a straightforward goods transaction recorded in the current account, not the financial account.
  114. The correct answer is B (Country X has a lower opportunity cost in wheat, so both countries gain if X specializes in wheat and Y in cloth): Country X's opportunity cost of one unit of wheat is 0.5 units of cloth forgone, while Country Y's opportunity cost of one unit of wheat is 1 unit of cloth forgone, meaning X has the comparative advantage in wheat despite also having an absolute advantage in both goods. Comparative advantage theory shows that mutually beneficial trade is still possible even when one country is absolutely more productive in everything, as long as opportunity costs differ between the two goods. Country Y instead holds the comparative advantage in cloth, since its opportunity cost of producing cloth is lower than Country X's.
  115. The correct answer is C (Absolute advantage compares raw productivity levels, while comparative advantage compares relative opportunity costs between goods): Absolute advantage simply identifies which country can produce more output from a given quantity of resources, without considering what must be given up to produce that output. Comparative advantage instead compares the opportunity cost of producing one good in terms of the other good forgone, which is the relevant basis for determining which country should specialize in which good to maximize mutual gains from trade. The two concepts are not identical and can point to different producers, since a country can hold an absolute advantage in a good without holding the comparative advantage in that same good.
  116. The correct answer is A (The domestic price rises, imported quantity falls, and domestic producers expand output at the now higher price): A tariff raises the effective price of the imported good in the domestic market, which reduces the quantity of imports demanded while simultaneously making domestic production more attractive at the higher price, leading domestic producers to expand output. This differs from option B, which describes the opposite effect and does not match how a tax on imports actually functions. Claiming the tariff has no measurable effect, as in option C, ignores the basic supply and demand mechanics through which an import tax raises domestic prices.
  117. The correct answer is C (A binding quota fixes the import quantity directly, while a tariff raises price and lets quantity adjust through the market): A quota directly restricts the physical quantity of imports allowed into the country, with the resulting domestic price determined by how the market responds to that fixed quantity, whereas a tariff instead sets a price wedge through taxation and allows the quantity of imports to adjust in response. Both a quota and a tariff typically raise the domestic price of the good, so claiming a quota has no price effect, as in option A, is incorrect. A tariff generates government revenue through the tax collected on imports, while a quota does not generate revenue unless the import licenses are explicitly auctioned, making option B's claim inaccurate.
  118. The correct answer is B (Net exports fall, because domestic goods become relatively more expensive for foreign buyers to purchase): When a currency appreciates, goods priced in that currency become more expensive for foreign buyers converting from their own currency, which tends to reduce export demand while making imports relatively cheaper for domestic consumers, together pushing net exports lower. This is the opposite of option A, which incorrectly describes the effect of a depreciation rather than an appreciation. Exchange rate movements are a well-established determinant of trade flows, so claiming no effect at all, as in option C, misstates the basic relationship between currency values and net exports.
  119. The correct answer is A (Growth in capital stock, labor force quality, and total factor productivity, particularly technological improvement): Sustainable long-run economic growth in a standard production function framework is driven by increases in the capital stock, improvements in the quality and skills of the labor force, and gains in total factor productivity, which largely reflects technological progress. Growth driven purely by short-run aggregate demand increases financed through government borrowing may raise output temporarily but does not expand the economy's underlying productive capacity over the long run. A rising price level similarly reflects inflation rather than genuine growth in real productive capacity, so it cannot serve as a durable source of long-run growth.
  120. This is the opposite of an overheating economy, which occurs when actual output exceeds potential output and generates upward pressure on inflation instead. Claiming actual and potential GDP are always identical, as in option C, contradicts the very premise of an output gap, which by definition describes a divergence between the two measures.

Under IFRS 15, a performance obligation satisfied over time exists when the customer controls the asset as it is created and the seller has an enforceable right to payment for work performed to date. This contrasts with point-in-time recognition, which applies when control transfers only at delivery. Recognizing revenue over time better matches revenue with the construction progress actually completed each period.

Under LIFO, the most recently purchased, higher-cost inventory units are assumed sold first, so cost of goods sold rises when prices are rising steadily.   134. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Antidilutive, and must be excluded from the diluted EPS calculation. A security is dilutive only if its inclusion in the calculation reduces EPS below the basic figure, reflecting a true dilution of ownership and earnings per share. Since including these options would raise EPS rather than lower it, they are classified as antidilutive and must be excluded from diluted EPS. Diluted EPS is designed to present the most conservative, lowest reasonable EPS figure that could result from potentially issuable shares.

  160. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): 6.0 times, calculated as net credit sales divided by average accounts receivable. Receivables turnover divides net credit sales of $9,000,000 by average accounts receivable of $1,500,000, giving 6.0 times. This ratio indicates how many times during the year the company collected its average outstanding receivables balance, a measure of collection efficiency. Inverting the calculation, as in dividing receivables by sales, would produce a fraction with a different and less commonly used interpretation.
  161. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): 36.5 days, calculated as 365 divided by the receivables turnover ratio. Days sales outstanding converts receivables turnover into a time-based measure by dividing 365 days by the turnover ratio of 10.0, producing 36.5 days. This indicates the company takes an average of 36.5 days to collect payment after a credit sale is made. Treating the turnover ratio itself as the number of days, without performing the conversion, would significantly understate the actual collection period.
  162. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): 2.0 times, calculated as total revenue divided by average total assets. Total asset turnover divides total revenue of $18,000,000 by average total assets of $9,000,000, giving 2.0 times. This ratio measures how efficiently the company generates sales revenue from its overall asset base, with a higher figure generally indicating more effective asset utilization. Inverting the calculation, as in dividing assets by revenue, would instead measure how many dollars of assets are required per dollar of revenue.

The five-factor DuPont model splits the single net profit margin component of the three-factor model into tax burden, interest burden, and EBIT margin, allowing an analyst to separately assess how taxes, financing costs, and core operating profitability each contribute to overall net profit margin. This finer decomposition helps identify whether a change in ROE stems from operations, financing decisions, or tax effects. Inventory and receivables efficiency are instead captured through separate activity ratios, not through this particular decomposition.

The financial leverage ratio equals total assets of $5,000,000 divided by total equity of $2,000,000, or 2.5. Applying the three-factor DuPont formula, return on equity equals 6% times 2.0 times 2.5, which equals 30.0%. Omitting the financial leverage component, or using an incorrect leverage figure such as 1.25, would understate the true return generated for equity holders.

Of the 250 units sold, 100 units come from beginning inventory at $10 = $1,000, and 150 units come from the purchase at $12 = $1,800. Total cost of goods sold is $1,000 + $1,800 = $2,800, leaving the remaining 50 units at $12 in ending inventory.

That leaves 50 units of the original $10 beginning inventory unsold. Ending inventory value is therefore 50 × $10 = $500.

Cost of goods sold for 250 units is 250 × $11.333 ≈ $2,833. This falls between the FIFO and LIFO cost of goods sold results, as expected.

Here that is ($50,000 minus $5,000) ÷ 9 years = $45,000 ÷ 9 = $5,000 per year. This annual amount stays constant throughout the asset's useful life under the straight-line method.
  169. The correct answer is C ($9,600, calculated as 40% of the remaining book value): The double-declining balance rate is 2 ÷ 5 years = 40%. Year one depreciation is $40,000 × 40% = $16,000, leaving a book value of $24,000. Year two depreciation is then $24,000 × 40% = $9,600, since the declining balance method applies the rate to remaining book value each year.

  171. Here that is $500,000 minus $420,000 = $80,000. The asset's carrying amount is then written down to $420,000.

The correct answer is A (No impairment loss is recognized because undiscounted cash flows exceed carrying amount): Under US GAAP's two-step model, impairment is only recognized if the asset fails a recoverability test comparing carrying amount to undiscounted future cash flows. Since $520,000 of undiscounted cash flows exceeds the $500,000 carrying amount, the asset passes the recoverability test. No impairment loss is measured or recognized, even though fair value is lower than carrying amount.
  173. The deferred tax liability equals the temporary difference multiplied by the tax rate, so $50,000 × 30% = $15,000. This liability represents taxes that will become payable in future periods.

The deferred tax asset equals the temporary difference multiplied by the tax rate, so $20,000 × 25% = $5,000. This asset represents future tax savings once the warranty claims are actually paid and deductible.
  175. This gives $200,000 + $30,000 minus $15,000 + $10,000 + $8,000 = $233,000.
  176. A decrease in accounts receivable means more cash was collected than revenue recognized, so it is added to revenue. Cash collections equal $500,000 plus $20,000 = $520,000.
  177.
