Cash paid to suppliers then equals purchases minus the increase in accounts payable, since a higher payable balance means less cash was actually paid out. That gives $325,000 minus $10,000 = $315,000.
  178. The correct answer is C (Trade receivables, because they are expected to convert to cash within one year): Current assets are those expected to be converted to cash, sold, or consumed within one year or one operating cycle. Trade receivables meet this definition since customers are expected to pay within the normal collection period. Goodwill and long-term equity investments are noncurrent assets because they are not expected to convert to cash within the short term.
  179. The correct answer is A (Financing activities, since it relates to transactions with the company's owners): Under US GAAP, dividends paid to shareholders are always classified as a financing activity because they represent a distribution to the company's owners rather than an operating or investing transaction. This treatment differs from IFRS, where companies have some flexibility to classify dividends paid as either an operating or a financing activity. Consistent classification under US GAAP aids comparability across companies.
  180. The correct answer is A (Operating activities or financing activities, depending on management's chosen policy): Under IFRS, interest paid may be classified as either an operating activity, consistent with its role in determining net income, or a financing activity, consistent with it being a cost of obtaining financing. This flexibility does not exist under US GAAP, where interest paid is always classified as an operating activity. Analysts must check a company's chosen policy when comparing IFRS-based cash flow statements.
  181. The correct answer is B ($450,000, equal to total assets minus total liabilities): The accounting equation states that total assets equal total liabilities plus total shareholders' equity. Rearranging this equation, equity equals total assets minus total liabilities, so $800,000 minus $350,000 = $450,000. This relationship must hold true on every balance sheet.
  182. The correct answer is C ($70,000, equal to current assets minus current liabilities): Working capital is calculated as current assets minus current liabilities, and it measures a company's short-term liquidity position. Here that is $220,000 minus $150,000 = $70,000. Positive working capital generally indicates a company can cover its near-term obligations from its current resources.
  183. The correct answer is B (1.47, indicating current assets exceed current liabilities by that multiple): The current ratio is calculated as current assets divided by current liabilities, so $220,000 ÷ $150,000 ≈ 1.4667, which rounds to 1.47. A current ratio above 1.0 indicates that current assets exceed current liabilities, suggesting adequate short-term liquidity. This ratio does not distinguish between more and less liquid current assets, unlike the quick ratio.
  184. The correct answer is A (1.07, calculated using current assets excluding inventory divided by current liabilities): The quick ratio excludes inventory, the least liquid current asset, from the numerator. Quick assets equal $220,000 minus $60,000 of inventory = $160,000, and the quick ratio equals $160,000 ÷ $150,000 ≈ 1.0667, which rounds to 1.07. This ratio is generally considered a more conservative liquidity measure than the current ratio.
  185. The correct answer is C (5.0, calculated as cost of goods sold divided by average inventory): Inventory turnover equals cost of goods sold divided by average inventory, so $600,000 ÷ $120,000 = 5.0. This ratio indicates how many times a company's average inventory is sold and replaced during the year. A higher turnover ratio generally suggests more efficient inventory management, though it should be compared against industry norms.
  186. The correct answer is C (73 days, calculated as 365 divided by the turnover ratio): Days of inventory on hand equals 365 divided by the inventory turnover ratio, so 365 ÷ 5.0 = 73 days. This measure converts the turnover ratio into an average holding period, showing roughly how many days inventory sits before being sold. Shorter holding periods generally indicate more efficient inventory management, all else equal.
  187. The correct answer is A (FIFO, because it expenses the oldest, lower-cost inventory units first): When prices are rising, FIFO matches the oldest, cheaper costs against current revenue, producing a lower cost of goods sold figure. This lower expense flows directly into a higher reported gross profit and net income compared to LIFO or weighted average. LIFO instead expenses the newest, higher-cost units first, producing the opposite effect during rising prices.
  188. The correct answer is B (LIFO, because it expenses the newest, lower-cost inventory units first): When prices are declining, LIFO matches the newest, cheaper costs against current revenue, producing a lower cost of goods sold figure than FIFO. This lower expense results in higher reported net income compared to FIFO, which under declining prices would expense the older, more expensive units first. The relative advantage of each method therefore reverses depending on the price trend.
  189. The correct answer is B (LIFO, since it can understate inventory values during inflationary periods): International Financial Reporting Standards prohibit the use of LIFO, partly because it can understate ending inventory relative to current replacement cost during periods of rising prices. US GAAP continues to permit LIFO, creating a notable difference between the two accounting frameworks. Analysts comparing companies under different frameworks must adjust for this difference when it applies.
  190. The correct answer is C (Channel stuffing, an aggressive revenue recognition practice that inflates sales): Channel stuffing occurs when a company ships more inventory to distributors than they can realistically sell, recording the shipments as revenue despite an expectation of later returns. This practice artificially inflates current period sales and earnings while borrowing from future period demand. It is considered an aggressive and often misleading revenue recognition practice rather than a legitimate business strategy.
  191. The correct answer is A (A potential red flag suggesting the company relies on aggressive accrual accounting): Net income that consistently exceeds cash flow from operations over multiple years can indicate that reported earnings rely heavily on accruals rather than actual cash generation. This divergence is a commonly cited red flag for lower earnings quality and warrants closer analysis of revenue recognition and expense timing policies. Sustainable, high-quality earnings are generally expected to be reasonably supported by operating cash flow over time.
  192. The correct answer is B (Big bath accounting, a practice used to shift losses into one period): Big bath accounting involves recognizing unusually large charges, such as impairments or restructuring costs, all at once during an already weak year. This concentrates losses into a single period, making subsequent periods appear relatively stronger by comparison even without genuine operational improvement. It is considered a form of earnings management that distorts the trend of reported profitability over time.
  193. The correct answer is A (The allowance for doubtful accounts, since it relies on subjective estimates): The allowance for doubtful accounts requires management to estimate future uncollectible receivables, giving discretion that can be used to manage reported earnings in either direction. Increasing or decreasing this estimate directly affects bad debt expense and net income without any change in actual underlying cash collections. Cash and common stock par value, by contrast, are objectively verifiable and offer little room for estimate-driven manipulation.
  194. The correct answer is C (Double-declining balance depreciation, since it accelerates expense recognition early on): Double-declining balance is an accelerated depreciation method that applies a constant rate to the asset's declining book value, producing higher depreciation expense in the earlier years and lower expense later. Straight-line depreciation spreads expense evenly, while units-of-production depreciation varies with actual usage rather than time. Accelerated methods are often used when an asset is expected to be more productive, or to lose value faster, earlier in its life.
  195. The correct answer is B ($48,000, equal to original cost minus accumulated depreciation): Book value on the balance sheet equals an asset's original cost minus its accumulated depreciation to date. Here that is $80,000 minus $32,000 = $48,000. Book value represents the remaining undepreciated cost of the asset, not its current market value.
  196. The correct answer is A (A gain of $7,000, equal to proceeds minus the book value): When an asset is sold, a gain or loss equals the sale proceeds minus the asset's book value at the time of sale. Here that is $55,000 minus $48,000 = a $7,000 gain, which is recognized on the income statement. This gain arises because the company received more cash than the asset's remaining recorded value.
  197. The correct answer is C (A loss of $8,000, equal to the book value minus proceeds): When sale proceeds are less than an asset's book value, the company recognizes a loss equal to the difference. Here that is $48,000 minus $40,000 = an $8,000 loss, recognized on the income statement. This loss reflects that the asset was sold for less cash than its remaining recorded value.
  198. The correct answer is B (The major engine overhaul, since it extends the vehicle's useful life): Expenditures that extend an asset's useful life, increase its capacity, or improve its efficiency are capitalized and depreciated over the remaining useful life, since they provide future economic benefit. Routine maintenance items like oil changes and detailing simply maintain the asset in its normal operating condition and are expensed as incurred. Capitalizing the overhaul matches its cost against the additional periods over which it will provide benefit.
  199. The correct answer is A (Net income is overstated and total assets are also overstated): Incorrectly capitalizing an expenditure that should have been expensed keeps the cost off the income statement in the current period, overstating net income. At the same time, the capitalized amount is recorded as an asset on the balance sheet rather than being expensed, overstating total assets. Both errors work in the same direction, making current period financial performance and position appear stronger than they actually are.
  200. The correct answer is C ($190,000, equal to LIFO inventory plus the disclosed LIFO reserve): The LIFO reserve represents the difference between what inventory would be valued at under FIFO and its reported value under LIFO, disclosed specifically so analysts can convert between the two methods. Adding the LIFO reserve back to LIFO inventory approximates the FIFO inventory value. Here that is $150,000 plus $40,000 = $190,000.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (A LIFO liquidation, caused by inventory quantities declining during the period): A LIFO liquidation occurs when a company using LIFO sells more units than it purchases during a period, forcing it to sell through older, lower-cost inventory layers. Because these older costs are typically lower than current replacement costs, this produces an artificially high, one-time boost to gross profit that is not sustainable. Analysts generally adjust for LIFO liquidation gains when evaluating the underlying trend in a company's profitability.
  2. The correct answer is C (When it is more likely than not that some portion will not be realized): Under US GAAP, a company must record a valuation allowance against a deferred tax asset when it is more likely than not, meaning a probability greater than 50%, that some or all of the asset will not be realized. This typically occurs when a company lacks sufficient expected future taxable income to use the deferred tax benefit. The valuation allowance reduces the net deferred tax asset reported on the balance sheet to its estimated realizable amount.
  3. The correct answer is A (25%, calculated as income tax expense divided by pretax income): The effective tax rate equals income tax expense divided by pretax income, so $100,000 ÷ $400,000 = 25%. This rate reflects the actual overall tax burden a company reports, which can differ from the statutory tax rate due to permanent differences, tax credits, and other adjustments. Analysts often compare the effective tax rate over time to identify unusual or non-recurring tax items.
  4. The correct answer is B (Tax-exempt interest income earned on municipal bond investments): A permanent difference is an item that is recognized for either book or tax purposes but never reverses in a future period, and municipal bond interest income is a classic example since it is never taxable. Accelerated tax depreciation and accrued but not-yet-deductible warranty expense are both temporary differences, since the timing difference between book and tax treatment eventually reverses. Permanent differences do not create deferred tax assets or liabilities, unlike temporary differences.
  5. The correct answer is C (Both methods produce the identical total cash flow from operations figure): The direct and indirect methods are simply two different presentations of the same operating cash flow information, and both must arrive at an identical total cash flow from operations figure. The direct method reports actual cash receipts and payments, while the indirect method starts with net income and adjusts for non-cash items and working capital changes. Only the investing and financing sections are unaffected by this choice, since they are presented identically under both methods.
  6. The correct answer is A (Earnings that are consistently backed by, and convert into, operating cash flow): High-quality earnings are generally characterized by being sustainable, repeatable, and adequately supported by actual cash flow from operations over time. Reliance on non-recurring gains or frequent changes in accounting estimates are both commonly cited warning signs of lower-quality, less sustainable reported earnings. Analysts often compare net income to operating cash flow over several periods as one check on earnings quality.
  7. The correct answer is B (Financing activities, since it involves a transaction with the company's owners): Proceeds from issuing common stock represent a transaction between the company and its owners, raising capital rather than reflecting day-to-day operations or long-term asset purchases. This places it in the financing activities section of the statement of cash flows, alongside items like debt issuance, debt repayment, and dividend payments. Investing activities instead cover the purchase and sale of long-term assets and investments.
  8. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): US GAAP permits the LIFO inventory cost method for external financial reporting, while IFRS explicitly prohibits its use under IAS 2. FIFO and weighted average cost are acceptable under both frameworks. This divergence complicates cross-border comparisons of inventory and cost of goods sold between US GAAP and IFRS reporters.
  9. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): IAS 2 allows reversal of a previous inventory write-down when net realizable value recovers, limited to the amount originally written down. US GAAP prohibits any reversal of an inventory write-down once it has been recognized, treating the new cost basis as permanent. This is a well-known asymmetry between the two frameworks affecting reported inventory and gross margin.
  10. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): IAS 38 requires capitalization of development costs once six specific criteria, including technical feasibility and probable future benefit, are met. US GAAP generally requires research and development costs to be expensed as incurred, with narrow exceptions such as certain internal-use software. This difference can materially affect reported assets and near-term profitability between IFRS and GAAP reporters.
  11. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): IAS 16 allows companies to elect the revaluation model, carrying property, plant, and equipment at fair value less subsequent depreciation, as an alternative to historical cost. US GAAP requires the historical cost model for property, plant, and equipment with no revaluation option available. This means IFRS reporters can show materially higher asset values and equity than comparable GAAP reporters.
  12. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): IFRS gives companies a choice to classify interest paid as either an operating or a financing cash flow, provided the choice is applied consistently. US GAAP requires interest paid to be classified strictly as an operating cash flow, with no election available. This flexibility under IFRS can make cash flow from operations appear higher relative to an otherwise identical GAAP reporter.
  13. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The IASB Conceptual Framework identifies relevance and faithful representation as the two fundamental qualitative characteristics that financial information must possess to be useful. Comparability, verifiability, timeliness, and understandability are enhancing characteristics that increase the usefulness of information that is already relevant and faithfully represented. Information lacking either fundamental characteristic cannot be made useful by the enhancing characteristics alone.
  14. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): IAS 16 requires companies to depreciate each significant component of an asset separately when those components have materially different useful lives, known as componentized depreciation. US GAAP permits but does not require this component approach, so most GAAP reporters depreciate the asset as a single unit. This difference can affect the pattern and timing of depreciation expense between otherwise similar companies.
  15. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Both IFRS and US GAAP require a noncurrent asset classified as held for sale to be measured at the lower of its carrying amount and fair value less costs to sell. Depreciation on the asset ceases once it is classified as held for sale under both frameworks. This measurement rule is one of the more converged areas between the two accounting standards.
  16. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Discontinued operations, representing a component that has been disposed of or is held for sale and meets the criteria for separate reporting, are presented net of tax as a distinct line below income from continuing operations. Unusual or infrequent items remain within continuing operations, presented pretax and disclosed separately. Foreign currency translation adjustments flow through other comprehensive income, not the income statement.
  17. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): An item that is unusual or infrequent but does not qualify as discontinued operations remains part of continuing operations and is reported on a pretax basis, disclosed separately so analysts can identify its effect. It is not reported net of tax like discontinued operations, nor is it a direct adjustment to retained earnings. Separate disclosure helps analysts assess the sustainability of core operating performance.
  18. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The FASB eliminated the separate extraordinary items classification from US GAAP for fiscal years beginning after December 15, 2015, requiring such items to be presented within continuing operations instead. IFRS never permitted a separate extraordinary items classification at all, treating all such items as part of ordinary operations. Items that were once labeled extraordinary are now disclosed as unusual or infrequent items under both frameworks.
  19. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A voluntary change in accounting principle is generally applied retrospectively, meaning prior period financial statements are restated as if the new method had always been used. This retrospective treatment improves comparability across periods for financial statement users. Recording a one-time gain or applying the change only prospectively would understate the effect on historical trends and mislead analysts comparing periods.
  20. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A change in accounting estimate, such as revising an asset's useful life based on new information, is applied prospectively, affecting depreciation expense in the current and future periods only. Prior period financial statements are not restated, and no adjustment is made to beginning retained earnings. This differs from a change in accounting principle, which generally requires retrospective restatement of prior periods.
  21. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Total net income equals $8,000,000 from continuing operations minus the $1,200,000 discontinued operations loss, or $6,800,000. Dividing $6,800,000 by 4,000,000 weighted average shares gives total basic EPS of $1.70 per share. The $2.00 figure reflects only continuing operations EPS before the discontinued operations loss is included, so it overstates total company performance.
  22. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Correction of a material error from a prior period is recorded as a prior-period adjustment, restating the beginning balance of retained earnings and the affected prior period financial statements rather than flowing through current period net income. Extraordinary item classification is no longer available under US GAAP and was never permitted under IFRS. Running the correction through current income would distort the current period's operating performance.
  23. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Operating income after the charge equals $6,000,000 minus $1,500,000, or $4,500,000. Dividing $4,500,000 by revenue of $50,000,000 gives a reported operating margin of 9.0%. Analysts often separately identify the effect of the unusual charge to assess the company's underlying, ongoing operating profitability apart from this one-time item.
  24. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): On a vertical common-size income statement, every line item, including cost of goods sold, operating expenses, and net income, is expressed as a percentage of net revenue, or sales, for the period. This standardization allows analysts to compare cost structures across companies of different sizes or across different years for the same company. Total assets is instead the denominator used for a common-size balance sheet.
  25. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): On a vertical common-size balance sheet, every line item, whether an asset, liability, or equity account, is expressed as a percentage of total assets. This allows analysts to compare capital structure and asset composition across companies of very different absolute sizes. Total revenue is instead the denominator used on a common-size income statement, not the balance sheet.
  26. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A horizontal common-size analysis, often called trend or index analysis, expresses each line item in later years as a percentage of that same line item's value in a chosen base year, which is typically set to 100. This highlights the pace of growth or decline in each account over time. It differs from vertical common-size analysis, which instead expresses items relative to total assets or revenue within a single year.
  27. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Dividing cost of goods sold of $5,200,000 by revenue of $8,000,000 gives 0.65, or 65.0 percent of revenue. This common-size figure implies a gross profit margin of 35.0 percent, since gross profit equals revenue minus cost of goods sold. Common-size percentages allow this cost structure to be compared directly against competitors of very different revenue levels.
  28. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The primary advantage of common-size financial statements is that they allow analysts to meaningfully compare companies of very different sizes, or a single company's own structure across multiple years, by removing the effect of absolute scale. Common-size analysis complements rather than replaces ratio analysis, since ratios provide additional relationships beyond percentage-of-sales or percentage-of-assets figures. It also does not address changes in accounting methods, which require separate adjustment.
  29. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Since cost of goods sold is 62 percent of revenue, the gross profit margin is 100 percent minus 62 percent, or 38 percent, of revenue. Multiplying 38 percent by revenue of $12,000,000 gives gross profit of $4,560,000. This shows how a common-size percentage can be converted back into an absolute dollar figure once the underlying revenue base is known.
  30. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): With the base year indexed at 100, an increase to 128 in year three represents a rise of 28 index points from the base, equal to approximately 28 percent growth in revenue. The index value of 128 itself is not the percentage growth figure, since the base of 100 must first be subtracted. Indexed trend analysis makes multi-year growth rates easy to read directly from the index values.
  31. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A key limitation of common-size statements is that the percentages alone do not reveal the absolute dollar magnitude of the underlying figures, so two companies could show identical common-size percentages while differing enormously in actual scale. This is the opposite of the technique's main advantage, which is comparing firms of different sizes, so that option misstates the limitation. Currency restatement is a separate issue unrelated to common-size percentages themselves.
  32. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The current ratio equals current assets divided by current liabilities, or $3,600,000 divided by $2,400,000, which equals 1.50. A current ratio above 1.0 generally indicates that current assets exceed current liabilities, suggesting adequate short-term liquidity. This ratio does not distinguish between more liquid and less liquid current assets, unlike the quick ratio.
  33. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Quick assets equal cash of $500,000 plus marketable securities of $300,000 plus accounts receivable of $700,000, totaling $1,500,000, which excludes inventory. Dividing quick assets of $1,500,000 by current liabilities of $1,500,000 gives a quick ratio of exactly 1.00. This ratio is a more conservative liquidity measure than the current ratio because it excludes inventory, which may be harder to convert quickly to cash.
  34. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Inventory turnover equals cost of goods sold divided by average inventory, or $4,500,000 divided by $900,000, which equals 5.0 times. This implies the company sells and replaces its average inventory balance five times during the year. A higher turnover ratio generally suggests more efficient inventory management, though excessively high turnover could also indicate stockout risk.
  35. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Days sales outstanding equals average accounts receivable divided by credit sales, multiplied by 365 days, or $600,000 divided by $7,300,000, multiplied by 365, which equals approximately 30 days. This means the company collects its average receivable balance in about a month on average. A lower days sales outstanding figure generally indicates faster and more efficient collection of credit sales.
  36. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Net profit margin equals net income divided by revenue, or $2,100,000 divided by $21,000,000, which equals 0.10, or 10.0 percent. This means the company retains 10 cents of net income for every dollar of revenue generated during the year. Net profit margin is a widely used measure of overall profitability after all expenses, interest, and taxes have been deducted.
  37. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The three-factor DuPont formula multiplies net profit margin, total asset turnover, and financial leverage together, or 8 percent multiplied by 1.5 multiplied by 2.0, which equals 24.0 percent. This decomposition shows that return on equity is driven jointly by profitability, efficient asset use, and the use of financial leverage. A change in any one of the three factors will directly change the resulting return on equity.
  38. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Basic EPS equals net income available to common shareholders divided by weighted average common shares, calculated as $5,000,000 minus $500,000 of preferred dividends, or $4,500,000, divided by 3,000,000 shares, which equals $1.50 per share. Preferred dividends must always be subtracted from net income before dividing by common shares, since preferred shareholders have a prior claim to those earnings. Ignoring the preferred dividend would overstate basic EPS.
  39. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Under the if-converted method, the numerator adds back after-tax interest of $300,000 to net income, giving $6,300,000, while the denominator adds the 500,000 potential shares to the 4,000,000 basic shares, giving 4,500,000 shares. Dividing $6,300,000 by 4,500,000 shares gives diluted EPS of $1.40 per share. Since $1.40 is lower than the basic EPS of $1.50, the convertible bonds are dilutive and must be included in the diluted EPS calculation.
  40. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Under US GAAP, dividends received from investments are classified as operating activities, while dividends paid to the company's own shareholders are classified as financing activities. IFRS instead permits companies a choice for both dividends received and dividends paid, allowing classification within either operating or financing activities. This is an important distinction for analysts reconciling cash flow statements prepared under the two different frameworks.
  41. An increase in a current asset like receivables represents a use of cash, while an increase in a current liability like payables represents a source of cash under the indirect method.
  42. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): During rising prices, LIFO matches the most recently purchased, higher-cost inventory against revenue, producing a higher cost of goods sold than FIFO would report. Because the oldest, lower-cost inventory layers remain on the balance sheet under LIFO, reported ending inventory is correspondingly lower than under FIFO. This also generally results in lower reported taxable income and lower income taxes paid under LIFO during inflationary periods.
  43. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The LIFO reserve represents the difference between LIFO-based inventory and what inventory would have been under FIFO, so FIFO inventory equals LIFO inventory plus the LIFO reserve, or $2,000,000 plus $450,000, which equals $2,450,000. This adjustment allows analysts to compare a LIFO reporter's inventory levels and liquidity ratios directly against a FIFO reporter on a more consistent basis. The same reserve can also be used to adjust cost of goods sold for comparison purposes.
  44. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The double-declining-balance rate equals twice the straight-line rate, or 2 divided by 5 years, which equals 40 percent. Applying this 40 percent rate to the full $100,000 cost, without subtracting salvage value in the first year, gives first-year depreciation expense of $40,000. This accelerated method front-loads depreciation expense relative to straight-line, which would instead produce depreciation of only $18,000 in the first year.
  45. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Accelerated tax depreciation reduces taxable income more than straight-line depreciation reduces pretax financial income in the early years of an asset's life, so pretax financial income exceeds taxable income during those years. This creates a deferred tax liability, since income tax expense reported on the financial statements exceeds the taxes currently payable to the tax authority. The liability reverses in later years as tax depreciation slows and taxable income exceeds financial income instead.
  46. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The effective tax rate equals income tax expense divided by pretax income, or $1,000,000 divided by $4,000,000, which equals 0.25, or 25.0 percent. The effective tax rate reflects the combined effect of current and deferred taxes and can differ meaningfully from a company's statutory tax rate due to permanent differences, tax credits, and other adjustments. Analysts compare effective tax rates across periods and companies to assess earnings quality.
  47. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A valuation allowance must be recorded against a deferred tax asset when it is more likely than not, meaning a probability greater than 50 percent, that some or all of the asset will not be realized through future taxable income. This reduces the carrying value of the deferred tax asset on the balance sheet to its expected recoverable amount. The valuation allowance can be reversed in future periods if the outlook for realizing the asset improves.
  48. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Interest coverage equals EBIT divided by interest expense, or $6,000,000 divided by $1,200,000, which equals 5.0 times. This means the company's operating earnings before interest and taxes are five times larger than its annual interest obligation, indicating a solid ability to meet its debt service from operations. A higher interest coverage ratio generally suggests lower default and solvency risk to creditors and analysts.
  49. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Both IFRS and US GAAP generally require interest cost directly attributable to financing the construction of a qualifying asset to be capitalized as part of that asset's total cost rather than expensed immediately. Capitalized interest is then depreciated over the asset's useful life along with the rest of its cost basis once construction is complete. This treatment matches the borrowing cost with the future economic benefits the constructed asset is expected to generate.
  50. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Total asset turnover equals revenue divided by average total assets, or $9,000,000 divided by $6,000,000, which equals 1.50 times. This ratio measures how efficiently a company uses its total assets to generate revenue, with a higher turnover generally indicating more efficient asset utilization. This figure is also one of the three components multiplied together in the DuPont decomposition of return on equity.
  51. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A director who receives ongoing consulting fees from the company has a material financial relationship with management beyond standard board compensation. Governance frameworks require directors to be free of relationships that could reasonably compromise their objectivity when overseeing management. Because the consulting fee creates economic dependence on the company, this director cannot be classified as independent regardless of tenure. Proper disclosure and reclassification protect the integrity of committees that require independent oversight, such as audit and compensation committees.
  52. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The preemptive right entitles existing shareholders to purchase new shares in proportion to their current ownership before shares are offered to outside parties. A private placement to a strategic investor bypasses this offering to existing holders and can dilute their proportional ownership and voting power. Rights offerings are the mechanism specifically designed to protect this right, so choosing a private placement instead directly circumvents it. Companies must often justify or obtain shareholder waivers when preemptive rights are set aside in this way.
  53. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Perquisite consumption occurs when managers use company resources, such as expanded travel budgets or luxury amenities, for personal benefit rather than to enhance shareholder value. Seeking a larger jet allowance and discretionary travel budget after weak performance is a textbook example of extracting private benefits at the firm's expense. This differs from empire building, which involves value-destroying acquisitions, and from excess risk aversion, which involves forgoing good projects. Perquisite consumption directly reduces cash flow available to shareholders without a corresponding business justification.
  54. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Materiality in ESG analysis means prioritizing factors that are likely to have a meaningful financial impact on a specific company given its industry and business model. Environmental risks vary widely by sector, so an analyst should first assess which factors are genuinely decision-useful for a manufacturer rather than applying a generic checklist. Peer disclosure practices and regulatory mandates may inform the analysis but do not substitute for a company-specific materiality assessment. Focusing on materiality improves the relevance of ESG analysis to investment decisions.
  55. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Modigliani and Miller's Proposition I under the no-tax assumption states that in perfect capital markets, a firm's value and its weighted average cost of capital are independent of its financing mix. Because there are no taxes, bankruptcy costs, or informational frictions, shifting between debt and equity simply reallocates the same total cash flows among different claimants without creating or destroying value. This result establishes capital structure irrelevance as a baseline before real-world frictions like taxes are introduced. It is a foundational, though idealized, starting point for later capital structure theories.
  56. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The static trade-off theory posits that firms weigh the tax advantages of debt financing against the increasing probability and cost of financial distress as leverage rises. This balancing process implies that an optimal capital structure exists at the point where the marginal benefit of additional debt equals its marginal cost. It contrasts with Modigliani-Miller's no-tax irrelevance result and with the pecking order theory, which does not target a specific optimal ratio. Firms following this framework actively manage leverage toward a targeted range rather than treating financing as irrelevant.
  57. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A staggered, or classified, board structure means only a fraction of directors face election each year, making it difficult for shareholders to replace a majority of the board quickly even if dissatisfied with performance. This structure can entrench incumbent management and directors by slowing the pace at which shareholders can effect change, particularly during a hostile takeover attempt. It is widely viewed as an antitakeover defense mechanism and a governance weakness from a shareholder rights perspective. Governance advocates often push for annual election of all directors instead.
  58. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Cumulative voting allows a shareholder to allocate their total voting power, calculated as shares held multiplied by open board seats, across candidates in any combination rather than casting a fixed number of votes per candidate. This flexibility makes it easier for a coordinated minority bloc to concentrate votes on a single candidate and secure at least one board seat. Under statutory or straight voting, majority shareholders can sweep every board seat, leaving minority holders without representation. Cumulative voting is therefore generally viewed as more favorable to minority shareholder interests.
  59. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Asset substitution occurs when shareholders, who benefit from unlimited upside but have limited downside due to limited liability, favor riskier investments financed by debt because bondholders bear a disproportionate share of the downside risk. Approving a large leverage increase for a risky expansion transfers risk toward creditors while shareholders retain the potential gains. This wealth transfer dynamic is a core source of conflict between shareholders and bondholders in agency theory. Bond covenants are often used specifically to constrain this type of risk-shifting behavior.
  60. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A say-on-pay vote is a non-binding advisory vote through which shareholders express approval or disapproval of executive compensation packages without legally overturning the board's decision. Although the vote does not have direct legal force, a strong negative result creates reputational and market pressure that often prompts the compensation committee to revisit its approach. This mechanism gives shareholders a formal voice in pay-setting even where binding authority rests with the board. It has become a standard governance practice in many major markets.
  61. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Stakeholder theory holds that a firm has obligations not only to shareholders but also to employees, customers, suppliers, and the communities in which it operates, and that long-term strategy should account for these broader interests. This contrasts with the strict shareholder primacy view, which frames management's duty as maximizing shareholder value above other considerations. Explicitly weighing multiple constituencies in strategic decisions reflects this stakeholder-oriented governance philosophy. Many modern governance codes now incorporate elements of both perspectives.
  62. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Under Proposition II with taxes, the cost of equity rises as leverage increases because higher debt levels increase the financial risk borne by equity holders, who face a more volatile residual claim on cash flows after interest obligations. Although the after-tax cost of debt is lower than the cost of equity, the rising cost of equity offsets part of this benefit as leverage grows. This linear relationship between cost of equity and the debt-to-equity ratio reflects the additional financial risk premium shareholders demand. The overall WACC still tends to decline with moderate leverage due to the tax shield, even as the cost of equity itself rises.
  63. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The audit committee is responsible for overseeing financial reporting integrity, the external audit relationship, and internal control effectiveness, and best practice calls for it to consist entirely of independent, non-executive directors. Independence reduces the risk that management can unduly influence financial reporting or audit outcomes, while relevant financial expertise ensures the committee can meaningfully evaluate complex accounting and audit issues. Staffing the committee with executives or family members would undermine its ability to provide objective oversight. Regulators and listing standards in many markets mandate this level of independence for audit committees.
  64. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Shareholder activism involves an investor, often holding a significant equity stake, publicly pressing for changes in strategy, governance, or board composition to unlock perceived value. Campaigning to replace directors and pursue a divestiture is a classic activist tactic aimed squarely at influencing corporate direction rather than passively holding shares. This differs from passive index investing, which avoids engagement, and from appraisal rights, which apply to dissenting shareholders in a completed transaction. Activist campaigns can be a meaningful catalyst for governance and operational change at underperforming companies.
  65. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Monitoring costs are expenses the principal, in this case shareholders acting through the board, incurs to oversee and constrain management's actions, such as hiring compensation consultants or commissioning independent audits. These costs are distinct from bonding costs, which are incurred by the agent to demonstrate trustworthy behavior, and from residual losses, which represent the value lost despite monitoring and bonding efforts. Investing in monitoring mechanisms is a direct way for shareholders to reduce the risk of managerial actions that diverge from their interests. Together, monitoring costs, bonding costs, and residual losses make up total agency costs.
  66. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The pecking order theory holds that firms prefer internal financing first, then debt, and only issue new equity as a last resort because equity issuance is most sensitive to information asymmetry and can signal that shares are overvalued. This financing hierarchy minimizes adverse selection costs that arise when outside investors are uncertain about a firm's true prospects relative to insiders. Unlike the static trade-off theory, the pecking order does not target a specific optimal debt ratio but instead reflects a sequential preference driven by information concerns. This theory helps explain why profitable firms often carry lower debt levels despite tax incentives to borrow.
  67. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A dual-class share structure grants different voting rights to different classes of common stock, allowing a founder or insider group to retain control through disproportionately high-vote shares while holding a smaller share of overall economic ownership. This structure can insulate management from shareholder discipline and takeover pressure, raising governance concerns even when it preserves founder vision. It stands in contrast to a one-share, one-vote structure, which aligns voting power directly with economic ownership. Regulators and index providers have increasingly scrutinized dual-class arrangements for this reason.
  68. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Appraisal rights allow a shareholder who dissents from a merger or similar fundamental transaction to demand that the company purchase their shares at a fair value determined through an independent or judicial process, rather than accepting the merger consideration. This remedy protects minority shareholders who believe the transaction undervalues their shares or was not conducted at arm's length. It operates independently of the shareholder's voting decision and provides an exit mechanism outside the terms of the deal itself. Appraisal proceedings can be a meaningful check on inadequately priced transactions.
  69. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Board diversity statistics, executive pay ratios, and related-party transaction review frequency all concern how the company is directed and controlled, placing them squarely within the governance pillar of ESG. The governance pillar focuses on board composition, executive oversight, shareholder rights, and internal controls rather than environmental impact or social outcomes. While governance practices can indirectly influence environmental and social performance, these specific disclosures are governance metrics by nature. Investors use such disclosures to assess the quality of oversight and accountability structures at a company.
  70. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Once corporate taxes are introduced, interest payments on debt are tax deductible, creating a tax shield whose present value adds directly to the value of a levered firm relative to an identical unlevered firm. This result modifies the original no-tax irrelevance proposition by showing that, absent other frictions, increasing leverage increases firm value through this tax benefit. It does not imply that leverage is costless in practice, since bankruptcy and agency costs eventually offset the tax advantage at higher debt levels. This tax-adjusted framework is a key extension of the original Modigliani-Miller propositions.
  71. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): An independent director is typically defined as one who lacks material business, financial, or family ties to the company beyond standard board compensation and has not recently been an employee. The description given, with no such conflicting relationships and no recent employment history, matches the standard definition of independence used in governance codes and stock exchange listing requirements. This classification matters because independent directors are expected to provide objective oversight of management, particularly in committees such as audit and compensation. Directors who fail these criteria, even with long board tenure, may still be considered independent if no disqualifying relationship exists.
  72. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The free cash flow hypothesis describes how managers of mature firms with limited growth opportunities may retain and misallocate excess cash, for example by pursuing value-destroying acquisitions, rather than returning it to shareholders through dividends or buybacks. This behavior reflects an agency conflict in which entrenched management prioritizes control over firm resources over efficient capital allocation. Distributing excess cash reduces the discretionary funds available for such overinvestment, which is why payout policy is often viewed as a governance mechanism. The scenario described is a canonical illustration of this hypothesis in action.
  73. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Under the static trade-off theory, firm value is maximized at the debt level where the marginal benefit of the interest tax shield exactly equals the marginal expected cost of financial distress. Below this point, adding debt increases value because the tax shield dominates; beyond this point, rising distress costs outweigh the tax benefit and further leverage destroys value. This framework implies an interior optimum rather than a corner solution at zero or maximum feasible debt. Firms use this logic to guide target leverage ratios in practice.
  74. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Proxy voting allows a shareholder who cannot attend the annual general meeting in person to authorize another party, often a designated management representative, to cast votes on their behalf according to submitted voting instructions. This mechanism enables broad shareholder participation in corporate decisions without requiring physical presence at the meeting. Proxy materials typically accompany the meeting notice and outline the matters to be voted on, including director elections and other proposals. Proxy voting is a standard and widely used method of exercising shareholder voting rights in publicly traded companies.
  75. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Shareholders generally hold a statutory right to inspect certain corporate books and records, such as board minutes and shareholder lists, particularly when they have a proper purpose such as investigating suspected mismanagement. This right supports shareholder oversight of management and the board by providing access to information not otherwise disclosed in public filings. It is distinct from the preemptive right, which concerns new share issuances, and from any right to compel a specific dividend, which shareholders generally do not hold. Jurisdictions typically impose procedural requirements on how and when this inspection right can be exercised.
  76. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Negative screening involves excluding entire industries or specific companies from a portfolio based on predefined criteria, such as involvement in tobacco, weapons, or thermal coal, regardless of those companies' individual ESG performance scores. This approach applies a blanket exclusion rule rather than selectively favoring the best performers within a sector, which would instead describe positive or best-in-class screening. It differs from thematic investing, which actively seeks exposure to a specific trend rather than avoiding certain sectors. Negative screening remains one of the most widely used ESG integration approaches among asset managers.
  77. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A two-tier board structure legally separates the management board, responsible for day-to-day operations, from a distinct supervisory board that oversees management with no overlapping membership between the two bodies. This separation is intended to strengthen independent oversight by structurally preventing executives from also sitting on the body that supervises them. It differs from a one-tier board, where executives and non-executives sit together on a single unified board, and it is unrelated to staggered election terms. Two-tier structures are common in certain European corporate governance systems.
  78. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Information asymmetry refers to the gap between what company insiders know about the firm's true prospects and what outside investors can observe, which can cause new securities to be mispriced by the market. This concept underlies several capital structure theories, including the pecking order theory, which predicts firms will prefer financing sources that minimize the costs created by this informational gap. It differs from cumulative voting, which concerns board election mechanics, and from appraisal rights, which apply to dissenting shareholders in a merger. Reducing information asymmetry through disclosure can lower a firm's cost of capital over time.
  79. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Bankruptcy costs, which include both direct costs such as legal and administrative fees and indirect costs such as lost customers or suppliers, rise as a firm's leverage increases beyond the point where the tax shield benefit is offset by growing default risk. Lenders demand higher yields to compensate for this elevated risk, further raising the firm's overall cost of capital. These costs are a central input into the static trade-off theory's determination of an optimal capital structure. They are distinct from flotation costs, which relate specifically to the process of issuing new securities.
  80. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Related-party transactions, such as a director's firm receiving a contract without competitive or independent review, require formal evaluation and approval by independent directors to guard against self-dealing and conflicts of interest. This process typically involves disclosure of the relationship, an assessment of whether the terms are fair to the company, and documented approval outside the influence of the conflicted director. Automatic ratification or delegation to an unrelated department would not provide the independent scrutiny such transactions require. Strong governance codes treat related-party transaction oversight as a core board responsibility.
  81. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Shareholders generally hold the right to vote on extraordinary corporate transactions such as mergers, because these transactions can fundamentally alter the company's structure, ownership, and risk profile. Requiring shareholder approval for a stock-for-stock merger ensures that owners have a formal say before such a significant change is completed. This right is distinct from routine inspection rights or preemptive rights, which apply to different circumstances entirely. Merger votes are typically subject to a specified approval threshold set by corporate statute or the company's governing documents.
  82. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Thematic investing targets a specific long-term environmental or social trend, such as carbon capture technology or sustainable water infrastructure, by directing capital toward companies positioned to benefit from that trend rather than screening out particular industries. This approach is forward-looking and trend-specific, contrasting with negative screening, which excludes companies based on exclusionary criteria rather than seeking positive thematic exposure. It also differs from proxy voting, which concerns exercising voting rights rather than portfolio construction. Thematic strategies have grown as investors seek targeted exposure to structural sustainability trends.
  83. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Modigliani and Miller's original irrelevance propositions rest on the assumption of perfect capital markets, meaning no taxes, no bankruptcy costs, no transaction costs, and no information asymmetry between managers and investors. Under these idealized conditions, the mix of debt and equity used to finance a firm's operations does not affect its total value or its weighted average cost of capital. Real-world capital structure theories, such as the trade-off and pecking order theories, relax these assumptions to explain observed financing behavior. The original propositions serve as a theoretical benchmark rather than a description of actual markets.
  84. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Managerial entrenchment occurs when executives take actions, such as surrounding the board with allies, resisting value-enhancing takeover offers, or structuring pay to reward tenure, that insulate them from shareholder discipline and the market for corporate control. This behavior prioritizes management's own job security over shareholder value maximization, representing a clear agency conflict. It differs from bonding costs, which are voluntary commitments to align with shareholders, and from the pecking order preference, which concerns financing choices rather than control dynamics. Entrenchment can persist for years absent effective board oversight or external pressure.
  85. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Favorable financial leverage occurs when a firm's operating return on assets exceeds its after-tax cost of debt, so that borrowing to fund operations magnifies the returns available to shareholders on their smaller equity base. Because operating performance remains constant and the cost of debt is lower than the return generated on assets, adding leverage increases return on equity rather than diminishing it. This relationship reverses if operating returns fall below the cost of debt, at which point leverage would reduce return on equity instead. This dynamic illustrates why leverage decisions interact directly with profitability outcomes for shareholders.

Debt contributes the smallest amount because it carries the lowest cost despite a large weight. Common equity dominates the result since it carries both the highest cost and the largest weight in the structure.

This tax shield lowers the effective cost of debt below its stated yield to maturity. Using the pretax 8% figure directly in WACC would overstate the true cost of debt financing.

Here, $6 ÷ $75 = 0.08, or 8.0%. Because preferred dividends are not tax deductible, no tax adjustment is applied, unlike the cost of debt calculation.

The beta of 1.2 indicates the stock is more volatile than the overall market, so it earns a return above the market risk premium alone. This CAPM estimate is commonly used as the cost of common equity in WACC.

This dividend discount approach assumes dividends grow at a stable rate indefinitely. It provides an alternative to CAPM for estimating a firm's cost of common equity.

This method identifies how quickly the original outlay is recovered but ignores any cash flows generated after year four. It also ignores the time value of money, unlike the discounted payback period.
  95. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Discounted payback is always equal to or longer than the simple payback period: discounting reduces the present value of each future cash flow relative to its nominal amount, so more discounted dollars of cash flow are needed to recover the same initial investment. As a result, the number of periods required under the discounted method can never be shorter than under the undiscounted method. The two methods converge only in the special case of a zero discount rate.

This measures how long cash is tied up in operations before it is collected from customers, net of supplier financing. A shorter cash conversion cycle generally reduces a firm's need for external short-term funding.

  100. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Choose the project with the higher NPV, since it uses a realistic reinvestment rate assumption: NPV assumes interim cash flows are reinvested at the firm's cost of capital, a defensible and consistent assumption, while IRR implicitly assumes reinvestment at the project's own IRR, which can be unrealistic for high-IRR projects. When NPV and IRR rankings conflict due to scale or timing differences, NPV correctly measures the absolute increase in shareholder wealth. Following the higher-NPV project therefore better achieves the goal of wealth maximization.
  101. Here, $2,000,000 ÷ 0.40 = $5,000,000 in total new capital. Beyond this level of total financing, the firm must raise additional debt at a higher cost, raising the marginal cost of capital.

This lower after-tax figure, not the pretax yield, is the appropriate input for the weighted average cost of capital.
  103. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Market value weights are theoretically preferred because they reflect investors' current claims on firm value: book values reflect historical accounting costs that may diverge substantially from what investors would actually receive today, while market values represent the current opportunity cost of capital for debt and equity holders. Using market value weights ensures the WACC reflects the firm's true current financing mix. This is why analysts generally adjust reported book values to approximate market values when data allows.
  104. This positive NPV represents a direct addition to firm value beyond what the required 9% return demands. No further payback analysis is needed to justify accepting an independent project with a clearly positive NPV.
  105. Since 15% exceeds the firm's 10% required return, the project earns a return above its financing cost and creates value for shareholders. For an independent, conventional-cash-flow project, IRR above the cost of capital and NPV above zero lead to the same accept decision.
  106. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): It ignores cash flows occurring after the payback period and, in its simple form, ignores the time value of money: because payback only measures how long it takes to recover the initial outlay, it disregards any profitability generated after that breakeven point, potentially favoring a shorter but less valuable project over a longer, more profitable one. The simple version also fails to discount future cash flows, treating a dollar received in year five the same as a dollar received today. These limitations mean payback should supplement, not replace, NPV and IRR in capital budgeting decisions.
  107. This delays the firm's own cash outflows while collections and inventory turnover remain unchanged, freeing up cash and reducing the need for external short-term financing. Extending customer credit terms or holding more inventory would instead lengthen, not shorten, the cash conversion cycle.
  108. Adjusting the WACC upward instead would understate NPV for long-lived projects and overstate it for short-lived ones, since flotation is a one-time cost being spread incorrectly across time. Ignoring flotation costs entirely would overstate the project's true net present value.

With beta at 1.5, cost of equity equals 4% + 1.5(5%) = 11.5%. The difference, 11.5% − 9.0%, equals 2.5 percentage points, reflecting the fact that a 0.5 increase in beta scales directly by the 5% market risk premium.

Because preferred dividends do not grow and are paid in perpetuity, no growth adjustment is applied, unlike the dividend discount model used for common equity. Using target weights provides a more consistent and forward-looking cost of capital estimate for capital budgeting.

This approach backs out the market's implied required return from the observed stock price rather than starting from an assumed discount rate. It requires the assumption that dividends grow at a constant rate indefinitely.
  116. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Projects with cash flows that change sign more than once can produce multiple IRRs, making NPV the more reliable decision criterion: Descartes' rule of signs implies that a cash flow stream with more than one sign change can yield more than one mathematically valid discount rate that sets NPV to zero. This makes the IRR rule ambiguous or misleading for such nonconventional projects. NPV remains reliable in these cases because it produces a single, unambiguous dollar value at the firm's cost of capital regardless of how many sign changes occur.
  117. Below the crossover rate, the project preferred under NPV may differ from the project preferred under IRR, creating a ranking conflict. Above the crossover rate, both criteria typically agree on which project is superior.

  121. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A callable feature giving the issuer the right to redeem. A callable preferred share gives the issuer, not the holder, the right to repurchase the shares at a stated call price after a call-protection period ends. Issuers add this feature so they can retire the shares if market interest rates fall or their capital needs change. A putable share instead gives the holder the right to sell back, and a participating share allows extra dividends tied to earnings, neither of which matches the issuer buy-back right described.
  122. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): a private placement sold directly to qualified institutional buyers. A private placement involves selling securities directly to a limited number of accredited or institutional investors without a public offering, registration process, or broad marketing. This route avoids the cost and disclosure burden of a public issue while giving the issuer negotiated terms with sophisticated buyers. A shelf registration and a rights offering both involve securities registered for, or offered to, a broader or existing shareholder base rather than a small private group.
  123. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Unpaid dividends accumulate as arrears owed before common dividends. Cumulative preferred stock requires any skipped preferred dividends to build up as arrears that the company must pay in full before it can resume paying common shareholders. Non-cumulative preferred stock has no such requirement, so a skipped dividend on non-cumulative shares is simply forfeited. Neither type automatically converts unpaid dividends into common shares, which describes a separate convertible feature entirely.
  124. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): sponsored American Depositary Receipt backed by deposited foreign shares. A sponsored ADR is created when a depositary bank, working with the foreign issuer, holds the underlying foreign shares and issues receipts that trade in the local market, giving investors indirect exposure without cross-border settlement. A dual listing instead means the actual shares themselves trade directly on two separate exchanges. A global registered share is a single class of shares traded and settled in multiple currencies and markets rather than a bank-issued receipt.
  125. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): market-capitalization weighting based on total company market value. Market-capitalization weighting sets each constituent's index weight equal to its share price multiplied by shares outstanding relative to the total market value of all constituents, so larger companies exert proportionally more influence. Price weighting instead bases weight solely on share price, ignoring shares outstanding entirely. Equal weighting assigns every constituent the same weight regardless of either price or market value.

In a price-weighted index, a stock split reduces the sum of prices without any real change in underlying value, so the divisor must be lowered to keep the index level constant at the moment of the split. Raising the divisor would further distort the index level in the wrong direction. Changing the number of constituents is unrelated, since a split does not add or remove a stock from the index.

After the change, the sum becomes 10 + 20 + 36 = 66, and dividing by the unchanged divisor of 3 gives a new index level of 22. The percentage change is (22 - 20) / 20 = 10.0%, so the other two figures overstate the true price-weighted index movement.

Float adjustment excludes shares held by insiders, governments, or other strategic holders that are not available for public trading, so only the actual investable float determines each constituent's weight. This produces a more accurate benchmark for the shares an investor could realistically buy. Fundamental weighting instead uses financial metrics rather than trading availability, and price weighting ignores share counts altogether.
