  130. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): fundamental weighting built directly from company financial data. Fundamental weighting assigns index weights based on company fundamentals such as revenue, book value, cash flow, and dividends rather than market price or number of shares outstanding. Proponents argue this reduces the tendency of price or capitalization weighting to overweight temporarily overvalued stocks. Equal weighting and float-adjusted weighting both rely on either uniform allocation or market-based float, neither of which uses fundamental financial metrics.
  131. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Frequent rebalancing since constituent prices drift apart between dates. Because equal weighting assigns the same weight to every constituent only at the rebalancing date, price movements immediately after rebalancing cause weights to drift apart, requiring frequent trading to restore equal weights. Market-capitalization-weighted indexes self-adjust as prices move and need rebalancing mainly for additions, deletions, or corporate actions. Equal weighting has no such self-adjusting property, so it demands materially more turnover to maintain its target scheme.
  132. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Weak-form efficiency, concerning only historical price and volume data. The weak-form efficient market hypothesis holds that current prices already reflect all past price and volume information, so technical analysis based purely on historical price patterns cannot consistently produce abnormal risk-adjusted returns. Semi-strong form additionally requires that all public information, not just price history, be reflected, while strong form requires private information also be reflected. The argument described concerns only historical price patterns, which is specifically the weak-form claim.
  133. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): value anomaly tied to cheap valuation multiples outperforming. The value anomaly refers to the persistent tendency of low price-to-earnings, low price-to-book, or similarly cheaply valued stocks to generate higher risk-adjusted returns than their theoretically implied fair value would predict. This pattern challenges market efficiency because it suggests a predictable, exploitable relationship using only public valuation data. A calendar anomaly instead relates to returns clustering around specific dates, and a momentum anomaly relates to continuation of recent price trends rather than valuation levels.
  134. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Strong form, which requires private information be fully reflected. Strong-form market efficiency asserts that prices reflect all information, public and private, meaning even insiders with material nonpublic information should be unable to earn abnormal profits. Consistent insider profits from private information directly contradicts this strongest efficiency claim while remaining fully consistent with both weak-form and semi-strong-form efficiency, since neither of those forms addresses private information at all. This distinction is why strong form is considered the most demanding and least empirically supported version of the hypothesis.
  135. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): all publicly available information, including past prices and fundamentals. Semi-strong form efficiency encompasses weak-form efficiency and extends it to include all publicly disclosed information such as financial statements, earnings announcements, and economic data, not just historical trading data. It stops short of requiring that private, nonpublic information be reflected in prices. This is why fundamental analysis of public filings should not reliably generate abnormal returns if markets are semi-strong-form efficient.
  136. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The momentum effect, tied to continuation of recent trends. The momentum effect describes the empirical finding that stocks with strong recent price performance tend to continue outperforming over intermediate horizons of roughly three to twelve months, which directly contradicts weak-form efficiency's prediction that past price trends carry no predictive power. The small-firm effect concerns size-based return premiums rather than trend continuation, and the January effect concerns calendar-based seasonal patterns. Momentum specifically exploits sequential price behavior, making it the clearest weak-form challenge among the three.
  137. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Semi-strong form, since public financial data should already be priced. If a fund can consistently generate statistically significant positive alpha using only publicly available financial statement data, this directly contradicts the semi-strong-form claim that prices already fully incorporate all public information. Weak-form efficiency alone would not necessarily be violated since financial statement data extends beyond mere price history. The fund's success relies entirely on public information rather than private insider access, so it does not speak to strong-form efficiency specifically.
  138. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): statistical government classification organized around production process similarity. Statistical or government classification systems, such as those used for national economic data, group companies according to similarities in inputs, raw materials, and production processes rather than by the markets they ultimately serve. Products-and-services-based classification and customer-and-market-oriented classification instead organize companies by what they sell and to whom, reflecting demand-side characteristics rather than production-side inputs. This production-process orientation is the defining feature that separates statistical systems from commercial index classification schemes.
  139. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Strong bargaining power of suppliers, which compresses industry profitability. When only a few suppliers control a critical input, switching costs are high, and no substitutes exist, suppliers can dictate pricing and terms, squeezing the margins of the industry that depends on them. This scenario describes supplier power, not buyer power, since the concentration and leverage sit on the input side rather than the customer side. High supplier concentration is one of Porter's five forces most directly linked to eroding industry-wide profitability.
  140. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A high threat of new entrants given the weak entry barriers. Low capital requirements, no proprietary technology, and weak brand loyalty all remove the barriers that would otherwise deter new competitors from entering the industry, so the threat of new entrants is high. A high threat of entry typically increases rivalry rather than lowering it, and it does not by itself say anything about buyer bargaining power. Industries with minimal entry barriers generally experience compressed long-run profitability as new capacity keeps flowing in.
  141. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): embryonic stage, marked by explosive early growth and risk. The embryonic stage of the industry life cycle features rapid percentage growth from a small revenue base, high uncertainty and business risk, often negative or thin profitability, and substantial reinvestment needs as the industry establishes itself. The shakeout stage instead follows a period of strong growth and features slowing demand and intensifying competition, while the decline stage features falling demand and firm exits. Only the embryonic stage combines a small base with explosive early growth and heavy risk as described.
  142. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Slowing growth, intensifying price competition, and declining profit margins. The shakeout stage follows rapid growth and is marked by decelerating demand growth as the industry matures, forcing surviving competitors into aggressive price competition that compresses margins and drives weaker firms out. Accelerating growth with broadly rising margins instead describes the earlier growth stage, not shakeout. Stable demand with no change in competitive intensity contradicts the defining shakeout characteristic of intensifying rivalry as growth slows.
  143. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Growth converging toward overall economic growth with stable market shares. In the mature stage, industry growth converges toward the broader economy's growth rate, competitive positions and market shares stabilize among surviving firms, and new entry becomes less attractive. Growth well above GDP with frequent new entrants instead characterizes the earlier growth stage. Negative growth with widespread firm exits describes the later decline stage rather than maturity.
  144. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): statutory or cumulative voting right tied to share ownership. Common shareholders typically receive voting rights that let them elect directors and vote on major corporate actions like mergers, exercised through either a statutory (one vote per share per director) or cumulative (votes may be concentrated on fewer candidates) voting structure. A callable feature instead describes an issuer's right to redeem a security, and a conversion privilege describes the right to exchange one security for another, neither of which relates to electing directors.
  145. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Minority shareholders trying to secure at least one board seat. Cumulative voting allows a shareholder to concentrate all of their available votes on one or a few director candidates instead of spreading a fixed number of votes across every open seat, making it easier for a minority bloc to secure at least one board seat. This mechanism reduces, rather than reinforces, the advantage that a controlling majority shareholder would otherwise hold under straight statutory voting. Preferred shareholders are excluded from this analysis entirely since the question addresses common shareholder voting mechanics.
  146. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): dual-class common stock giving founders disproportionate voting control. Dual-class common stock structures issue two or more classes of common shares with different voting rights per share, commonly used to let founders retain control while raising capital from public investors who receive economically similar but less powerful shares. Non-voting cumulative preferred stock is a fixed-income-like security with no ordinary voting rights at all, not a common share class. Putable common stock is not a standard equity structure, since put features are associated with certain preferred or debt securities rather than common shares.
  147. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): a decentralized network of competing dealers rather than one location. Over-the-counter markets operate through a decentralized network of dealers who quote bid and ask prices and trade directly with clients or other dealers, without a single physical or electronic centralized order book. A single centralized limit order book with set hours instead describes many organized exchanges. An auction market with a designated specialist for every stock is also a hallmark of certain exchange structures, not the dealer-driven over-the-counter model.
  148. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Inventory risk and the ongoing cost of providing liquidity. The bid-ask spread compensates a dealer for holding inventory risk, the opportunity cost of capital tied up in the position, and the risk of trading against better-informed counterparties, all of which are the costs of continuously standing ready to buy and sell. The spread has no direct relationship to the stock's expected dividend growth rate or the company's total market capitalization, both of which are valuation inputs rather than market-making cost drivers. Thinly traded stocks typically show wider spreads precisely because these liquidity-provision costs are higher.
  149. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The primary market, where newly issued shares raise capital. An initial public offering involves the sale of newly issued shares directly from the company to investors for the first time, raising fresh capital for the issuer, which is the defining feature of the primary market. The secondary market instead involves trading of already-issued shares among investors with no new capital flowing to the company. A dark pool is a type of trading venue for secondary-market transactions, not a mechanism for issuing new shares.
  150. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): secondary market, where ownership simply changes hands over time. Trading between existing investors after a company's initial listing occurs in the secondary market, where ownership changes hands but no new capital is raised for the issuing company. The primary market is reserved for the original sale of newly issued securities. An underwriting syndicate is a group of investment banks that helps distribute a new issue in the primary market, not a venue for ongoing investor-to-investor trading.
  151. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): margin, which uses borrowed funds secured by the shares. Buying on margin means financing part of a purchase with borrowed funds from a broker, using the purchased securities as collateral, which amplifies both potential gains and losses relative to an all-cash purchase. A short sale instead involves selling borrowed shares to profit from an expected price decline, not financing a purchase. A rights offering is a method companies use to raise capital from existing shareholders, unrelated to an individual investor's use of borrowed funds.
  152. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): short sale intended to profit from an anticipated price decline. A short sale involves borrowing shares from a broker, selling them immediately in the market, and later repurchasing identical shares to return to the lender, profiting if the price falls in the interim. Rights subscription instead refers to existing shareholders exercising rights to buy newly issued shares at a discount. A leveraged buyout describes an acquisition financed largely with debt, an entirely different corporate finance transaction unrelated to borrowing and selling shares.
  153. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): discount to net asset value, since price is below twenty. Because the closed-end fund's market price of $18.50 is below its net asset value per share of $20.00, the shares trade at a discount, a common phenomenon for closed-end funds whose fixed share count means market price is set by supply and demand rather than automatic arbitrage to net asset value. A premium would occur if the market price exceeded net asset value instead. Trading exactly at net asset value would require price and net asset value to be identical, which is not the case here.
  154. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The fund's net asset value calculated once at the end of each day. Open-end mutual funds transact directly with investors at a price equal to the fund's net asset value per share, calculated once daily after markets close, rather than through continuous intraday market pricing. A fixed price set only at the initial offering describes neither open-end nor closed-end funds accurately, since both structures reflect ongoing valuation. The prevailing exchange-traded market price throughout the day instead describes how closed-end funds and exchange-traded funds are priced.
  155. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): An arbitrage mechanism where authorized participants create or redeem shares. Authorized participants can create or redeem exchange-traded fund shares in large blocks in exchange for the underlying basket of securities, and this arbitrage opportunity keeps the fund's market price closely aligned with its net asset value throughout the trading day. A fixed daily share count would actually work against this alignment, since exchange-traded funds rely on a flexible, continuously adjustable share count. No government guarantee is involved in exchange-traded fund pricing at all.
  156. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): share dilution, since the company must issue brand new shares. Exercising a warrant requires the company to issue brand-new common shares directly to the warrant holder, increasing total shares outstanding and diluting existing shareholders' proportional ownership. This differs from exercising an exchange-traded call option, which involves the transfer of already-existing shares between two market participants and creates no new shares. Warrant exercise also brings in new cash to the issuer rather than reducing the company's outstanding debt.
  157. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): increase earnings per share by reducing the total share count. Repurchasing shares reduces the total number of shares outstanding, and with net income held constant, dividing the same earnings figure by a smaller share count mechanically increases earnings per share. An increasing share count would instead decrease earnings per share, the opposite of what a buyback accomplishes. Claiming no effect under any circumstances ignores this basic mechanical relationship between share count and per-share earnings metrics.
  158. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): DuPont analysis, which multiplies margin, turnover, and leverage together. DuPont analysis decomposes return on equity into the product of net profit margin, total asset turnover, and the financial leverage multiplier, allowing analysts to identify which operational or financing driver is responsible for changes in profitability over time. Porter's Five Forces instead evaluates competitive industry structure rather than a company's own financial ratios. Industry life-cycle analysis examines growth-stage dynamics across an industry, neither of which involves multiplying financial ratio components to explain return on equity.
  159. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Intense rivalry among firms that compresses industry-wide profit margins. When many similarly sized competitors offer largely undifferentiated products, firms compete primarily on price, which drives industry-wide rivalry higher and compresses profit margins for everyone involved. Elevated pricing power expanding margins would require differentiation or concentration that this scenario explicitly lacks. Complete insulation from competitive pressure directly contradicts the described conditions of numerous close substitutes and minimal differentiation.
  160. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): a differentiation competitive strategy built on brand loyalty and features. Building strong brand loyalty and unique, hard-to-replicate product features is the hallmark of a differentiation strategy, which allows a company to command premium pricing and reduce direct price competition. A pure cost leadership strategy instead focuses on being the lowest-cost producer rather than on distinctive branding or features. Vertical integration concerns controlling supply chain stages rather than building customer-facing brand loyalty, so it does not describe the strategy in the scenario.
  161. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): greater than 20 percent, having drifted upward with its price. In an equal-weighted index, each of the five constituents starts at a 20% weight at rebalancing, but if one stock rises 40% while the other four are flat, that stock's market value grows relative to the total, pushing its weight above 20% until the next rebalancing restores equal weights. The weight cannot remain exactly 20% once relative prices diverge between rebalancing dates. A weight below 20% would require the stock to underperform, the opposite of the described 40% gain.
  162. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): fundamental weighting, since a macroeconomic country statistic is used. Weighting by gross domestic product uses a macroeconomic fundamental characteristic of the constituent's home country rather than the security's own market price or float-adjusted market capitalization, which places it within the broader category of fundamental weighting schemes. Price weighting relies exclusively on nominal share price, which is unrelated to national output. Float-adjusted market-capitalization weighting instead relies on tradable share value, not a country-level economic statistic.
  163. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Negative or flat growth accompanied by excess capacity and firm exits. The declining stage of the industry life cycle is characterized by shrinking or stagnant demand, often due to technological substitution or changing consumer preferences, leaving the industry with excess production capacity and prompting weaker firms to exit. Growth well above the overall economy with abundant new entrants instead describes an earlier growth-stage industry, the opposite of decline.   164. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): equal weighting that ignores both share price and market value. Equal weighting assigns every constituent stock the identical weight at construction and each rebalancing date, regardless of its share price or total market capitalization, in contrast to schemes that scale weight with either of those factors. Market-capitalization weighting instead gives larger companies proportionally greater influence based on total market value. Price weighting gives higher-priced stocks greater influence based on nominal share price alone, neither of which produces identical starting weights across constituents.
  165. The correct answer is A ($33.33): Under the Gordon growth model, V0 = D1 ÷ (r − g). Substituting the given values gives $2.00 ÷ (0.10 − 0.04) = $2.00 ÷ 0.06 = $33.33. The model assumes the stock pays a growing dividend forever at a constant rate below the required return, so a smaller denominator (r minus g) directly produces a higher valuation.
  166. The correct answer is B ($39.38): The next dividend D1 must first be grown one period from D0, so D1 = $1.50 × 1.05 = $1.575. Applying the Gordon growth model, V0 = D1 ÷ (r − g) = $1.575 ÷ (0.09 − 0.05) = $1.575 ÷ 0.04 = $39.375, which rounds to $39.38. A common error is discounting D0 instead of D1, which understates the true intrinsic value.

Substituting gives 0.40 ÷ (0.11 − 0.06) = 0.40 ÷ 0.05 = 8.0. This formula follows directly from dividing the Gordon growth model's price by next year's expected earnings per share.

  169. The correct answer is A (10.0%): Rearranging the Gordon growth model, the required return equals the dividend yield plus the growth rate, r = D1 ÷ P0 + g. Substituting gives $2.00 ÷ $40.00 + 0.05 = 0.05 + 0.05 = 0.10, or 10.0%. This decomposition shows total expected return as the sum of dividend yield and capital appreciation.
  170. The correct answer is C ($21.90): Dividends grow at 20% for three years, giving D1 = $1.20, D2 = $1.44, and D3 = $1.728, then D4 = $1.728 × 1.05 = $1.8144 in the stable stage. The terminal value at year 3 is D4 ÷ (0.12 − 0.05) = $25.92, so total value is the present value of D1, D2, and (D3 + terminal value), all discounted at 12%, which sums to approximately $21.90. This two-stage structure captures both the high-growth years and the stable perpetuity that follows.
  171. The correct answer is C ($42.86): The constant growth FCFE model values equity as FCFE1 ÷ (r − g), where r is the required return on equity. Substituting gives $3.00 ÷ (0.11 − 0.04) = $3.00 ÷ 0.07 = $42.857, which rounds to $42.86. FCFE is used directly with the required return on equity rather than the WACC because it already reflects the financing effects available to shareholders.

Since firm value represents the claims of both debt and equity holders, equity value is found by subtracting the market value of debt, $833.33 million − $200 million = $633.33 million. The WACC is used because FCFF is available to all capital providers before financing effects.

Substituting gives 18 ÷ 9 = 2.0. A PEG ratio near 1.0 is often viewed as fair value relative to growth, so a ratio of 2.0 suggests the stock is priced richly relative to its forecast growth.

  176. The correct answer is A ($62.50): Nonconvertible preferred stock with a fixed dividend and no maturity is valued as a perpetuity, V0 = D ÷ r. Substituting gives $5.00 ÷ 0.08 = $62.50. This is a special case of the Gordon growth model where the growth rate is zero because the dividend never changes.
  177. The correct answer is B ($50.00): The market price implied by a trailing multiple equals trailing earnings per share multiplied by the trailing price to earnings ratio. Substituting gives $48.00 ÷ $3.20 = 15.0. Leading P/E is generally lower than trailing P/E for a growing company since the earnings figure in the denominator is larger.

This approach backs out the market's implied growth expectation from the observed price, dividend, and required return.

  181. A smaller positive denominator applied to an unchanged dividend produces a mathematically larger intrinsic value estimate. This relationship also explains why the model is highly sensitive to small changes in the assumed required return.
  182. The correct answer is A (growing dividends rapidly for a defined period before shifting to a stable long-run growth rate): A two-stage model explicitly forecasts an initial high-growth phase with its own dividend path, followed by a terminal value based on stable perpetual growth. This structure fits companies whose growth is expected to decelerate toward the economy's long-run rate rather than firms already in steady state. A single-stage Gordon growth model is instead appropriate for mature firms already growing at one constant rate.
  183. The correct answer is B (always positive even when the company reports a net operating loss): Sales figures remain positive as long as a company generates revenue, even during periods of negative net income, making the price to sales ratio usable when the price to earnings ratio is meaningless. Sales are also less distorted by accounting choices such as depreciation method or noncash impairment charges, which instead flow through the income statement. This makes price to sales a common tool for valuing early-stage or currently unprofitable companies.
  184. The correct answer is C (net borrowing activity and interest expense net of its related tax shield): Free cash flow to equity starts from free cash flow to the firm but subtracts after-tax interest expense and adds net borrowing, isolating the cash flow available specifically to common shareholders. Free cash flow to the firm, by contrast, is calculated before any financing effects and represents cash available to all capital providers. This distinction determines which discount rate applies, the cost of equity for FCFE versus the WACC for FCFF.
  185. A smaller denominator applied to the same payout ratio produces a larger justified multiple. This is why fast-growing companies typically trade at higher P/E multiples than slow-growing ones, all else equal.
  186. The correct answer is B (13.13): The justified trailing price to earnings multiple equals payout × (1 + g) ÷ (r − g). Substituting gives 0.50 × 1.05 ÷ (0.09 − 0.05) = 0.525 ÷ 0.04 = 13.125, which rounds to 13.13. The (1 + g) term adjusts the current payout ratio forward one period since the trailing multiple is based on this year's already-reported earnings.

This multiple is often preferred for companies with volatile or negative earnings, since sales figures are less prone to becoming negative or distorted by accounting adjustments.
  188. The correct answer is A (7.0%): The sustainable growth rate equals the retention ratio multiplied by return on equity, g = b × ROE. Substituting gives 0.70 × 0.10 = 0.07, or 7.0%. This figure represents the maximum growth rate the firm can sustain using only internally generated equity capital, holding the retention ratio and ROE constant.

The result confirms that as the spread between the required return and growth rate narrows, the same dividend supports a proportionally higher valuation.

This calculation reveals the constant growth rate the market is implicitly pricing into the current share price.

The terminal value at year 2 is D3 ÷ (0.10 − 0.04) = $45.847, so total value is the present value of D1 and (D2 plus terminal value) discounted at 10%, which sums to approximately $42.17. This two-stage approach separates the temporary high-growth phase from the perpetual stable-growth phase that follows.

Analysts typically switch to alternative multiples such as price to sales or price to book for such companies. Stable, positive earnings growth, by contrast, is exactly the setting where the P/E multiple works well.
  197. This makes the price to sales ratio one of the few multiples that can meaningfully be applied and compared across peer companies in this situation. The price to earnings ratio is unusable here because reported earnings are negative or not yet meaningful.

The correct answer is B (all dividends expected to be paid from that point forward under the stable growth assumption): The terminal value is itself a Gordon growth model applied at the end of the explicit forecast horizon, capturing the present value, as of that future date, of the entire remaining perpetuity of stable-growth dividends. It is then discounted back to today alongside the explicitly forecast dividends from the high-growth stage. This structure lets the model value an indefinite dividend stream without forecasting every future period individually.

This confirms that a smaller spread between the required return and the growth rate raises the present value assigned to a given dividend stream.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (1.75): The price to sales ratio equals price per share divided by sales per share. Substituting gives $63.00 ÷ $36.00 = 1.75. This multiple allows comparison across companies regardless of differences in profitability or capital structure, since it is based on the top line of the income statement.
  2. The correct answer is C (estimates value by comparing a company's multiples to those of similar firms or the market): Relative valuation, such as applying price to earnings or price to sales multiples, benchmarks a company's pricing against comparable firms or a broader index rather than discounting the company's own projected cash flows. Intrinsic models such as the dividend discount model instead derive value directly from forecast future dividends discounted at a required return. The two approaches can produce different value estimates when peer companies are themselves mispriced.
  3. The correct answer is A (the required rate of return must exceed the assumed constant growth rate): The Gordon growth model formula, D1 ÷ (r − g), is mathematically undefined or produces a nonsensical negative value if the growth rate equals or exceeds the required return. This means the model cannot be applied to firms assumed to grow forever faster than the required return on equity. Combined with its high sensitivity to the growth rate assumption, this constraint is one of the model's key practical limitations.
  4. The correct answer is C ($119.48): FCFF grows 8% for two years, giving FCFF1 = $8.00 and FCFF2 = $8.64, then FCFF3 = $8.64 × 1.03 = $8.8992 in the stable stage. The terminal value at year 2 is FCFF3 ÷ (0.10 − 0.03) = $127.13, so total firm value per share is the present value of FCFF1 and (FCFF2 plus terminal value) discounted at the 10% WACC, which sums to approximately $119.48. This two-stage FCFF approach values the entire firm before any adjustment for outstanding debt.
  5. The correct answer is B (a free cash flow to equity model, which values the cash actually available to shareholders): When a company has never paid a dividend or uses buybacks and reinvestment instead of cash dividends, the dividend discount model has no usable input and cannot capture the true value delivered to shareholders. A free cash flow to equity model instead values the cash generated that is actually available for distribution, regardless of whether it is paid out as a dividend. This makes FCFE the more reliable intrinsic approach for firms with an unconventional or nonexistent dividend policy.
  6. The correct answer is A (3.0): The PEG ratio equals the price to earnings multiple divided by the expected earnings growth rate expressed as a whole number. Substituting gives 24 ÷ 8 = 3.0. A PEG ratio this far above 1.0 suggests the stock's multiple is elevated relative to its forecast earnings growth rate.
  7. The correct answer is B (less than the required rate of return on equity used to discount the dividends): The stable-stage growth rate feeds into a Gordon growth model terminal value calculation, D ÷ (r − g), which becomes undefined or nonsensical if growth equals or exceeds the required return. Analysts therefore constrain the terminal growth assumption to a modest, sustainable rate, often close to long-run economic growth, that remains safely below the required return. Violating this constraint would produce an infinite or negative terminal value with no economic meaning.
  8. The correct answer is A (the weak form, in which prices fully reflect all past market trading information): Weak-form efficiency asserts that current prices already incorporate all historical price and volume data, so technical analysis using charting patterns cannot consistently produce abnormal returns. Semi-strong form extends this to all publicly available information, including financial statements, while strong form adds private or insider information. Because the analyst's claim is limited specifically to historical trading data, it describes weak-form efficiency rather than the broader semi-strong or strong forms.
  9. The correct answer is A (technical analysis based on historical price and volume patterns will not reliably beat the market): Weak-form efficiency states only that current prices reflect all past trading data, so patterns in that data cannot be exploited for abnormal profits. It says nothing about whether prices reflect other public information such as financial statements, so it does not by itself imply fundamental analysis is useless. It also says nothing about private information, so a claim about insider trading goes beyond what weak-form efficiency alone establishes.
  10. The correct answer is B (semi-strong form efficiency, concerning all publicly available information): Semi-strong form efficiency holds that prices adjust rapidly and fully to all publicly released information, including earnings reports, dividend announcements, and stock splits, leaving no exploitable delay in price adjustment. Testing whether prices react immediately to such public disclosures is the standard event-study methodology used to assess this specific form. Weak-form tests instead focus narrowly on historical price and volume patterns, and strong-form tests require examining private information, neither of which matches this study's design.
  11. The correct answer is C (fundamental analysis using public information will not consistently generate abnormal risk-adjusted returns): If prices already reflect all publicly available information, then analyzing that same public information, including financial statements, cannot reliably identify mispriced securities. This is a stronger implication than weak-form efficiency provides, since semi-strong form covers public fundamental data in addition to historical trading data. Insider information remains a separate category under strong-form efficiency and is not addressed by the semi-strong form assumption.
  12. The correct answer is B (strong-form efficiency, since it requires prices to reflect all private information): Strong-form efficiency is the only form of market efficiency that requires prices to fully incorporate both public and private, or insider, information. An insider consistently profiting from undisclosed information directly contradicts this requirement, since that information had not yet been reflected in the market price. Weak-form and semi-strong form efficiency make claims only about historical and public information respectively, so this evidence does not necessarily contradict either of them on its own.
  13. The correct answer is A (strong form, because insiders profit from information not reflected in current prices): Strong-form efficiency requires that all information, including private material nonpublic information, already be reflected in security prices, leaving no opportunity for abnormal profit. Insiders earning abnormal returns from undisclosed information directly violates this requirement, which is why regulators treat such trading as evidence against strict strong-form efficiency. Weak-form and semi-strong form efficiency address only historical prices and public information respectively, and insider trading based on private information does not directly test either of those narrower claims.
  14. The correct answer is C (calendar-based market anomaly that challenges the efficient market hypothesis): A persistent, predictable seasonal pattern such as elevated January returns for small-cap stocks is exactly the kind of exploitable regularity that an efficient market should arbitrage away, making it a documented anomaly rather than evidence of efficiency. Because the pattern survives risk adjustment, it cannot be explained as simple compensation for known risk factors. Such calendar anomalies are widely cited as empirical challenges to strict forms of the efficient market hypothesis.
  15. The correct answer is C (size anomaly, reflecting outperformance of smaller-capitalization companies): The size anomaly refers specifically to the historical tendency of small-capitalization stocks to outperform large-capitalization stocks on a risk-adjusted basis, even after controlling for measures like beta. The value anomaly instead concerns outperformance based on valuation multiples such as price-to-book, and momentum concerns outperformance based on recent price trends, neither of which is the distinguishing factor described here. Because the pattern is explicitly tied to market capitalization, it is classified as the size effect.
  16. The correct answer is B (value effect, driven by outperformance of relatively cheap stocks): The value effect describes the empirical tendency of stocks with low valuation multiples, such as low price-to-book or price-to-earnings ratios, to outperform higher-multiple stocks on a risk-adjusted basis over long horizons. The size effect instead concerns market-capitalization differences, and the January effect concerns seasonal timing, neither of which matches a comparison based on valuation ratios. Because the pattern is driven by relative cheapness rather than firm size or calendar timing, it is classified as the value effect.
  17. The correct answer is A (momentum anomaly, where recent relative performance tends to persist): The momentum anomaly refers to the documented tendency for stocks with strong recent performance to continue outperforming, and for weak recent performers to continue underperforming, over intermediate horizons of several months to a year. This is distinct from the overreaction anomaly, which instead describes reversal rather than continuation of returns over longer periods. Because the strategy described buys recent winners and sells recent losers and profits from continuation, it is best classified as exploiting momentum.
  18. The correct answer is B (post-earnings-announcement drift, reflecting delayed incorporation of earnings surprises): Post-earnings-announcement drift describes the empirical finding that stock prices continue moving in the direction of an earnings surprise for weeks or months after the announcement, rather than adjusting instantly as strict semi-strong efficiency would predict. This delayed adjustment allows a trading strategy that follows earnings surprises to earn abnormal returns, which is a well-documented anomaly in the literature. It is distinct from the size effect and the January effect, which relate to firm size and calendar timing rather than earnings-driven price adjustment.
  19. The correct answer is C (overreaction to salient news, later corrected as prices revert): A pattern in which extreme past losers subsequently outperform and extreme past winners subsequently underperform is consistent with investors initially overreacting to salient news and pushing prices too far, followed by a corrective reversal toward fundamental value. This differs from underreaction, which would instead predict continuation rather than reversal of the initial extreme performance. Because the pattern shows a subsequent reversal rather than persistence, overreaction followed by correction is the best behavioral explanation.
  20. The correct answer is A (it is unlikely to consistently outperform a comparable passive index fund): If a market is semi-strong form efficient, publicly available information cannot be used to reliably identify mispriced securities, so active management based on that same public information should not consistently beat a passive benchmark, especially once fees and trading costs are subtracted. This makes the claim of consistent outperformance inconsistent with the stated evidence of efficiency. Restricting strategy exclusively to historical price charts is also unsupported, since semi-strong efficiency already implies technical analysis of past prices is similarly unlikely to add value.
  21. The correct answer is B (the efficient market hypothesis's assumption of rational, unbiased pricing): The efficient market hypothesis generally assumes that investor behavior, in aggregate, leads prices to reflect fundamental value without persistent systematic bias. Behavioral finance directly challenges this assumption by documenting recurring psychological biases that can cause prices to deviate from fundamental value for extended periods rather than being quickly corrected. This critique is specifically about pricing rationality and efficiency, not about the risk-free rate assumption in the capital asset pricing model or the growth rate assumption in the dividend discount model.
  22. The correct answer is A (event study, a standard method for testing semi-strong form efficiency): An event study examines abnormal returns around a specific, dated public announcement to measure how quickly and completely prices incorporate that new information, which is the standard empirical approach for testing semi-strong form efficiency. This differs from a regulatory compliance audit, which focuses on detecting improper trading rather than measuring price adjustment speed. It also differs from a technical indicator backtest, which evaluates historical price-pattern trading rules and is instead associated with testing weak-form efficiency.
  23. The correct answer is C (primary market, where newly issued securities are sold to investors): The primary market is where a company sells newly created securities directly to investors for the first time, with the proceeds of the sale flowing to the issuing company to raise new capital. The secondary market, by contrast, involves trading of already-issued securities among investors, with no new capital going to the issuer. A dealer market describes a trading mechanism based on dealer quotes rather than the distinction between newly issued and previously issued securities, so it does not describe this transaction.
  24. The correct answer is B (secondary market, which provides liquidity for previously issued securities): The secondary market is where investors trade securities that have already been issued, allowing holders to convert their positions into cash and providing overall market liquidity, without directing any new proceeds to the issuing company. The primary market instead involves the original sale of newly issued securities, which channels capital directly to the issuer, unlike the ongoing trading described here. A private placement market involves securities sold directly to a limited group of investors outside public trading, which also does not match this scenario of ongoing exchange trading.
  25. The correct answer is B (rights offering that preserves existing shareholders' proportional ownership): A rights offering grants existing shareholders the option to purchase additional shares at a discounted price in proportion to their current holdings, allowing them to maintain their percentage ownership if they choose to exercise the right. This differs from an initial public offering, which involves a company's first sale of shares to the general public rather than to existing shareholders specifically. It also differs from a private placement, which is sold to a small group of typically institutional investors rather than offered proportionally to existing public shareholders.
  26. The correct answer is A (firm commitment underwriting, where the bank bears the placement risk): In a firm commitment underwriting, the investment bank purchases the entire issue from the issuer at an agreed price and then resells the shares to the public, absorbing the risk that some shares may not sell at the expected price. This differs from a best-efforts underwriting, in which the bank only attempts to sell the shares without guaranteeing the issuer a fixed amount of proceeds. It also differs from a private placement, which involves selling securities directly to a small group of investors rather than a broad public resale by an underwriter.
  27. The correct answer is C (private placement sold directly to a limited group of investors): A private placement involves selling newly issued securities directly to a small number of typically institutional or accredited investors, without a public prospectus or exchange listing, which allows the issuer to avoid many public disclosure requirements. This differs from a seasoned equity offering, which is a public sale of additional shares by an already-listed company on a public exchange. It also differs from a secondary market transaction, which involves trading of already-issued shares between existing investors rather than a new capital-raising sale by the company.
  28. The correct answer is A (order-driven market, where trades are matched directly against the order book): In an order-driven market, investor buy and sell orders are matched automatically according to price and time priority rules through a central limit order book, without a dealer setting intermediary quotes. This differs from a quote-driven market, in which a dealer posts bid and ask prices and investors trade against those dealer quotes rather than directly against each other. It also differs from the primary market, which concerns the initial issuance of new securities rather than the trading mechanism used for existing securities.
  29. The correct answer is B (quote-driven dealer market, where the dealer trades from its own inventory): In a quote-driven or dealer market, a designated dealer posts bid and ask prices and stands ready to buy or sell from its own inventory, providing continuous liquidity to investors who trade directly against those quotes. This differs from an order-driven market, in which investor orders are matched against each other through a central limit order book without a dealer intermediary. It also differs from a single-session auction market, which aggregates orders for a periodic call rather than providing continuous dealer-quoted liquidity.
  30. The correct answer is C (bid-ask spread, the dealer's compensation for market-making): The bid-ask spread is the difference between the price at which a dealer is willing to buy a security and the price at which it is willing to sell, and it compensates the dealer for providing immediacy while bearing inventory risk and the risk of trading against better-informed counterparties. This is unrelated to a dividend yield, which reflects expected cash distributions to shareholders rather than a dealer's trading quotes. It is also unrelated to a primary market issuance discount, which concerns the pricing of a new securities offering rather than ongoing secondary market dealer quotes.
  31. The correct answer is C (market order, which executes promptly at the prevailing market price): A market order instructs the broker to execute the trade immediately at the best available current price, prioritizing speed of execution over any specific price target. This differs from a limit order, which sets a maximum or minimum acceptable price and may not execute immediately, or at all, if that price is not available. It also differs from a stop order, which remains inactive until a specified trigger price is reached, unlike a market order that is intended for immediate execution.
  32. The correct answer is A (limit order, which sets a minimum acceptable execution price for the sale): A limit order on a sale specifies the lowest price the investor will accept, and the order will not execute below that price even though this may delay or prevent execution. This differs from a market order, which is designed to execute immediately at whatever price is currently available rather than waiting for a specified price target. It also differs from a primary offering, which concerns the initial sale of newly issued shares by the company rather than a secondary market trading instruction from an investor.
  33. The correct answer is B (a dark pool, an alternative trading system with limited pre-trade transparency): A dark pool is an alternative trading system that allows large institutional orders to be matched with limited or no pre-trade disclosure of order size and price, reducing the market-impact cost that would otherwise result from other traders reacting to a visible large order. This differs from a traditional lit exchange, where order book information is publicly displayed before execution, which is precisely the transparency the institutional investor wants to avoid. It also differs from the primary market, which involves the original issuance of new securities rather than the private negotiation of existing shares.
  34. The correct answer is A (liquidity, reflecting the ease of trading without moving the price): Market liquidity refers to the ability to buy or sell a security quickly, at a price close to its recently observed value, and without incurring substantial transaction costs from the trade itself. This differs from market efficiency, which concerns how completely and quickly prices reflect available information rather than the ease of executing a trade. It also differs from the concept of a primary issuance calendar, which relates to scheduled new securities offerings rather than the trading characteristics of already-issued shares.
  35. The correct answer is C (block trade, a large transaction negotiated outside normal order flow): A block trade is a large transaction, well above normal trading volume, that is typically negotiated privately through a broker-dealer network rather than being routed entirely through the public limit order book, in order to minimize price disruption. This differs from a rights offering, which involves a company issuing new shares to existing shareholders rather than an investor selling an existing large position. It also differs from an initial public offering, which is the first public sale of a company's shares rather than a secondary market sale of previously issued shares.
  36. The correct answer is B (over-the-counter market operating through a dealer quotation network): An over-the-counter market involves securities trading through a decentralized network of dealers who post electronic quotes, rather than through a centralized exchange with formal listing requirements and a physical trading floor. This differs from an initial public offering, which concerns the original sale of new shares rather than the ongoing venue where existing shares subsequently trade. It also differs from an auction market conducted on an exchange floor, which relies on a centralized location for matching orders rather than a dispersed dealer network.
  37. The correct answer is A (Porter's five forces framework for analyzing industry structure): Porter's five forces framework systematically evaluates an industry's competitive intensity and long-run profit potential by examining rivalry among existing competitors, buyer power, supplier power, threat of new entrants, and threat of substitutes. This differs from the industry life cycle model, which classifies industries by growth stage rather than by these five specific competitive pressures. It also differs from the dividend discount model, which is a valuation tool for estimating intrinsic stock value rather than a framework for assessing industry competitive structure.
  38. The correct answer is C (the threat of new entrants challenging established industry participants): High capital requirements, regulatory hurdles, and strong brand loyalty are classic barriers to entry that make it difficult for new firms to enter an industry, directly reducing the threat of new entrants as one of Porter's five forces. These same factors do not directly describe supplier bargaining power, which instead concerns the leverage input providers hold over industry firms. They also do not directly describe rivalry among current incumbents, which concerns competitive intensity among firms already operating in the industry rather than the difficulty faced by potential new entrants.
  39. The correct answer is B (bargaining power of buyers relative to the manufacturer): When a small number of large buyers account for most of a seller's sales volume, those buyers gain significant leverage to negotiate favorable prices and terms, which is the defining characteristic of high buyer bargaining power in Porter's framework. This differs from the threat of substitutes, which concerns alternative products satisfying the same need rather than the concentration of the buyer base. It also differs from supplier bargaining power, which describes leverage held by input providers over the manufacturer rather than leverage held by the manufacturer's customers.
  40. The correct answer is A (bargaining power of suppliers over firms in the buying industry): When a single supplier controls nearly all economically viable production of a critical input and no ready substitute exists, that supplier holds significant leverage over the pricing and terms it can impose on firms that depend on the input, which is the defining characteristic of high supplier bargaining power. This differs from buyer bargaining power, which instead describes leverage held by customers over the supplying industry rather than leverage held by the supplier itself. It also differs from rivalry among existing competitors, which concerns competitive intensity among firms in the buying industry rather than their dependence on a concentrated supplier.
  41. The correct answer is C (threat of substitute products satisfying the same underlying customer need): Internet-based calling and messaging applications serve the same core communication function as landline telephones at a lower cost, making them close substitutes rather than direct rivals within the same industry or suppliers to it. Porter's five forces framework identifies this substitution risk as a distinct competitive force that can erode an industry's pricing power and long-run profitability even without any new direct competitor appearing. Because the pressure comes from a different product category meeting the same customer need, it is not classified as rivalry among existing landline competitors or as supplier bargaining power.
  42. The correct answer is C (rivalry among existing competitors within the industry): Slow industry growth, undifferentiated products, high fixed costs that pressure firms to maintain volume through price competition, and high exit barriers from specialized assets are all classic drivers of intense rivalry among firms already competing in a mature industry. This differs from the threat of new entrants, which the high fixed-cost and specialized-asset conditions described would actually tend to discourage rather than encourage. It also differs from buyer bargaining power, since undifferentiated products in this context are described as a driver of price competition among sellers rather than as a source of leverage specifically attributed to buyers.
  43. The correct answer is B (the growth stage, marked by rapid expansion and rising competition): The growth stage of the industry life cycle is characterized by rapid sales expansion, significant product innovation, and an influx of new entrants seeking to capture early profits before industry standards and a dominant product design have fully stabilized. This differs from the mature stage, which instead features slower growth and more stable, established market shares among fewer competitors. It also differs from the decline stage, which is marked by shrinking demand and firms exiting the industry rather than the rapid expansion and evolving standards described here.
  44. The correct answer is A (cost leadership strategy focused on being the low-cost producer): A cost leadership strategy centers on achieving the lowest production and distribution costs in the industry through scale economies and operational efficiency, allowing the firm to price competitively while still earning acceptable margins. This differs from a differentiation strategy, which instead competes on unique product features, quality, or service rather than on being the lowest-cost producer. It also differs from a focus strategy, which targets a narrow market segment rather than describing the broad cost-based approach outlined in the scenario.
  45. The correct answer is A (differentiation strategy built on unique, hard-to-replicate value): A differentiation strategy competes by offering unique, hard-to-replicate product features, quality, or service that justify premium pricing and build customer loyalty, rather than by competing primarily on the lowest available price. This differs from a cost leadership strategy, which instead focuses on achieving the lowest production costs to support competitive pricing rather than premium, differentiated positioning. It also has nothing to do with a primary market strategy, which concerns how a company raises capital through new securities issuance rather than how it competes for customers.
  46. The correct answer is B (a fragmented industry structure with no single dominant competitor): A fragmented industry consists of many small firms, none of which controls a large enough market share to dictate industry pricing, resulting in limited individual pricing power and intense localized competition. This differs from a concentrated oligopoly, in which a small number of large firms collectively hold significant market share and pricing influence. It also differs from a natural monopoly, which is characterized by a single dominant firm protected by extremely high barriers to entry rather than by many small, competing firms.
  47. The correct answer is C (preferred stock, with a senior but non-voting equity claim): Preferred stock typically pays a fixed dividend that must be paid before any dividend to common shareholders, generally carries no voting rights, and holds a claim on assets senior to common stock but subordinate to all creditors in liquidation. This differs from common stock, which carries voting rights and only a residual claim after preferred shareholders and creditors are paid. It also differs from a corporate bond, which represents a fixed contractual debt obligation with a legally enforceable claim ahead of all equity, unlike the equity classification described here.
  48. The correct answer is A (American depositary receipts, representing indirect ownership of foreign shares): American depositary receipts are negotiable certificates issued by a US bank that holds the underlying foreign shares in custody, allowing US investors to gain indirect exposure to a foreign company while trading in US dollars on a US exchange without directly settling in the foreign market. This differs from a rights offering, which grants existing shareholders the option to purchase additional new shares rather than describing a cross-border custody and trading mechanism. It also differs from a block trade, which is a large privately negotiated transaction in existing shares rather than a depositary structure for foreign equity exposure.
  49. The correct answer is B (public equity from private equity as forms of ownership): Public equity consists of shares listed and continuously traded on an exchange with frequent price transparency, while private equity consists of ownership stakes in companies that are not publicly listed, typically offering limited liquidity and less frequent valuation. This distinction is unrelated to weak-form versus semi-strong form market efficiency, which concerns the speed and completeness of price adjustment to information rather than public versus private listing status. It is also unrelated to the distinction between preferred and common stock, which concerns priority of claims and voting rights rather than public listing versus private ownership.
  50. The correct answer is C (common stock, carrying voting rights and a residual claim): Common stock grants shareholders voting rights on major corporate matters such as electing directors and approving mergers, along with a residual claim on assets and earnings after creditors and preferred shareholders have been satisfied. This differs from preferred stock, which typically carries no voting rights and instead receives a fixed dividend with priority over common stock. It also differs from a depositary receipt, which represents indirect ownership of foreign shares through a custodial structure rather than describing the voting and residual claim characteristics of a domestic equity class.

  179.
