  180. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The inverse relationship between bond price and yield: Because a bond's price is the present value of its fixed future cash flows discounted at the prevailing yield, an increase in yield lowers the present value of those cash flows and thus lowers price, while a decrease in yield raises price, producing an inverse relationship. This relationship is not positive, contradicting the second option. It also applies broadly to coupon-paying bonds, not merely to zero-coupon government bonds as the first option suggests.
  181. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A forward commitment, since both counterparties are obligated to transact at the agreed terms. Both the buyer and seller must complete the transaction at the agreed price and date regardless of how the underlying's price moves. This symmetric obligation is what distinguishes forward commitments (forwards, futures, swaps) from contingent claims like options. A contingent claim would instead give one party the choice, not the obligation, to transact.
  182. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): An over-the-counter market, where terms are privately negotiated bilaterally. Bilateral negotiation of custom terms without a central clearinghouse guaranteeing performance is the defining feature of OTC derivatives markets. Exchange-traded markets instead impose standardized contract terms and central clearing to promote liquidity and reduce counterparty risk. Since no exchange or clearinghouse is involved here, the transaction is clearly an OTC contract.
  183. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Risk management, since the farmer transfers price risk to another party. By locking in a selling price ahead of harvest, the farmer eliminates uncertainty about future revenue, transferring the price risk to the counterparty on the other side of the contract. This is a textbook hedging application of derivatives, not speculation, since the farmer already holds the underlying exposure (the crop) and is reducing rather than adding risk. Arbitrage would instead require exploiting a mispricing for a riskless profit, which is not described here.
  184. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A $7 gain, since the long position profits when the spot price exceeds the forward price. The payoff to a long forward position equals the spot price at expiration minus the forward price, or $57 minus $50, which equals a $7 gain. The long holder benefits because they can buy the underlying at the lower contracted forward price of $50 while it is worth $57 in the market. Option B incorrectly treats the entire forward price as the gain rather than isolating the price differential.
  185. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): It increases contract liquidity by making positions easier to trade and offset. Standardized contract size, expiration, and quality mean any market participant's futures position is fungible with any other's, so traders can close out positions easily by taking an offsetting trade. This liquidity benefit is separate from delivery, which most futures positions never reach since they are closed out before expiration. Standardization also does not eliminate price risk, since futures prices still move with the underlying.
  186. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A gain of $2,500, based on the $2.50 per barrel increase times 1,000 barrels. Mark-to-market gain for a long futures position equals the change in settlement price multiplied by contract size, or ($72.50 minus $70.00) times 1,000 barrels, which equals $2,500. A long position gains when the futures price rises, so this is correctly a gain, and the size of the contract (1,000 barrels) must be applied to the per-barrel price change to get the dollar amount.
  187. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): An exchange of fixed-rate for floating-rate interest payments on a common notional amount. In a plain vanilla interest rate swap, both counterparties calculate their payments off the same notional principal, with one side paying a fixed rate and the other paying a rate that resets periodically based on a reference rate. Because both parties reference the identical currency and notional, the principal itself is never exchanged, only the periodic net interest difference. This distinguishes it from a currency swap, where notional principal typically is exchanged.
  188. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Currency swaps typically exchange notional principal amounts at both initiation and maturity. Because the two legs of a currency swap are denominated in different currencies, exchanging the notional principal is necessary to actually deliver the intended currency exposure to each counterparty, unlike a single-currency interest rate swap where identical-currency principal exchange serves no purpose. This principal exchange occurs both at the start of the swap and again, in reverse, at maturity. Periodic interest payments still occur on each leg throughout the swap's life.
  189. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A contingent claim, since the payoff depends on a future contingent decision. An instrument granting the right but not the obligation to transact is an option, the classic example of a contingent claim, whose payoff is asymmetric and depends on whether the holder chooses to exercise. Forward commitments obligate both parties symmetrically with no such discretion, and futures contracts are simply exchange-traded forward commitments with daily settlement, neither of which describes an optional right to exercise.
  190. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A loss of $6, since the short must deliver an asset now worth more than the forward price. The payoff to a short forward position equals the forward price minus the spot price at expiration, or $40 minus $46, which equals a loss of $6. The short holder is obligated to sell at the lower contracted price of $40 even though the asset is worth $46 in the market, creating an opportunity loss equal to that $6 difference. Option B incorrectly reverses the direction of the short position's exposure to rising prices.
  191. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Price discovery, since futures prices reveal the market's consensus expectation of future value. Because futures prices aggregate the views and trading activity of many participants, they serve as a visible, continuously updated estimate of the market's collective expectation for the underlying's future value. This informational role, known as price discovery, is distinct from risk transfer, which describes how individual counterparties reallocate exposure between themselves rather than how prices convey market-wide information.
  192. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): It substantially reduces counterparty credit risk by guaranteeing each side's performance. The clearinghouse becomes the legal counterparty to both the buyer and the seller through novation, so each trader faces the clearinghouse rather than the original counterparty, and daily margin settlement limits the size of any potential default. This central guarantee is the core credit-risk-reduction function of a clearinghouse, distinct from setting prices or negotiating customized terms, which it does not do.
  193. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Forward contracts carry greater counterparty credit risk than comparable futures contracts. Without daily mark-to-market settlement or a clearinghouse guarantee, unrealized gains and losses on a forward contract can build up substantially over the contract's life, exposing each party to the risk that the other will default at expiration when the amount owed is largest. Futures contracts mitigate this by settling gains and losses daily through margin accounts backed by a central clearinghouse.
  194. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A series of forward rate agreements, each settling on a separate future date. Each exchange of fixed for floating payments within an interest rate swap can be viewed as an individual forward-starting agreement settling at its own future date, so the whole swap is conceptually a strip of sequential forward contracts. This decomposition is useful for valuation, since each implied forward agreement can be priced and discounted separately using the term structure of interest rates.
  195. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A loss of $1,500, since the short position loses value as gold prices rise. Mark-to-market change for a short futures position equals the negative of the change in settlement price multiplied by contract size, or negative ($1,915 minus $1,900) times 100 ounces, which equals a $1,500 loss. A short position profits when prices fall and loses when prices rise, the opposite of a long position, so the $15 per ounce increase produces a loss rather than a gain for this trader.
  196. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Derivatives can derive value from virtually any measurable underlying variable, not just securities. The wide range of underlyings, spanning commodities, interest rates, credit events, and even weather indices, illustrates that a derivative simply requires an observable and contractible variable, not a traditional tradable security. Derivatives are not restricted to exchange-listed underlyings, since many OTC derivatives reference non-traded variables, and many derivatives, including most financial futures, settle in cash rather than requiring physical delivery.
  197. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The over-the-counter market, since dealers can customize terms to match specific client needs. Nonstandard notional amounts, unusual maturities, and bespoke reference rates cannot be accommodated on an exchange, which only offers standardized listed contracts, so an OTC dealer negotiating directly with the pension fund is required. This flexibility is the defining trade-off of OTC markets against the greater liquidity and reduced credit risk offered by standardized exchange-traded contracts.
  198. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A gain of $30,000, since the company sold euros above the lower prevailing spot rate. The payoff to a short forward position equals the forward price minus the spot price, multiplied by the notional, or ($1.10 minus $1.07) times 1,000,000 euros, which equals a $30,000 gain. The company locked in the right to sell euros at $1.10 even though the market rate fell to $1.07, so it captured the $0.03 per euro price differential as a gain on its short forward position.
  199. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A net payment of $70,000, reflecting the 0.7% fixed-over-floating rate differential on notional. The net cash flow equals the difference between the fixed and floating rates, or 4.5% minus 3.8%, which equals 0.7%, multiplied by the $10,000,000 notional, giving $70,000. Because the counterparty pays fixed and receives floating, and the fixed rate exceeds the floating rate this period, the counterparty owes a net payment rather than receiving one, ruling out option B.
  200. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Arbitrage, since the trader exploits a temporary mispricing to earn a riskless profit. Simultaneously selling the overpriced futures contract and buying the underlying asset locks in a profit from the price discrepancy with no net market exposure, the defining feature of arbitrage. This differs from speculation, where a trader deliberately takes on directional exposure hoping prices move favorably, and from hedging, which reduces exposure on an existing position rather than exploiting a mispricing.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): It protects the clearinghouse and counterparties against the risk of trader default. Initial and maintenance margin requirements ensure that traders have sufficient collateral posted to cover potential losses, and the requirement to top up the account when the balance falls below the maintenance level limits the exposure the clearinghouse takes on from any single trader. This margin system works alongside, not in place of, daily settlement, since margin calls are triggered precisely because of daily mark-to-market changes.
  2. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Forward commitments obligate both parties, while contingent claims give one party a choice to exercise. Forward commitments, such as forwards, futures, and swaps, create a symmetric obligation for both counterparties to transact regardless of price movement, while contingent claims, such as options, give the holder a right that may or may not be exercised depending on how prices move. Daily mark-to-market settlement is a feature specific to futures contracts, not a distinguishing trait of contingent claims generally, and both instrument types can trade OTC or on exchanges.
  3. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): 8,000,000 euros, calculated by dividing the dollar notional by the $1.25 spot exchange rate. Converting the USD 10,000,000 notional into euros at the spot rate of $1.25 per euro requires dividing by the exchange rate, giving 10,000,000 divided by 1.25, which equals 8,000,000 euros. This initial notional exchange establishes the principal amounts each counterparty will pay interest on and will exchange back, in reverse, at the swap's maturity.
  4. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A loss of $25, since the long must pay more than the bond's current market value. The payoff to a long forward position equals the spot price at expiration minus the forward price, or $955 minus $980, which equals a loss of $25. The long holder is obligated to buy the bond at the higher contracted forward price of $980 even though it is only worth $955 in the market, resulting in an opportunity loss equal to that $25 differential rather than a gain.
  5. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Greater market liquidity, since standardized terms make offsetting positions easy to arrange. Because every futures contract on a given underlying has identical terms, any trader can offset an existing position by trading with any other market participant rather than needing to renegotiate terms bilaterally, which deepens liquidity. This standardization does not remove margin requirements, which remain mandatory on every futures position, and most contracts are closed out before expiration rather than settled through physical delivery.
  6. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Since both legs are in the same currency, exchanging identical principal amounts serves no purpose. Because both the fixed-rate and floating-rate payments are calculated in the same currency and on the same notional, exchanging that identical principal amount between counterparties would simply offset itself with no net economic effect. The notional is therefore used only as a reference amount for calculating each period's interest payments, not physically exchanged, which is why interest rate swap settlements involve only the net interest difference.
  7. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Derivatives provide efficient risk exposure or transfer without transacting in the underlying itself. Because a derivative's value is linked to an underlying asset without requiring ownership of that asset, investors can gain or shed exposure with less capital and often lower transaction costs than trading the underlying directly. This efficiency does not guarantee superior returns or eliminate risk, since derivatives still carry market risk tied to the underlying's price movements, only the mechanism of gaining that exposure is more efficient.
  8. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): A net receipt of $30,000, reflecting the 0.6% fixed-over-floating rate differential on the notional. The net cash flow equals the difference between the fixed and floating rates, or 5.2% minus 4.6%, which equals 0.6%, multiplied by the $5,000,000 notional, giving $30,000. Because the counterparty receives fixed and pays floating, and the fixed rate exceeds the floating rate this period, the counterparty is owed a net receipt rather than making a payment, ruling out option A.
  9. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Daily settlement prevents large unrealized gains and losses from accumulating over the contract's life. By requiring gains and losses to be realized and settled through margin accounts each trading day, futures contracts prevent the buildup of large unsettled exposure that a forward contract can accumulate until expiration. This daily settlement does not eliminate liquidity risk entirely, since traders must still be able to meet margin calls, and it has no bearing on whether physical delivery of the underlying occurs.
  10. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A contingent claim, since the protection buyer's payoff depends on a contingent credit event. A credit default swap pays out only if a specified credit event occurs, making its payoff asymmetric and dependent on a future contingency rather than guaranteed, the defining characteristic of a contingent claim. This distinguishes it from forward commitments, which create symmetric obligations for both parties regardless of any triggering event, and it is an OTC instrument rather than an exchange-traded futures contract.
  11. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): At expiration the call's payoff equals max(S − X, 0) = max($58.00 − $50.00, 0) = $8.00. Subtracting the $3.00 premium paid gives a net profit of $8.00 − $3.00 = $5.00. Because the stock price exceeds the strike price the call finishes in-the-money and is worth exercising rather than allowing it to expire worthless.
  12. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A put option is in-the-money only when the stock price is below the strike price, since the holder profits from selling at the higher strike. Here the stock price of $45.00 exceeds the $40.00 strike, so immediate exercise would produce a negative payoff and is never optimal. The put therefore has zero intrinsic value and is classified as out-of-the-money.
  13. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The put's exercise value equals max(X − S, 0) = max($70.00 − $65.00, 0) = $5.00, which the writer must pay out upon assignment. Netting this $5.00 payoff obligation against the $4.00 premium collected gives $4.00 − $5.00 = −$1.00. The short put writer therefore incurs a net loss of $1.00 on the position.
  14. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Intrinsic value for a call equals max(S − X, 0) = max($55.00 − $50.00, 0) = $5.00. Time value is whatever remains of the premium after removing intrinsic value, so time value = $7.00 − $5.00 = $2.00. This decomposition holds for every option premium, since total premium always equals intrinsic value plus time value.
  15. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The put's exercise payoff equals max(X − S, 0) = max($30.00 − $22.00, 0) = $8.00.   16. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A call option is in-the-money only when the stock price exceeds the strike price, since exercise then produces a positive payoff. Here the $95.00 stock price is below the $100.00 strike, so max(S − X, 0) equals zero and immediate exercise offers no benefit. The option is therefore classified as out-of-the-money.
  17. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): With no dividends or storage costs, the cost-of-carry forward price equals F = S × (1 + r) = $80.00 × (1 + 0.05) = $80.00 × 1.05 = $84.00. This reflects the fact that a forward buyer avoids paying for the asset today, so the forward price must compensate the seller for the forgone interest on the spot price. Any deviation from $84.00 would create a riskless arbitrage opportunity.
  18. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The fair forward price is $50.00 × 1.04 = $52.00, but the market quotes the forward at $54.00, which is overpriced relative to fair value. To exploit this, the arbitrageur sells the overpriced forward, borrows $50.00 today to buy the stock, and holds it to delivery, locking in a riskless profit of $54.00 − $52.00 = $2.00 per share. This cash-and-carry strategy captures the mispricing with no net investment or risk.
  19. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Rearranging put-call parity C + PV(X) = P + S to solve for the put gives P = C + PV(X) − S. Substituting the given values yields P = $6.00 + $48.00 − $52.00 = $2.00. If the put traded at any other price, an arbitrageur could combine the mispriced option with the stock, call, and a risk-free bond to earn a riskless profit.

  23. Zero intrinsic value occurs precisely when the option is at-the-money or out-of-the-money, since only in-the-money options carry positive intrinsic value. An in-the-money option, by contrast, always has a positive intrinsic value component within its premium.

The dividend yield reduces the forward price below the no-dividend case because the stockholder receives cash income that the forward buyer forgoes. This ensures intrinsic value can never be negative, since a rational holder would simply decline to exercise an option that would produce a loss. Time value, by contrast, is the separate remaining portion of the premium above this intrinsic value.

As the stock price climbs, the writer's loss grows without bound, since the cost of acquiring shares in the market to deliver keeps rising indefinitely. This asymmetry is precisely why naked call writing is considered one of the riskier options strategies available.

This combined position gains dollar-for-dollar with the stock above the strike and loses dollar-for-dollar below it, exactly mirroring a long stock position. Buying a put and selling a call, by contrast, would create a synthetic short stock position instead.

  40. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): With a dividend yield exceeding the risk-free rate, the cost-of-carry model gives F = S × (1 + r − d) = $60.00 × (1 + 0.03 − 0.05) = $60.00 × 0.98 = $58.80. This situation, where forward prices sit below spot prices, is an example of the market condition known as backwardation.
  41. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Illiquidity, since committed capital is often locked up for several years. Private equity and hedge fund vehicles restrict redemptions and require multi-year commitments because their underlying positions cannot be sold quickly without loss of value. Investors are compensated for accepting this reduced trading flexibility through an illiquidity premium built into expected returns. Dividend yields and capital guarantees are not the defining feature that separates alternatives from traditional public securities.
  42. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): Valuation smoothing that causes reported figures to lag true economic value. Appraisal-based, infrequent marks tend to understate volatility and correlation because valuations are updated slowly and reflect stale information relative to continuously priced public markets. This statistical artifact, not a genuinely superior process, is the standard explanation for the low reported risk statistics of appraisal-priced assets. It does not eliminate idiosyncratic risk, it merely obscures the timing of when that risk is recognized.
  43. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Improved diversification achieved through low correlation with the traditional assets. Adding an asset class whose return drivers differ from equities and bonds can reduce total portfolio volatility even if the new asset's own expected return is unremarkable, because portfolio risk depends on covariances as well as individual variances. Global macro strategies often generate returns from macroeconomic and directional trades unrelated to equity market beta. Fee elimination and guaranteed positive returns are not realistic outcomes of adding any hedge fund allocation.
  44. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Survivorship bias arising from the exclusion of failed hedge funds. Indices built only from currently reporting funds omit managers who closed after poor performance, mechanically inflating the average historical return of the remaining survivors. This is a well-documented distortion in hedge fund database research and differs from backfill bias, which concerns new funds adding historical track records retroactively. Selection bias tied to redemption timing is not the primary driver of this overstatement.
  45. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): A materially longer investment horizon than typical public market holdings. Buyout funds call capital progressively and hold portfolio companies for several years while operational improvements are implemented before an exit event generates distributions. This capital call and distribution structure is fundamentally different from a public equity index, which offers immediate and continuous liquidity. Daily net asset value pricing and unlimited redemption rights are characteristic of open-end mutual funds, not closed-end private equity structures.
  46. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): Lower net returns because of an additional layer of fund fees. A fund-of-funds typically charges its own management and incentive fees on top of the fees already charged by the underlying hedge fund managers, creating a double layer of costs that directly reduces the investor's net return relative to investing directly. Diversification benefits do not offset this fee drag dollar for dollar, and there is no guarantee of superior manager selection outweighing the extra cost.
  47. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Lighter regulatory oversight and limited standardized disclosure requirements overall. Many alternative investment vehicles are exempt from the extensive periodic reporting and disclosure rules that apply to registered mutual funds, placing greater responsibility on investors to independently verify strategy, valuation practices, and operational controls. There is no mandatory daily third-party audit requirement, and government-backed principal guarantees do not exist for hedge fund or private equity investments.
  48. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Matching investor liquidity terms to the fund's underlying illiquid positions. Lockups and notice periods allow managers to hold positions in less liquid securities without being forced into disadvantageous sales to meet sudden redemption demands, aligning fund-level liquidity terms with the actual liquidity of the underlying portfolio. This structure is unrelated to exchange listing rules and does not itself reduce the fund's expense ratio.
  49. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): They fall outside the conventional long-only public stock and bond categories. Real assets and private capital vehicles are grouped as alternatives primarily because they sit outside the traditional publicly traded, long-only equity and fixed-income universe, not because they share a common cash flow structure. Farmland and commodities do not pay a fixed coupon, and private equity and much of real estate are not exchange-traded with continuous intraday pricing.
  50. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): Infrequent pricing makes standard volatility-based risk measures unreliable here.   61. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The management fee, a fixed annual percentage charged regardless of performance. This recurring charge, typically 1% to 2% of assets under management annually, compensates the manager for operating the fund and is paid regardless of investment performance in a given year. The incentive or performance fee, by contrast, is contingent on generating investment profits, and a one-time subscription fee is charged only when an investor initially enters the fund rather than annually.
  62. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The incentive or performance fee, paid only on profits above the management fee. This variable charge, commonly 20% of profits, is paid only when the fund generates gains above the fixed management fee, aligning manager compensation with investor outcomes. A redemption fee is instead charged when an investor withdraws capital, and a subscription fee is a one-time charge at the time of initial investment, neither of which is tied to ongoing trading profits.
  63. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The hurdle rate, the minimum annual return required before incentive fees apply. This minimum required annual return, such as 6%, must be exceeded before the manager can collect any performance fee, protecting investors from paying for merely average or below-benchmark results. The high-water mark instead addresses recovering prior losses before charging new fees, and a clawback threshold relates to repayment of previously collected fees rather than a minimum return trigger.
  64. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): A hard hurdle, charging the incentive fee only on profit above the hurdle. Under a hard hurdle, the incentive fee applies only to the portion of profit that exceeds the hurdle rate, meaning investors keep the entire hurdle amount fee-free while the manager shares only in the excess return. A soft hurdle, by comparison, allows the manager to earn a fee on the entire profit once the hurdle is cleared, which is more favorable to the manager than a hard hurdle structure.
  65. The correct answer is C (option C's exact text): The high-water mark, requiring net asset value to first exceed its prior peak. This provision requires the fund's net asset value to first exceed its previous peak before any new incentive fee can be charged, preventing the manager from collecting performance fees on gains that merely recover prior losses. The soft hurdle provision concerns a minimum return threshold rather than loss recovery, and the gate provision instead limits investor redemption amounts during stressed periods.
  66. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The clawback provision, requiring repayment of previously collected performance fees. This contractual term requires the manager to return previously collected performance fees if subsequent losses mean the manager's cumulative fees exceeded what was ultimately earned over the life of the fund, protecting investors against front-loaded fee payments. The lockup extension clause instead concerns redemption timing, and a subscription discount is unrelated to repayment of already-collected fees.
  67. The correct answer is A (option A's exact text): The lockup period, an initial restriction preventing withdrawals for a set time. This initial restriction prevents investors from redeeming their capital for a specified time after investing, such as twelve months, giving the manager stability to deploy capital into less liquid strategies without facing early withdrawal pressure. The gate provision instead limits redemptions during ongoing operation of the fund, and the high-water mark relates to fee calculation rather than redemption timing.
  68. The correct answer is B (option B's exact text): The gate provision, capping the percentage of assets redeemable in one period. This mechanism allows the manager to cap the percentage of fund assets redeemable in a given period, preventing a rush of withdrawals from forcing the manager into disadvantageous asset sales during stressed markets. The clawback provision instead concerns repayment of previously earned incentive fees, and the hurdle rate relates to the minimum return required before performance fees apply, not redemption limits.

A quantified required return tied to specific goals such as a home purchase and retirement gives the advisor a concrete number to target rather than a vague qualitative statement. Stating the objective only relative to a benchmark ignores her actual spending needs and does not address her stated concern about inflation and taxes.
  122. The correct answer is C (Risk tolerance should be relatively low because the underfunded status and maturing liabilities reduce the plan's ability to bear investment losses): A 15% funding shortfall combined with an aging, soon-to-retire workforce reduces the plan's cushion against further losses, since benefits will need to be paid out sooner and the sponsor has less room to absorb additional declines. Ability to take risk, not just willingness, is a core input to a pension plan's risk objective, and both factors point toward caution here. Pursuing aggressive risk to close the gap quickly would expose the plan to the possibility of an even larger shortfall, and defined benefit plans do not transfer investment risk to employees the way defined contribution plans do.
  123. The correct answer is B (An essentially infinite, multistage time horizon reflecting the endowment's perpetual mission): An endowment created to fund scholarships in perpetuity has no defined end date, and its need to balance annual spending against preserving purchasing power for future generations means it effectively operates across multiple, overlapping stages rather than one fixed period. Treating the horizon as a short, fixed ten-year window or a purely annual cycle would understate the endowment's actual planning horizon and could lead to an overly conservative allocation. The perpetual, multistage nature of the horizon is a defining feature that distinguishes endowments from most individual investor IPS structures.
  124. The correct answer is B (The liquidity constraint, since committed capital cannot be accessed on short notice for spending needs): An eight-year lockup with irregular capital calls and distributions means the investor cannot convert this holding to cash on demand, which is precisely what the liquidity constraint in an IPS is designed to capture. The appropriate allocation size to an illiquid vehicle like this depends on how much of the investor's overall portfolio can reasonably be locked up given other near-term spending needs. This is not primarily a tax issue, since private equity gains are not automatically taxed the same as public equity gains, and lockups are a real, common consideration in setting overall asset allocation.
  125. The correct answer is A (Legal and regulatory constraints, since regulators directly restrict eligible asset classes and required capital levels): Statutory capital requirements and permissible asset lists imposed by insurance regulators are external rules the insurer must comply with, which is the defining feature of the legal and regulatory constraint category in an IPS. These requirements directly limit which securities and asset classes the insurer may hold and in what proportions, regardless of the insurer's own return or risk preferences. This is not a time horizon issue, since filing frequency does not determine investable asset classes, and it is not unique to one insurer since similar rules apply to every insurer operating in that jurisdiction.
  126. The correct answer is C (As part of the unique circumstances constraint, since it reflects a donor-specific investment restriction): Exclusionary restrictions tied to a founding donor's values, rather than to a regulatory requirement or a universal industry practice, are classic examples of the unique circumstances constraint in an IPS. This constraint category exists specifically to capture investor-specific preferences and restrictions that do not fit neatly into the other standard categories. It is not a return objective, since excluding sectors does not by itself define a target return, and it is not a liquidity constraint, since excluded industries are not inherently less liquid than included ones.
  127. The correct answer is A (His ability to take risk is high, but his willingness to take risk is comparatively low): Devon's financial resources and stable income give him a high objective ability to absorb losses, while his emotional discomfort with even modest declines reflects a lower subjective willingness to take risk. This gap between ability and willingness is a common and important distinction the advisor must reconcile when setting the overall risk objective, typically by leaning toward the more conservative of the two. Treating the two factors as identical, or letting willingness always override a clearly higher ability, would misrepresent his actual circumstances and risk objective.
  128. The correct answer is B (The tax constraint, since after-tax returns and the timing of realized gains directly affect her wealth): Priya's sensitivity to realized capital gains in a high tax bracket, and the advisor's recommendation to hold longer and harvest losses, directly address how taxes reduce her net investment returns, which is the essence of the tax constraint in an IPS. Tax-loss harvesting and holding period management are standard tools for managing this constraint, not the time horizon constraint, which concerns when funds are needed rather than how gains are taxed. Tax rules governing personal investment income are not set exclusively by securities regulators, so framing this as a legal and regulatory constraint would be inaccurate.
  129. The correct answer is C (In a defined contribution plan, investment risk is borne by the individual employee rather than pooled at the plan sponsor level): Because each employee directs their own account and receives whatever balance results from investment performance, the defined contribution structure shifts investment risk onto the individual rather than the plan sponsor absorbing it as in a defined benefit plan. This distinction matters for IPS purposes because each employee's account may need its own risk and return objectives tailored to that individual's circumstances rather than one plan-wide IPS. The employer does not guarantee a fixed benefit under a defined contribution arrangement, and the two plan types are structured, and typically documented, quite differently.
  130. The correct answer is A (The mandated minimum spending rate plus the foundation's own operating expenses and expected inflation): A foundation's required return objective should be built from the components that determine how much the portfolio must earn to sustain itself, namely the legally mandated distribution rate, the foundation's own operating costs, and expected inflation needed to preserve real capital over time. These are the standard building blocks used to derive a required return for a perpetual, spending-driven institution. The founder's historical personal salary and the trading volume of held securities have no bearing on how much the portfolio needs to earn to meet its spending obligations.
  131. The correct answer is C (A liability-driven objective emphasizing asset cash flows matched to the timing and amount of policyholder obligations): Because a life insurer prices policies based on assumed investment returns and must be able to pay claims decades into the future, its investment objective is fundamentally shaped by matching asset cash flows to the timing and size of those long-dated liabilities. This liability-driven framing is central to how life insurers set return and risk objectives, distinguishing them from investors without predictable long-term obligations. Life insurance liabilities do in fact have reasonably predictable actuarial durations, and a purely short-term trading focus would be inconsistent with meeting long-dated policyholder claims.
  132. The correct answer is B (It generally leads the nonlife insurer to hold shorter-duration, more liquid assets and greater liquidity reserves): Because property and casualty claims can arise suddenly and unpredictably, such as after a catastrophic weather event, nonlife insurers typically need to hold shorter-duration, more liquid assets and maintain larger liquidity buffers than life insurers, whose claim timing is comparatively predictable. This difference in claim predictability is a key driver of how the two insurer types structure their investment portfolios differently despite both being insurance companies. Regulation does not require identical asset durations across insurer types, and holding zero fixed-income securities would be both impractical and inconsistent with prudent liquidity management.
  133. The correct answer is A (A very long, essentially generational time horizon consistent with intergenerational wealth transfer): A sovereign wealth fund explicitly designed to benefit future generations rather than smooth near-term government budgets has, by its own stated purpose, a very long time horizon that can extend across generations. This long horizon typically supports a greater capacity to accept short-term volatility in exchange for long-term growth, distinguishing this type of fund from a stabilization fund with near-term liquidity needs. A one-year horizon or the absence of any stated objective would directly contradict the fund's own generational purpose.
  134. The correct answer is B (Managing liquidity and interest rate risk so that assets can support potential deposit withdrawals and funding costs): Because deposits can be withdrawn on demand, a bank's investment portfolio objectives are shaped heavily by the need to maintain sufficient liquidity and to manage interest rate exposure relative to its funding costs, an approach commonly described as asset-liability management. Simply maximizing duration for the highest possible yield would ignore the very real risk that deposits could be withdrawn before long-dated assets mature or can be sold without loss. Deposit liabilities are a central, not an ignorable, input to how banks construct their investment portfolios.
  135. The correct answer is C (The fund's stated objectives are shaped by its published mandate and by aggregate shareholder liquidity needs, not one investor's circumstances): Because a mutual fund pools capital from many investors with differing goals and must meet daily redemption requests from any shareholder, its investment objectives are defined at the fund level through its published mandate and the aggregate liquidity needs of its shareholder base rather than any single individual's personal circumstances. This is a key structural difference from an individual IPS, which is built entirely around one investor's own goals, constraints, and tax situation. A daily-redeemable fund cannot ignore liquidity, and it does not adopt the personal tax profile of any one shareholder, however large that shareholder's position might be.
  136. The correct answer is C (The first describes a relative return objective and the second describes an absolute return objective): A return objective stated as an amount above a named benchmark index is a relative return objective, since success is measured against that benchmark's performance, while a fixed stated percentage return regardless of market conditions is an absolute return objective, since it does not reference any external benchmark. These are the two standard ways return objectives are framed in an IPS, and distinguishing between them affects how performance is later evaluated. Neither instruction describes a liquidity constraint or a legal and regulatory constraint, since both are purely statements about desired investment return.

Each stage carries its own liquidity needs and risk capacity, meaning a single blended allocation would not adequately serve his tuition need without also compromising his longer-term retirement goal. Describing this as a single simultaneous horizon or as having no identifiable horizon would misrepresent how his goals are actually staggered across time.
  138. The correct answer is B (Compute the withdrawal rate as $60,000 divided by $1,500,000, then combine it with expected inflation using the appropriate return formula): The standard approach to deriving a required pretax nominal return is to first calculate the withdrawal rate as the desired annual cash flow divided by the portfolio's value, and then adjust that rate for expected inflation to arrive at the nominal figure needed before taxes and fees. Inverting the ratio, or dividing portfolio value by the withdrawal amount, produces a meaningless multiple rather than a usable rate. Ignoring the portfolio value entirely and simply using the inflation rate alone would badly understate what the retiree actually needs to earn to sustain her spending.
  139. This combination is meaningfully different from an investor who has ample resources or genuine comfort with volatility. Savings level relative to spending needs is in fact directly relevant to ability to take risk, so treating it as irrelevant would be inconsistent with how risk capacity is properly assessed.
  140. The correct answer is C (The planning step, culminating in the development of a written investment policy statement): Identifying the client's objectives, constraints, and preferences before any securities are purchased is the defining activity of the planning step in the portfolio management process, and it results in the written investment policy statement that guides all later decisions. The execution step comes afterward and involves translating the IPS into an actual asset allocation and set of security holdings. The feedback step occurs later still, comparing realized results against the objectives that were established during planning, so it cannot describe this initial stage.
  141. The correct answer is B (The execution step, and this approach is called top-down asset allocation followed by security selection): Once the IPS is complete, deciding how to allocate capital across broad asset classes before selecting individual securities within each class is part of the execution step of the portfolio management process, and this sequencing, from broad allocation down to individual securities, is what defines a top-down approach. A bottom-up approach instead starts by identifying attractive individual securities without first setting the asset class weights, which is the opposite sequence. Strategic rebalancing and ongoing monitoring belong to the later feedback step rather than to this initial allocation decision.
  142. The correct answer is A (The feedback step, which includes performance monitoring, rebalancing, and periodically reassessing the IPS): Comparing actual portfolio performance and risk exposures against stated objectives, rebalancing to target weights, and periodically checking whether the client's circumstances have changed are the core recurring activities of the feedback step in the portfolio management process. This step is ongoing rather than a one-time event, which is why it recurs every quarter rather than only occurring once at account inception. Rebalancing toward existing target weights is a monitoring and adjustment activity, not the initial security selection that defines the execution step.
  143. The correct answer is B (Constraints such as liquidity and time horizon directly limit which asset classes and weights are feasible for the portfolio): The constraints section of an IPS defines practical boundaries, such as how much cash must remain accessible and how long capital can remain invested, that directly determine which asset classes and allocation weights are actually feasible for a given client. Finalizing strategic asset allocation before understanding these boundaries risks recommending an allocation the client cannot practically sustain given their real liquidity or time horizon needs. Constraints are not merely informational, and strategic asset allocation is deliberately tailored to each client's individual constraints rather than being uniform across all clients.
  144. The correct answer is C (Intergenerational equity, which seeks a fair balance between spending for current and future beneficiaries): The tension between spending more for current students and preserving more capital for future students is precisely what the concept of intergenerational equity addresses in an endowment's IPS, since it seeks a fair balance between meeting today's needs and preserving purchasing power for tomorrow's beneficiaries. This concept is distinctive to perpetual institutional investors like endowments and foundations rather than to individual investors. Liquidity and tax considerations, while relevant to an endowment's overall IPS, do not directly capture this specific spending-versus-preservation tradeoff between generations.
  145. The correct answer is A (Plan X can typically accept somewhat greater investment risk than Plan Y, which must protect its more limited surplus): An overfunded plan like Plan X has a cushion of assets beyond what is needed to meet its liabilities, giving it somewhat greater capacity to absorb investment losses, while an underfunded plan like Plan Y has a smaller or negative surplus and must be more careful to protect what assets it has to avoid worsening its funded status. Funded status is a direct and well-established input into a defined benefit plan's risk objective, so it is not irrelevant to setting the allocation. Claiming an underfunded plan should always take on far more risk ignores the real downside of further losses when there is little cushion to absorb them.
  146. This is distinct from a legal and regulatory constraint, since municipal building codes governing the renovation itself have no bearing on how the investor's portfolio should be structured. Simply adding the expense to her long-term growth target would fail to address the actual near-term liquidity need created by the planned expenditure.

This standard carries real legal consequences for a trustee who disregards it, so it is not merely a voluntary best practice. It is also not a tax rule, and it applies broadly to fiduciary relationships, including pension trusts, rather than being limited to individual taxable accounts.

While the vesting schedule does have a time-related component, the concentration and restriction themselves are the defining, individual-specific feature here. Concentrated positions do not automatically guarantee the highest possible return, and framing this purely as a return objective would ignore the real diversification and liquidity risk it creates.
  149. The correct answer is B (It typically supports somewhat more tax-inefficient, turnover-heavy strategies within that account since gains are not currently taxed): Because gains inside a tax-deferred account are not taxed until withdrawal, this account is generally a more suitable location for holdings that generate frequent taxable events, such as higher-turnover strategies, than an equivalent taxable account where those events would trigger an immediate tax cost. This concept, often called asset location, is a standard consideration when constructing an IPS across accounts with different tax treatments. It is inaccurate to say account tax status has no effect on portfolio construction, and there is no requirement that tax-deferred accounts hold only tax-exempt municipal securities, which would in fact waste the account's tax-deferral benefit.
  150. The correct answer is A (Reconcile the two, generally anchoring the objective closer to the required return supported by actual resources and risk capacity): When a client's desired return significantly exceeds what her actual resources and spending needs support, the advisor should reconcile the gap, typically anchoring the final return objective closer to the required return that reflects real capital sufficiency rather than simply adopting the more aspirational figure. Always defaulting to the higher desired return risks recommending an allocation that carries more risk than the client's actual capacity can support. Disregarding the required return entirely would ignore the very analysis meant to ground the return objective in reality.

These rules directly limit which asset classes and how much of each the insurer may hold, independent of what the insurer itself might otherwise prefer. This is not a return objective, since regulators do not set a required percentage return, and it is not a unique circumstance limited to employees, since it governs the insurer's entire investment portfolio.

This differs meaningfully from the accumulation phase, where an investor is still contributing rather than withdrawing and generally has lower near-term liquidity needs.   154. The correct answer is A (A relative risk objective, since risk is defined and constrained in comparison to a benchmark index): Defining acceptable volatility as not exceeding a specified reference index by more than a set margin ties the risk objective directly to that benchmark's own volatility, which is the defining feature of a relative risk objective. An absolute risk objective would instead state a fixed numeric volatility target with no reference to any external index. Standard deviation measures the dispersion of returns and is not used to measure cash availability, so framing this statement as a liquidity constraint would be inaccurate.
  155. The correct answer is B (A real return objective, since it is defined relative to inflation rather than as a fixed nominal figure): Defining success as maintaining purchasing power after inflation and fees, rather than stating a fixed percentage, is the defining feature of a real return objective, since it explicitly accounts for the erosive effect of inflation on portfolio value. A nominal return objective would instead be stated as a fixed percentage without adjusting for inflation, which is the opposite of what is described here. It is also not a relative return objective, since the objective is not being measured against any named market benchmark.
  156. The correct answer is A (A stabilization fund, which typically requires greater liquidity and a shorter effective horizon than a savings-oriented fund): A sovereign wealth fund created to smooth government spending when commodity export revenue falls is, by its stated purpose, a stabilization fund, and this near-term budget-smoothing role typically requires holding greater liquidity and adopting a shorter effective horizon than a savings-oriented fund built for multi-generational wealth accumulation. A savings fund is instead built around a very long horizon rather than near-term budget support, so it does not fit this fund's purpose. There is no requirement that such a fund hold only foreign real estate, and no fund type is defined that way.
  157. The correct answer is C (Reducing the bank's net interest margin volatility by matching asset and liability duration exposures): Aligning the interest rate sensitivity of investment assets with that of deposit and borrowing liabilities is a core asset-liability management practice aimed at reducing the volatility of the bank's net interest margin as rates move. Simply maximizing unrealized gains on held-to-maturity bonds without regard to liabilities would ignore the funding side of the balance sheet entirely, undermining the purpose of matching. Eliminating all fixed-income holdings would also be impractical and would not achieve the goal of aligning asset and liability interest rate sensitivity.
  158. The correct answer is B (A mutual fund's policy generally reflects the fund's own mandate and tax structure rather than any single shareholder's personal tax bracket or unique circumstances): Because a mutual fund is a pooled vehicle serving many shareholders with differing personal tax situations, its stated investment policy is built around the fund's own published mandate and its own tax structure rather than any individual shareholder's personal tax bracket or unique circumstances. This is a fundamental structural difference from a wealthy individual's IPS, which is deliberately personalized to that one investor. A fund cannot practically draft a separate unique circumstances section for every shareholder, and it is not taxed identically to any single individual investor's personal account.
  159. Treating the IPS as fixed permanently once signed would leave the portfolio managed against outdated objectives and constraints. Personal circumstances, not just market performance, are a recognized and important trigger for revisiting an IPS.

Treating this only as a return objective would overlook the practical limitations his situation actually imposes on portfolio construction. These are recognized as separate constraint categories in an IPS rather than a single blended figure, so collapsing them into one combined category would misrepresent the framework.
  161. The correct answer is A (10.2%, computed as 3% plus 1.2 times the 6% market risk premium): CAPM states required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta multiplied by the market risk premium. The market risk premium here is 9% minus 3%, or 6%. Multiplying 1.2 by 6% gives 7.2%, and adding the 3% risk-free rate yields a required return of 10.2%.
  162. The correct answer is B (10.4%, from 0.40 times 8% plus 0.60 times 12%): portfolio expected return is the weighted average of the individual asset returns using each asset's portfolio weight. Multiplying 0.40 by 8% gives 3.2%, and multiplying 0.60 by 12% gives 7.2%. Adding these two weighted contributions together produces a total portfolio expected return of 10.4%.
  163. The correct answer is C (unsystematic, firm-specific risk that is unique to individual companies): diversification works by combining securities whose firm-specific return drivers are not perfectly correlated, so individual company shocks tend to offset one another. Systematic risk, arising from market-wide factors like interest rates or economic growth, affects nearly every security simultaneously and cannot be diversified away. As a portfolio adds more uncorrelated holdings, unsystematic risk shrinks toward zero while systematic risk remains.
  164. The correct answer is B (14.19%, the square root of the full variance including the covariance term): portfolio variance equals w1 squared times sigma1 squared plus w2 squared times sigma2 squared plus twice w1 times w2 times rho times sigma1 times sigma2. Substituting the values gives 0.25 times 0.04 plus 0.25 times 0.0225 plus 0.5 times 0.30 times 0.20 times 0.15, which sums to 0.020125. Taking the square root of 0.020125 produces a portfolio standard deviation of approximately 14.19%.
  165. The correct answer is A (the set of portfolios offering the highest expected return for each level of risk): the efficient frontier is the upper boundary of all achievable risky-asset portfolios in risk-return space. Every portfolio on this frontier maximizes expected return for its given level of standard deviation, or equivalently minimizes risk for its given expected return. Portfolios lying below the frontier are considered inefficient because a better risk-return combination is achievable.
  166. The correct answer is C (combinations of the risk-free asset and the market portfolio of risky assets): the capital market line extends from the risk-free rate on the vertical axis and is tangent to the efficient frontier at the market portfolio. Every point along this line represents a mix of the risk-free asset and the market portfolio, achieved through lending or borrowing at the risk-free rate. This differs from the security market line, which relates individual security beta to required return rather than plotting portfolio combinations.
  167. The correct answer is A (1.2, from dividing the covariance of 0.06 by the market variance of 0.05): beta is defined as a security's covariance with the market divided by the market portfolio's own variance. Dividing 0.06 by 0.05 yields exactly 1.2, meaning this security's returns move somewhat more than proportionally with market returns. A beta of 1.2 indicates the stock has higher systematic risk than the overall market.
  168. The correct answer is B (the assets are not perfectly positively correlated with one another): the portfolio variance formula includes a covariance term that shrinks total portfolio risk whenever correlation between assets is less than perfectly positive. When returns do not move in perfect lockstep, gains in one asset can partially offset losses in another, reducing overall volatility below a simple weighted average of individual risks. If correlation equaled exactly 1.0, no diversification benefit would exist at all.
  169. The correct answer is C (the security market line uses beta as the risk measure while the capital market line uses standard deviation): the security market line plots expected return against beta and applies to any individual security or portfolio, whether efficiently diversified or not. The capital market line plots expected return against total standard deviation and applies only to efficient portfolios combining the risk-free asset with the market portfolio. This distinction reflects that beta captures only systematic risk while standard deviation captures total risk.
  170. Multiplying 0.30 by 15% gives 4.5%, and multiplying 0.70 by 7% gives 4.9%. Adding these two weighted values together produces a total expected portfolio return of 9.4%.

Multiplying 15% by 10% gives 0.015, and dividing the covariance of 0.006 by 0.015 produces a correlation coefficient of exactly 0.4. This positive but moderate correlation indicates the two securities tend to move together but not in perfect lockstep.
  172. This is a classic hedging strategy that reduces net exposure to adverse price movements rather than seeking additional profit. The hedge does not eliminate all systematic risk, since the futures contract only partially offsets exposure depending on its notional size relative to the portfolio.

  174. The correct answer is B (systematic risk plus unsystematic risk, the two components of total risk): total risk, as measured by a security's overall standard deviation of returns, decomposes into a market-wide systematic component and a firm-specific unsystematic component. Systematic risk reflects broad economic and market factors affecting nearly all securities, while unsystematic risk reflects factors unique to an individual company or industry. Diversification can reduce the unsystematic portion but cannot remove the systematic portion of total risk.
  175. The correct answer is C (0.50, from dividing the 8% excess return by the 16% standard deviation): the Sharpe ratio equals a portfolio's return minus the risk-free rate, divided by the portfolio's standard deviation. Subtracting the 3% risk-free rate from the 11% portfolio return gives an excess return of 8%. Dividing this 8% excess return by the 16% standard deviation produces a Sharpe ratio of exactly 0.50.
  176. The correct answer is A (the market price of risk, showing extra expected return per unit of risk): the slope of the capital market line equals the market risk premium divided by the market portfolio's standard deviation, a quantity finance theory labels the market price of risk. This slope indicates how much additional expected return investors demand for each additional unit of total portfolio risk taken on. It applies only to efficient portfolios lying on the capital market line, not to individual securities held in isolation.
  177. The correct answer is C (0, since its returns do not vary systematically with market movements): beta measures how a security's returns move relative to overall market returns, and a truly risk-free asset such as a short-term treasury bill has a fixed, guaranteed return that does not fluctuate with the market. Because its returns show no covariance with market movements, its beta is mathematically zero. This is consistent with the capital asset pricing model, where the risk-free asset anchors the vertical intercept of both the security market line and the capital market line.
  178. The correct answer is B (roughly 20 to 30 randomly selected stocks across different industries): empirical studies on portfolio diversification generally show that most of the reducible, firm-specific risk in a randomly selected equity portfolio is eliminated once the portfolio holds approximately 20 to 30 different stocks. Beyond that point, adding further securities produces diminishing marginal reductions in unsystematic risk. The remaining risk after this point is predominantly systematic and cannot be diversified away through further security selection.
  179. The correct answer is A (investors are rational, risk-averse, and seek to maximize expected utility): the capital asset pricing model assumes all investors are rational decision makers who prefer higher expected return for a given level of risk and prefer lower risk for a given level of expected return. This risk-averse, utility-maximizing behavior underlies the model's prediction that investors hold efficient, diversified portfolios combined with the risk-free asset. The model does not assume risk-seeking or risk-indifferent behavior, since such assumptions would be inconsistent with the existence of a positive market risk premium.

  182. The correct answer is A (a broad change in interest rates that affects valuations across the market): systematic risk arises from macroeconomic and market-wide factors, such as interest rate changes, inflation, or broad economic growth, that simultaneously influence the valuations of nearly all securities. Because these factors affect the entire market rather than a single company, systematic risk cannot be diversified away by holding more securities. Firm-specific events, such as a single company's product recall or lawsuit, represent unsystematic risk that diversification can meaningfully reduce.
  183. The correct answer is B (13.0%, computed as 4% plus 1.5 multiplied by the 6% market premium): the market risk premium is calculated first as the expected market return of 10% minus the risk-free rate of 4%, giving 6%. Multiplying the beta of 1.5 by this 6% premium yields 9.0%. Adding this 9.0% to the 4% risk-free rate produces a total required return of 13.0%.
  184. The correct answer is C (combinations of the risk-free asset and a single chosen risky portfolio): the capital allocation line can be drawn for any individual risky portfolio an investor selects, connecting the risk-free rate to that specific portfolio's risk-return point. This differs from the capital market line, which is a special case of the capital allocation line drawn specifically using the market portfolio as the risky asset. Every point along the capital allocation line represents a different mix of the risk-free asset and the chosen risky portfolio, achieved through lending or borrowing.
  185. The correct answer is A (an undiversified investor holding a concentrated position in few securities): total standard deviation captures both systematic and unsystematic risk, making it the relevant risk measure for an investor whose concentrated holdings still carry substantial firm-specific risk that has not been diversified away. A fully diversified investor, by contrast, has already eliminated most unsystematic risk and is more appropriately concerned with beta, which captures only the systematic risk that remains. Using beta for an undiversified investor would understate the investor's true total exposure to risk.
  186. The correct answer is C (15.0%, the simple weighted average of 0.5 times 10% plus 0.5 times 20%): when correlation equals exactly positive 1.0, the portfolio variance formula simplifies algebraically to the square of a simple weighted average, meaning portfolio standard deviation equals w1 times sigma1 plus w2 times sigma2 directly. Multiplying 0.5 by 10% gives 5%, and multiplying 0.5 by 20% gives 10%. Adding these weighted contributions together produces a portfolio standard deviation of exactly 15.0%, with no diversification benefit since the assets move in perfect lockstep.
  187. The correct answer is B (8.5%, from 0.20 times 25% plus 0.50 times 10% plus 0.30 times negative 5%): expected return under a probability distribution is calculated by multiplying each possible outcome by its probability and summing the results. Multiplying 0.20 by 25% gives 5%, multiplying 0.50 by 10% gives 5%, and multiplying 0.30 by negative 5% gives negative 1.5%. Adding these three weighted outcomes together produces a total expected return of 8.5%.
  188. The correct answer is A (a theoretical portfolio containing all risky assets weighted by market value): the CAPM market portfolio is a theoretical construct that includes every risky asset available in the economy, with each asset weighted according to its proportion of total market capitalization. This broad, fully diversified portfolio represents the highest possible degree of diversification achievable, containing only systematic risk with no remaining firm-specific risk. In practice, analysts often approximate the market portfolio using a broad equity index, though this is only an imperfect proxy for the true theoretical construct.
  189. The correct answer is C (the stock is undervalued, offering a return above what its risk level requires): a stock plotting above the security market line has an expected return higher than the return CAPM predicts is required for its given level of systematic risk, or beta. This gap suggests the market has underpriced the stock relative to its risk, since a higher expected return for the same risk implies the current price is too low. As market participants recognize this mispricing and buy the stock, its price should rise and its expected return should fall back toward the security market line over time.
  190. The correct answer is B (1.0, from 0.60 multiplied by the ratio of 25% to 15%): beta can be expressed as the correlation coefficient multiplied by the ratio of the individual security's standard deviation to the market's standard deviation. Dividing the stock's 25% standard deviation by the market's 15% standard deviation gives approximately 1.667. Multiplying this ratio by the 0.60 correlation coefficient produces a beta of exactly 1.0, meaning the stock's systematic risk closely matches the overall market's risk level.
  191. The correct answer is A (it limits downside loss on the portfolio while preserving upside potential): a protective put gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to sell the underlying index at a fixed strike price, capping potential losses if the market falls below that strike. Because the put is a right rather than an obligation, the investor still fully participates in any market gains above the premium paid for the option. This asymmetric payoff structure is what distinguishes a protective put hedge from strategies like futures hedging, which offset both gains and losses symmetrically.
  192. The correct answer is B (efficient diversification uses covariance among assets to minimize risk for a given return): efficient diversification, grounded in modern portfolio theory, selects and weights securities based explicitly on their covariance relationships to construct portfolios that minimize risk for a targeted level of expected return. Naive diversification simply adds securities without analyzing how their returns interact, which reduces some unsystematic risk but does not optimize the resulting risk-return tradeoff. As a result, efficient diversification generally achieves a superior risk-adjusted outcome compared to a naively diversified portfolio of similar size.
  193. The correct answer is C (it ranges between negative 1 and positive 1 inclusive, by mathematical definition): the correlation coefficient is a standardized measure derived by dividing covariance by the product of the two assets' standard deviations, which mathematically constrains its value to fall between negative 1 and positive 1. A value of positive 1 indicates perfect positive correlation, negative 1 indicates perfect negative correlation, and zero indicates no linear relationship between the two assets' returns. This standardization is precisely what allows correlation, unlike covariance, to be compared meaningfully across different pairs of assets.
  194. The correct answer is A (9.0%, from 0.25 times 12% plus 0.25 times 6% plus 0.50 times 9%): expected portfolio return for a three-asset portfolio is calculated as the sum of each asset's weight multiplied by its own expected return. Multiplying 0.25 by 12% gives 3.0%, multiplying 0.25 by 6% gives 1.5%, and multiplying 0.50 by 9% gives 4.5%. Adding these three weighted contributions together produces a total expected portfolio return of 9.0%.
  195. The correct answer is C (by borrowing at the risk-free rate to invest more than 100% in the market portfolio): points on the capital market line to the right of the market portfolio represent leveraged positions, where an investor borrows funds at the risk-free rate and invests the borrowed amount, together with their own capital, entirely in the market portfolio. This leverage increases both the expected return and the risk of the resulting position beyond what the unlevered market portfolio alone offers. Points to the left of the market portfolio, by contrast, represent lending at the risk-free rate rather than borrowing.
  196. The correct answer is B (11.40%, the square root of the sum of the two weighted variance terms): when correlation equals zero, the covariance term in the portfolio variance formula drops out entirely, leaving only w1 squared times sigma1 squared plus w2 squared times sigma2 squared. Substituting the values gives 0.09 times 0.09 plus 0.49 times 0.01, which sums to 0.0130. Taking the square root of 0.0130 produces a portfolio standard deviation of approximately 11.40%.
  197. The correct answer is A (it stems from macroeconomic factors affecting nearly all securities simultaneously): systematic risk arises from broad, economy-wide forces such as interest rate changes, inflation, and overall economic growth that influence the returns of virtually every security in the market at the same time. Because these factors affect all securities together rather than in isolated, offsetting ways, adding more securities to a portfolio cannot cause this shared risk to cancel out. This is precisely why systematic risk is also referred to as non-diversifiable or market risk, remaining even in a fully diversified portfolio.
  198. The correct answer is C (the market risk premium, calculated as the expected market return minus the risk-free rate): the security market line's slope reflects how much additional expected return the market demands for each additional unit of beta, which by definition equals the expected market return minus the risk-free rate. This slope quantifies the compensation investors require for bearing systematic risk, and a steeper slope implies investors demand more extra return per unit of beta. The line's vertical intercept, by contrast, represents the risk-free rate itself, not its slope.
  199. The correct answer is B (6.5%, computed as 3.5% plus 0.6 multiplied by the 5% market risk premium): the market risk premium is calculated first as the expected market return of 8.5% minus the risk-free rate of 3.5%, giving 5.0%. Multiplying the beta of 0.6 by this 5.0% premium yields 3.0%. Adding this 3.0% to the 3.5% risk-free rate produces a total required return of 6.5%, reflecting this defensive stock's lower systematic risk relative to the market.
  200. The correct answer is A (to maximize expected return for any given level of acceptable portfolio risk): modern portfolio theory's diversification framework is built around constructing the efficient frontier, the set of portfolios that offer the highest achievable expected return for each level of risk an investor is willing to accept. This objective does not claim to eliminate systematic risk, which remains inherent to holding risky assets regardless of diversification. Nor does the framework guarantee positive returns in every market environment, since even efficiently diversified portfolios remain exposed to overall market downturns.
